### My Especially Highly-Esteemed, 
### Highly and Much-Honored Gentlemen.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap "O" featuring intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

**O**lthough I almost doubt whether anyone still lives among your distinguished councils <term>Councils: From the Latin "Collegiis," referring to the various administrative and governing bodies of the city.</term> who knew me in my youth as a fellow student <term>Fellow student: From the Latin "Commilitonem," a term used by scholars to refer to those with whom they studied.</term> among you; I have nonetheless deemed it my duty to offer the now-living, renowned regents and leaders in both spiritual and worldly offices a testament of the constant love I have maintained for your most worthy city.

And certainly, I would expect the reward of the ungrateful if I did not observe this duty. For I can truly say that, regarding my studies <note>original Latin: "Studia"</note>, I owe much to those very faithful professors <note>original Latin: "Professoribus"</note> of that time, who have long since entered into their rest; and as for the hospitality <term>Hospitality: From the Latin "Hospitia," referring here to the lodgings, board, and general reception the author experienced as a resident.</term> I received, I found it nowhere as good, much less better, than in beloved Thorn <note>Modern-day Toruń.</note>.

With what kind of tender love, necessary

...compelled me, the late Doctor *Paul Hoffmann*, at that time the *Senior* <term>Senior: The presiding head of the local clergy or a high-ranking academic official.</term> and *Rector*, to stay for a while at your *Gymnasium* <term>Gymnasium: An elite academic secondary school preparing students for university.</term>. I had previously intended to go to *Königsberg* with two *Hungarian students*, *Adami* and *Krause*, with whom I had studied <note>original German: "frequentiret," from the Latin "frequentare"</note> at the *Breslau Gymnasium* in my most beloved father-city; we had only intended to take the route through *Thorn*. However, I still thank **GOD** even now that I decided <note>original: "resolviret"</note> to do so and remained there into my second year.

For what solid studies <note>original Latin: "Studia"</note> did I not find there! Among others, with the excellent Professor *Sartorius*, with whom I held private tutorials <note>original Latin: "Collegia privatissima," referring to intensive, small-group lessons</note>. Under his chairmanship <note>original Latin: "Præsidio"</note>, on December 23, 1688, I publicly disputed on:

->*Humanity and Politeness, Truthfulness and Silence, Justice, Law, and Gratitude*<-

In this academic debate <note>original: "Disputation"</note>, my opponents <term>Opponents: In a formal disputation, these were fellow students tasked with challenging the author's arguments to test his knowledge.</term>—as shown by their printed verses <note>original Latin: "Carmina"</note> which I still hold in high regard—were *Gottfried Schincke* of Fraustadt, Poland; *Friedrich Jäckwitz* of Lissa, Poland; *Johann Heinrich Schulze* of Thorn, Prussia; and *Martinus Krause* of Kaisersdorf, Hungary. I dedicated <note>original Latin: "dedicirte"</note> this work to Councilman von *Miltenberg*, to Inspector *Acoluthus*, to Pastor *Viccius* of St. Mary Magdalene, and to his Deacon *Reußner* in *Breslau*.

at Michael Ringeltaube’s, a clergyman <term>Ecclesiasticus: A Latin term for a member of the clergy or a church minister.</term> in the New Town <note>Thorn (Toruń) was historically divided into the Old Town and the New Town, each with its own administration and churches until their unification in 1454.</note>, I delivered my first sermon. And when would I ever finish, if I wanted to list here all the goodness I enjoyed in Thorn—as much as I can still recall after so many years?

Enough; my highly honored gentlemen can see from what I have dutifully praised that I have very great reasons to publicly praise before the whole world the kindness and love shown to me by your late ancestors, and to express my most bounden thanks to you, as their highly esteemed and highly deserving successors. If I, from my position here, can serve you or your children in any way in return, it would bring me the greatest joy if you would grant me the opportunity to do so.

Therefore, highly and worthily esteemed gentlemen, please most graciously accept this small treatise <term>Tractätchen: A humble, diminutive reference to the author’s own writing.</term> as a sure token <note>original: "Unterpfande," a pledge or security of intent.</note> of my heart, which is so eager to show its gratitude. I ask that you judge it according to the affability and love that resides within you, and believe that for the few remaining years of my life, I shall call upon GOD for the protection and preservation, in blessing, of your most beloved city; and also, that for you and your descendants,

# Preface.

In my recently published *Selected Library* <note>original: "Bibliotheca Selecta"</note> for those dedicated to the <term>Holy Ministry of Preaching: The office of a pastor or preacher in the Lutheran church (German: Predigt-Amt).</term>, etc., I promised on page 145 that, if the LORD willed and I lived, I might release another section or two of my as-yet unpublished *Exegetical Lexicon* <note>original: "Exegetisches Lexicon"; a dictionary focused on the interpretation and critical explanation of biblical texts.</note>. Since the goodness of God has spared my life until now, I wish to fulfill this promise and present these two sections—namely, the *Rabbinic Explanation of Holy Scripture* <note>original: "S. Scripturæ Rabbinicam Explicationem"</note> and *Roots* <note>original: "Radices"</note>—as a further sample.

However, I am providing them just as I had already prepared them nine years ago, and have now, in revising them, only added a little here and there; for my current circumstances do not allow me to set my hand to them once more in such a way that they could appear in a truly pleasing form, as I would have wished. Though I also ask that you consider this: that no detailed

<summary>In this preface, Friederich Opfergelt introduces two new sections of his "Exegetical Lexicon" concerning Rabbinic scripture interpretation and linguistic roots. He explains that these materials were drafted nine years earlier and are being published now as a sample of his larger ongoing work.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Exegetical Lexicon, Rabbinic interpretation, Hebrew roots, biblical scholarship, 18th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface. On the preceding page, the author, Friederich Opfergelt, introduced the two new sections of his "Exegetical Lexicon" (Rabbinic interpretation and linguistic roots), explaining that they are samples of his work drafted nine years prior.</meta>

# Preface.

---

writing a treatise here, but rather only providing **Titles** <note>Used here to mean subject headings or dictionary entries.</note> from a Lexicon, and indeed not for scholars, but only for those who have need of it.

That I have now incorporated the first section <note>original: "Titulum"</note> into my *Exegetical Lexicon* was by no means done because I believed that an interpreter <note>An interpreter or critical scholar of the Bible (Latin: *Exegeta*).</note>, when explaining divine pronouncements in these present times, primarily—if not indispensably—required these stubborn and blinded interpreters, or rather perverters and destroyers of the Holy Word of **GOD**; as if it were impossible for him to correctly understand even the New Testament without their assistance. No; rather, it is only so that those for whom I write this, in the absence of thorough information and the relevant books, might nevertheless have sufficient report of it, and commit no error in either **excess** or **deficiency** <note>original: "**Excessu** noch **defectu**"</note>, but may test everything well, and choose and apply the best.

I have nothing at all to do with their philosophical whims, as they are well known to be of little significance, and their nature is more suited to haggling and storytelling than to philosophizing: so that Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian.</note>, in his *Philological Treasury* <note>original: "**Thesauro Philolog.**"</note> on page 245, wrote quite rightly of them: *even in secular matters the Hebrews are more blind* <note>original: "in profanis vel Heberidæ sunt cæciores"</note>; though I would still wish to exempt some of them. *See also Stillingfleet's Sacred Origins <note>original: "Stillingfl. Origin. S." refers to Edward Stillingfleet's *Origines Sacrae* (1662).</note> page 98 and following.*

<meta>catchword: Was</meta>

<summary>The author clarifies that his inclusion of Rabbinic interpretations in his Lexicon is for reference and comparison, not because he views them as necessary for understanding the New Testament. He expresses a dismissive view of Rabbinic philosophy, citing contemporary scholars like Hottinger and Stillingfleet to support his critique.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Exegetical Lexicon, Rabbinic interpretation, New Testament, Johann Heinrich Hottinger, Edward Stillingfleet, biblical scholarship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of Friedrich Opfergelt’s "Exegetical Lexicon." In the previous section, the author clarified that he is providing a dictionary of terms for less-experienced readers rather than a scholarly treatise, and expressed his skepticism regarding the necessity of Rabbinic writings for understanding the New Testament.</meta>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: none</meta>

As for their dogmatic, polemical, and Kabbalistic <term>Kabbalistic: Referring to Jewish mystical traditions and esoteric interpretations of the Torah.</term> writings, I wish to refer my reader to the late Dr. Wagenseil’s *The Fiery Darts of Satan* <note>original: "Tela ignea Satanæ," a famous 1681 collection of Jewish anti-Christian polemics with Latin refutations by Johann Christoph Wagenseil.</note> and Professor Eisenmenger’s *Judaism Unmasked* <note>original: "entdecktes Judenthum." This was a highly controversial and influential work by Johann Andreas Eisenmenger, published around 1700, which critiqued Rabbinic literature.</note>; where they will find such thorough information, and also such clear evidence of their abominable blasphemies against **GOD**, that their hair will stand on end. Therefore, I shall content myself here only with their antiquities <note>Refers to the study of ancient customs, laws, and history.</note> and exegesis <note>The critical explanation or interpretation of religious texts.</note>, among which the former serves our purposes more than the latter.

However, I wish to solemnly protest right here that, however freely I write from the heart, I by no means intend to offend anyone; just as, on the other hand, I cannot hide the fact that those who oppose my opinion and, in these present times, place such a great value on the Rabbinic writings, have not yet given any satisfaction <note>In this context, a convincing or sufficient response.</note> to my objections, however laboriously <note>original: "*operose*"</note> they may have answered such points when raised by others.

But so that I may be rightly understood regarding what I actually think of this study, and the benefit to be expected from it in the explanation of Holy Scripture, I will therefore reveal my own thoughts <note>original: "*animi sensa*"</note> on the matter.

First of all, I do not say that the Jewish writings provide Christian interpreters with absolutely no

<summary>The author refers readers to polemical works by Wagenseil and Eisenmenger for "dogmatic" Jewish writings, while focusing his own work on historical antiquities and exegesis. He defends his critical stance on Rabbinic literature while clarifying that he does not intend to give personal offense.</summary>
<keywords>Wagenseil, Eisenmenger, Kabbalah, polemics, Jewish antiquities, biblical exegesis, Rabbinic writings, 18th-century theology</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: Was</meta>

...writing a treatise here, but rather only delivering *titles* <note>In this context, dictionary entries or headings.</note> from a *Lexicon*, and indeed not for scholars, but only for those who have need of it.

The fact that I have now incorporated the first *title* into my *Exegetical Lexicon* did not happen because I believe that an *exegete* <note>A scholar who interprets and explains religious texts.</note>, when explaining divine pronouncements in these present times, primarily or even indispensably needs these stubborn and blinded interpreters—or rather, perverters and corrupters—of the Holy Word of **GOD**; such that he could not possibly understand even the New Testament correctly without their assistance. No; but only so that those for whom I write this, in the absence of thorough information and the relevant books, may nevertheless have sufficient report of it, and commit no error either in *excess* <note>original: "Excessu"</note> or *deficiency* <note>original: "defectu"</note>, but rather may test everything well, and choose and apply the best.

I have nothing at all to do here with their philosophical whims <note>original: "Grillen" - refers to fanciful or unfounded ideas.</note>, since, as is well known, they do not signify much, and their nature is better suited to haggling and fabling than to philosophizing: so that *Hottinger* <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss scholar of Oriental languages and theology.</note> in his *Philological Treasury* <note>original: "Thesauro Philolog," page 345.</note> wrote quite rightly of them: *in secular matters they are blinder than a bean-husk* <note>original: "in profanis vel leberide sunt cæciores." A Latin idiom for being extremely ignorant or unobservant.</note>: although I would still like to have some of them exempted from this judgment. *See also Stillingfleet's* <note>Edward Stillingfleet (1635–1699), an influential English theologian and Bishop of Worcester.</note> *Sacred Origins* <note>original: "Origin. S." referring to "Origines Sacrae."</note>, page 98 and following.

<summary>Opfergelt explains that his lexicon is intended for laypeople rather than scholars, providing a balanced view of Rabbinic interpretations which he generally views as corrupt but informative for historical context. He cites notable scholars like Hottinger and Stillingfleet to support his skeptical view of Jewish philosophical traditions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of Friedrich Opfergelt’s "Exegetical Lexicon." The author is moving from a general justification of his sources to specific recommendations for polemical reading, while clarifying his own stance on the utility of Jewish scholarship for Christian theologians.</meta>

# Preface.

<meta>Catchword: none</meta>

As for their dogmatic, polemical, and Kabbalistic <note>Referring to the Jewish mystical tradition.</note> writings, I will refer my reader to the late Dr. Wagenseil’s *Fiery Darts of Satan* <note>original Latin title: *Tela ignea Satanæ*. Published in 1681, this was a famous collection of Jewish anti-Christian polemics with Wagenseil's own rebuttals.</note>, and Professor Eisenmenger’s *Judaism Unmasked* <note>original German title: *Entdecktes Judenthum*. An extremely influential and controversial anti-Jewish work published around 1700.</note>; where they will encounter such thorough information, and also such clear examples of their abominable blasphemies, that their hair will stand on end. Therefore, I restrict myself here only to their *antiquity* and *exegesis* <note>The critical interpretation of religious texts.</note>, of which the former serves us more than the latter.

However, I wish to solemnly *protest* <note>In the older sense of making a public declaration or affirmation.</note> here at once that, as freely as I write from the heart, I do not intend in any way to offend anyone; just as I cannot hide, on the other hand, that those who oppose my opinion—and who, in these present times, hold Rabbinical writings in such very high regard—have not yet given any *satisfaction* <note>A sufficient answer or proof to resolve a doubt.</note> to my objections, no matter how *laboriously* <note>original: *operose*</note> they have offered such answers against others.

But so that I may be rightly understood as to what I actually think of this *study* <note>original: *Studio*</note>, and the benefit to be expected from it for the explanation of Holy Scripture, I will here reveal my *inner thoughts* <note>original Latin: *animi sensa*</note> on the matter.

Firstly, I do not say that Jewish writings [are] to Christian interpreters at all...

I wish they had shown us how far these sources are to be trusted, and how the divine utterances might be illustrated <note>clarified or explained</note> by them, or even set in opposition to them; this is something those in the Eastern Church <note>original: *Ecclesia Orientali*</note> in particular could have done. 1. Because the **Strength of Israel** <note>original: "Held in Israel," a biblical epithet for God from 1 Samuel 15:29</note> does not lie (1 Samuel 15:29, Numbers 23:19), He must speak as truthfully in one place of the **Bible** as in another, and never contradict <note>original: "contradiciren"</note> Himself. 3. If I do not understand the prophecies, <term>types <note>original: "typos," symbolic foreshadowings in the Bible</note></term>, <term>rites <note>original: "ritus"</note></term>, and ceremonies of the Old Testament as the shadow of Christ—which the Jews understand even much less, especially regarding the purpose **GOD** had for them in His holy counsel—then I will hold myself to Christ as the body <note>the substance or reality, as opposed to the "shadow" of the ritual</note> itself, and believe that which I do not understand, and through His grace endeavor to become His reflection.

4. If I, as a teacher, am to release Christ for my congregation from the swaddling cloths of the Old Testament and paint Him clearly before their eyes; and also, as the kernel of the entire Holy Scripture, take Him out of the shells of the <term>Levitical service <note>the religious system and priesthood described in the book of Leviticus</note></term>, and draw back the curtain before the <term>Tabernacle <note>original: "Stifts-Hütte," the portable sanctuary of the Israelites</note></term> and the <term>Holy of Holies <note>the innermost part of the sanctuary where God's presence dwelt</note></term>: then I will [make use] of the divine scriptures, the Evangelists, and Apostles, as much as they [are] necessary

# Preface.

...[most necessary] found to let me know, I will avail myself of in childlike simplicity; but the rest, which the inquisitive claim—though they may not be worthy or deserving of it, because **HOLY SCRIPTURE** was given to us as a precious gift of **GOD**, not for criticizing but for practicing—I shall present to them from the rich storehouse of our Christian books, and let them chew on it until they have become full and weary.

And that is indeed what I have written in this chapter, *Concerning the Rabbinic Explication of Holy Scripture* <note>original: "de sacræ scripturæ Rabbinica Explicatione"</note>, for those who have need of it. I summarize the <term>Talmud <note>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing legal discussions and traditions</note></term> for them, based on Joshua Ha-Levi’s *Ways of Eternity* <note>original: "Halicoth Olam," a 15th-century introductory manual to the Talmud</note>, and Maimonides’ famous work *The Strong Hand* <note>original: "Jad Chadzaka," also known as the Mishneh Torah, a comprehensive code of Jewish law</note>, along with some small notes as I deemed necessary. I point out to them the Christian authors who treat <term>Jewish Antiquities <note>the study of the history, laws, and customs of the ancient Jewish people</note></term>, as many as have become known to me. I give them a small but sufficient account of the different sects of the Jews, and how they—but especially their most prominent <term>Rabbis</term>—have explained the **HOLY SCRIPTURE**. I have also assembled a small <term>Rabbinical Library <note>original: "Bibliothecam Rabbinicam," here referring to a bibliography or list of essential books</note></term> for them, because such people are rarely in a position to access Mr. Wolf’s *Hebrew Library* <note>original: "Bibliothecam Hebræam," a massive multi-volume bibliography of Jewish literature by Johann Christoph Wolf, published 1715–1733</note>, which I...

# Preface.

[...firstly] <note>completing the word "allererst" from the previous page's catchword</note> encountered only at the conclusion of this work, and which will now undoubtedly be the best one to acquire.
Furthermore, I have also shown you how you should deal with the Jews in matters of faith, if such an encounter is to bear any fruit. And finally, I have also added a detailed description of the <term>Kabbalah <note>a school of thought in Jewish mysticism</note></term>, drawn from Menasse ben Israel’s *The Reconciler* <note>original: "Conciliatore," a 1632 work aiming to reconcile seemingly contradictory passages in the Hebrew Bible</note>, and concluded the entire little work with a—hopefully not unpleasant—account *concerning the roots* <note>original: "de Radicibus"</note> and the *hieroglyphic alphabet of Hebrew literature* <note>original: "Alphabeto literaturæ hebraicæ hieroglyphico"</note>.
Thus my main intention, as with all the entries of my *Exegetical Lexicon* <note>original: "Exegetischen Lexici"</note>, and likewise with these two, is this: while the entire learned world strives with truly astonishing diligence to *multiply the objects of erudition* <note>original: "Entia Eruditionis"</note>, I may show, *diametrically* <note>original: "e diametro"</note> opposed, how one might avoid, as much as possible when considering Holy Scripture, *being anxious and troubled about many things* <note>original: "μεριμνᾶν καὶ τυρβάζεσθαι περὶ πολλὰ" (a reference to Luke 10:41)</note>, so that one does not lose the *one thing necessary* <note>original: "Vnum necessarium"</note> because of it.

->Friederich Opfergelt, Prelate<-

### Concerning the Talmud's

א. [1.] **Name.** In Hebrew, "Talmud" (תלמוד) means the same as "The Teaching" does for us: for it is derived from the word *lamad* (למד), which means <term>he learned <note>original Latin: "didicit"</note></term>. Or, as it might be rendered according to "hieroglyphic literature" <note>a method of interpreting the individual letters of a word as symbolic of a larger concept</note>: **ת** (T) <term>continual</term> **ל** (L) <term>application</term> **מ** (M) <term>of multitude to multitude</term> **ו** (V) <term>by connecting something</term> **ד** (D) <term>to promote</term> <note>The author provides a Latin acrostic for the Hebrew letters: "continua admotione multitudini multitudinem annectendo aliquid promovere"</note>. And the Jews say that the Talmud is a <term>Commentary <note>original Latin: "Commentarius"</note></term> on the written Law, and that it was given to Moses orally at night, just as the former was given by day,

### Babylonian Talmud.
A 2

...has been handed down in writing. <note>original Latin: "Videatur Lightfootus Tom. I. Opp. p. m. 199. a." — See the works of John Lightfoot, Volume 1, page 199a. Lightfoot was a 17th-century English churchman and Hebrew scholar.</note>

## 2. Twofold Compilation.

For there is:

**α. The Jerusalem Talmud**, which was prepared by Rabbi Jochanan, who lived around the year 230 AD. Concerning this, Scheringham <note>Robert Sheringham, a 17th-century scholar of the Mishnah</note> writes <note>original Latin: "quod in Mischna gemaraque figmenta nulla, aut raro, & insolenter occurrant"</note> that in its Mishnah and Gemara, fables occur not at all, or only rarely and unusually. Nevertheless, and perhaps primarily for this reason, it is not held in as high regard by the Jewish community as the Babylonian version. Therefore, it will not be necessary to mention anything further about it here. <note>original Latin: "Dassovii Præfat. Diatr. de Resurr. mort." — See the preface to Theodor Dassov's "Dissertation on the Resurrection of the Dead."</note>

**β. The Babylonian Talmud**, which our Jewish neighbors use. This consists of:

**a. The Mishnah**, or the text, which they call <term>deuterosis <note>original Greek: "δευτέρωσιν," meaning "repetition" or "seconding"</note></term>, that is, the <term>secondary and oral law <note>original Latin: "legem secundariam & oralem"</note></term>. In this regard, we must investigate:

**aa. The Author.** He is said to be—as the Jews constantly assert—Rabbi Judah the Holy <note>Hebrew: "Rabbi Yehuda HaKadosh," the primary redactor of the Mishnah</note>. Around the year 150 AD, by order of the Emperor Antoninus, he is said to have collected all the <term>written fragments <note>original German: "Scartecken" — refers to various loose papers, notes, or pamphlets</note></term> that he found among his people into a single book. This is cited by Buxtorf in his work *The Jewish Synagogue* <note>original Latin: "Buxtorfius, in Synagoga Judaica, cap. III. p. 58. sq."</note>. He titled this book <term>Sepher Mishnayoth <note>original Hebrew: "ספר משניות"</note></term>, or the <term>Book of Traditions <note>original Latin: "librum Traditionum"</note></term>.

Regarding this, I will quote from Rabbi Joshua Levita's <term>Halicoth Olam <note>original Hebrew: "הליכות עולם," a 15th-century work on Talmudic methodology</note></term>, that is, the *Paths of the World* <note>original Latin: "Itinera Sæculi"</note>—

# Mischnah

...to no one <note>original Latin: "nemini"</note>, or eternal (from Habakkuk 3:5). Regarding this book, Buxtorfius <note>Johannes Buxtorf, a prominent 17th-century scholar of Hebrew and Jewish literature</note> writes in his *Rabbinic Library* <note>original Latin: "Biblioth. Rabb."</note>, page 335, that no one who desires to successfully advance and engage in the reading of Talmudic writers should or can be without it—namely, the *Talmudic Key* <note>original Latin: "Clave Talmudica"</note>. I will now share the necessary information.

From the days of Moses—it says there, according to the translation of the learned L'Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur, a Dutch Hebraist who died in 1648</note>, who rests in peace—who received the law on Mount Sinai directly from God himself, until our holy teacher (Judah), no book regarding the Law handed down by word of mouth <note>original Latin: "viva voce"</note> had been written down. This, however, was once the custom and Law in Israel. Sages received the teachings from one another and learned the statutes by word of mouth, just as they had been handed down. The only exception was that disciples would apply certain marks to these statutes and write them down so that forgetfulness, creeping in among many, would not take hold; yet they kept these writings hidden and called them the **Book of Secrets** <note>original Latin: "librum occultorum"</note>. This custom prevailed until a Priest with the **Urim and Thummim** <note>sacred objects used by the High Priest of Israel to determine God's will</note> appeared, serving his God with wisdom and the fear of sin. He is our holy teacher, the most upright of all. He noticed that the power of wisdom was perishing, that heavy afflictions were wisely being driven away into the farthest lands, that decrees were being renewed every year, and that the law handed down by mouth was being greatly suppressed...

### Of the Babylonian Talmud

...no one dared to even open their mouth <note>original Latin: "hiscere audere"</note>, due to the tyranny of the Greeks and others: who, in order to lure them away from the words of the living God, drafted decrees, until the times of the Rabbi <note>referring to Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi, also known as Judah the Prince, the 2nd-century redactor of the Mishnah</note>, for whom God secured the grace and favor of Antoninus <note>likely the Roman Emperor Marcus Aurelius or Antoninus Pius, who according to Jewish tradition shared a close friendship with Rabbi Judah</note>.

*Just observe how these blinded people willfully deceive themselves. Here it is stated that their ancestors kept their traditions hidden until the arrival of a Priest with the **Urim and Thummim** <note>sacred objects used by the High Priest of Israel to determine God's will</note>. Yet they themselves admit that the Urim and Thummim were no longer present during the Second Temple period; as the Talmudic writers explicitly state: **"Since the death of the former Prophets, the Urim and Thummim have ceased"** <note>original Latin: "a quo mortui fuerunt Prophetae priores, cessarunt Urim & Tummim"</note>. And yet, here their Rabbi Judah is supposed to be the man who possessed the Urim and Thummim.*

For they breathed a sigh of relief from their distress, and he himself obtained mercy, glory, and grace before him <note>the Emperor</note>, and secured peace for the Law. He, together with his Council <note>original Latin: "Senatu"</note> and with the consent of the sages living in his time, set out to compile the **Book of Traditions** received at Sinai. He did this according to the mandate of the Holy Scriptures in Psalm 119:126: **IT IS TIME FOR THE LORD TO ACT.** <note>original Latin: "JEHOVAH AGENDI TEMPVS EST"</note> Therefore, he called together and gathered those who, in that age, were students <note>original Greek: "ἀκροαμά-" (akroama-), likely completed on the next page as "akroamatōn," referring to those who had heard the oral teachings directly</note>

# Mishnah.

...they were most expert in the <note>original Greek: των</note> and Traditions. To such an extent, that all the sages would gather there to teach the judgments of God. Each person would then bring forward into the midst what they had received by hearing—namely, through a long chain of **ancestral tradition** <note>original Greek: πατροπαραδότως</note>—received from our Lord, the blessed and eminent Moses. Therefore, our teacher <note>Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi</note> wrote down the Traditions **indisputably** <note>original Greek: ἀναμφισβητήτως</note>, and attributed those points where the sages disagreed to their respective authors by name in their proper places.

My God, what things these are! Firstly, regarding the Talmud itself, or the <term>Oral Law</term> <note>the Jewish tradition that God gave Moses additional laws by word of mouth that were not written in the Torah</note>, there is not the slightest trace to be found in the entire Old Testament that God was supposed to have given such oral laws to Moses alongside the written ones. Those among us <note>referring to Christian Hebraists or scholars who accepted Jewish claims about the antiquity of the Oral Law</note> should rightly be ashamed who, to please the Jews, want to conclude this from the words of **Exodus 33:11**: *“And the Lord spoke to Moses face to face, as a man is wont to speak to his friend,”* and **Numbers 12:8**: *“For I speak to him mouth to mouth: and plainly, and not through riddles and figures he sees the Lord.”*

As if these verses did not much more relate to the **Written Law**! Indeed, how zealously did our most precious Savior—as the best Interpreter of the Law—strive against the **ancestral traditions** <note>original Greek: πατροπαράδοτα</note> introduced by the Pharisees, or the prece- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword indicates the word is "precepts" or "ordinances"</note>

<summary>The author describes the Jewish belief that the Mishnah was compiled from a chain of oral tradition dating back to Moses, then immediately pivots to a critical argument that there is no biblical evidence for an "Oral Law."</summary>
<keywords>Mishnah, Oral Law, Talmud, Moses, Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi, Exodus, Numbers, Pharisees, Christian-Jewish polemics</keywords>

# Mishnah.

...the law of the fathers, and always opposed these now-Talmudic sayings to the ancient, written Law?

Furthermore, what this deceiver <note>Likely referring to the Jewish traditionalists or the redactor of the Mishnah</note> brings forward regarding **Antoninus Pius** <note>A legend in the Talmud claims that the Roman Emperor Antoninus Pius was a close friend or even a secret convert and student of Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi</note> is found in not a single **writer** <note>original Latin: *Scribenten*</note> who has described the life of this virtuous Emperor or otherwise noted his kindness. Thus, this whole heap of junk is nothing but a silly Jewish fiction.

**bb. The Style.** This is brief, impure, and **obscure**; yet nevertheless it is not as difficult as the style found in the **Gemara** <note>The second part of the Talmud, consisting of rabbinical commentary on the Mishnah</note>. Anyone who wishes to read it with profit must have a considerable knowledge of **antiquity**. **See Leusden, *Hebrew-Mixed Philology*, Dissertation 12, page 88.**

**cc. The Versions.** These vary. For as the man was <note>the translator</note>, so too the translation turned out. Among others, the versions by Buxtorf, L’Empereur, Scheringham, Coccejus, Wagenseil, Schmidt, and Surenhusius have gained great fame. Now, this last one <note>Willem Surenhuys (Surenhusius)</note> indeed published the entire **Mishnah in the year 1698** in Hebrew and Latin, for which the lovers of **Rabbinic oracles** <note>original Latin: *Oraculorum*; refers here to the authoritative legal pronouncements in the text</note> have long yearned; but who will [buy] them, unless he has very large, superfluous means...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Mishnah's publication history, specifically focusing on the massive 1698 Amsterdam edition by Surenhusius. # Mishnah.

...be able to purchase [them], as they consist of six folio volumes, and were furthermore printed in Amsterdam?

**dd. The Division and Contents.** We find these nowhere better described than in the following manner from the previously mentioned Levite's *Halichot Olam* <note>original: *Halichot Olam* (Ways of Eternity); a famous 15th-century introductory work to the Talmud by Yeshua ben Yosef ha-Levi</note>:

> This secondary book of the law <note>original Latin: *liber legis secundarius*; a term often used by Christian scholars to describe the Mishnah as a "second law"</note> rests upon five firm columns, consisting of five kinds: just as that great light, the perfectly wise one, our teacher **Moses, son of Maimon** <note>The famous medieval philosopher and jurist Maimonides, also known as Rambam</note>, wrote in the preface to his **Seeds** <note>original Hebrew: זרעים (*Zera'im*); the first of the six "Orders" of the Mishnah, primarily concerning agricultural laws</note> in the Mishnayoth, or *repetitions* <note>original Greek: δευτερώσεις (*deuteroseis*); a term used by the early Church Fathers to describe the Mishnah as a "repeated" or oral law</note> of the Commentaries.

> In the **first kind**, explanations are proposed that were received from the Almighty Himself through the intervention of Moses: which have hidden reasons in scripture, from whence they can be deduced by conjecture. Here there is no disagreement; but when someone affirms that he received this or that from his ancestors, it is not permitted to oppose him as if he were proven a liar.

> In the **second kind** are contained the statutes about which it is customary to say: *Laws of Moses from Mount Sinai* <note>original Latin: *Ritus Mosis e Monte Sinai*; corresponding to the Hebrew term *Halakhah le-Moshe mi-Sinai*, referring to laws with no biblical source but traditionally attributed to Moses</note>. These are learned neither by conjecture nor by dialectical reasoning: such as the black straps <note>referring to the requirement that the leather straps of the *tefillin* (phylacteries) be dyed black</note>, and the eleven days between two menstrual conditions, and many other things similar to these. In these also, they do not disagree.

<meta>catchword: Thirdly</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the 1698 Amsterdam edition of the Mishnah and begins a classification of its contents into five categories, drawing on the authority of Maimonides and Yeshua ben Yosef ha-Levi. It specifically defines the first two categories of Rabbinic law: divinely revealed explanations and laws traditionally attributed to Moses at Sinai.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the systematic categorization of the Mishnah's contents, following the classification system established by Maimonides. It picks up from the second category of laws discussed on the previous page.</meta>

### Mishnah.

<margin>A 5</margin>

In the **third category**, those ordinances are presented which are drawn out through logical inference, arguments from analogy, and other forms of evidence; and for this reason, the Sages disagree about them, whether it be two or more of them. Just as you will find throughout the whole Gemara <note>The part of the Talmud containing rabbinical analysis and commentary on the Mishnah</note> that they investigate the reasoning of the discourse which leads the dissenting parties in different directions, and likewise the lines of reasoning which they work to defend. For example, when they say in various places: *For what cause do they disagree?*—this Teacher <note>original Latin: *Doctor*; referring to a Rabbi of the Mishnaic or Talmudic era</note> considers the matter for himself by this reasoning, and the other by another. The "Great Light" <note>A common honorific for the medieval scholar Maimonides</note> deals with this matter at length in his previously mentioned preface to **Seeds** <note>original Hebrew: זרעים (*Zera’im*); the first section of the Mishnah, primarily concerning agricultural laws</note>. Let that passage be consulted.

In the **fourth category**, those decrees are proposed which the Sages of each individual generation established in order to place a "fence" around the law, and to encircle it as if with a stone wall. This is according to the command of God the Lawgiver in Leviticus 18:30 <note>The source text cites verse 13, but the Latin quote *observabitis observationem meam* corresponds to verse 30</note>: *And you shall keep my charge*—meaning, add a charge to my charge. And in these matters, it happens that the Sages disagree among themselves: when one person decrees in this way for this reason, while another, for a different reason, does not agree with him.

In the **fifth category** are contained the customs and ordinances whose observation the Prophets and Sages introduced, regarding both prohibitions and permissions, as well as in civil law <note>original Latin: *Judiciis pecuniariis*; literally "monetary judgments" or financial cases</note>. As when they say: "Moses ordained for Israel"; and similarly Joshua and Ezra, and the others. Are these not in the book of Mishnayoth <note>The plural form of Mishnah, referring to the collection of oral traditions</note>, or...

<meta>catchword: vete-</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the final three categories of Mishnaic law: laws derived through logic and debate, precautionary "fences" to protect divine law, and customs established by historical leaders like Moses, Joshua, and Ezra.</summary>

### Division of the Talmud

...are the writings of the ancient Constitutions <note>original Latin: *Constitutionum*; referring to the traditional legal rulings or Halakhot</note> read? In the same way they say: "Thus ordained Hillel"; "Thus ordained Master Joannes, son of Zachai" <note>Referring to the prominent scholar Yohanan ben Zakkai, a leader during and after the destruction of the Second Temple</note>: and there are many things of this kind.

These are the types of Constitutions which our holy Master <note>original Latin: *D. noster sanctus*; a reference to Rabbi Judah the Prince (Yehuda HaNasi), the compiler of the Mishnah</note> included in his compilation: and out of all of them, he constructed six classes, for which a mnemonic <note>original Greek: μνημόσυνον (*mnēmosynon*)</note> is found in Isaiah 33:6. It is thus: 1. stability, 2. of your times, 3. strength, 4. of all salvation, 5. wisdom, and 6. knowledge: the fear of the LORD is His treasure. <note>The author is using a traditional rabbinic interpretation (found in the Talmud, Shabbat 31a) that links each word of this biblical verse to one of the six orders of the Mishnah.</note>

These are the classes: Seeds, Festivals, Women, Damages, Holy Things, and Purifications, for which the Jews have a sign:

# Sages
<meta>catchword: proven.</meta>

...origin of the Sadducees, <note>the text likely begins with the end of the word 'Ursprunges' (origin)</note> namely that after this life, neither any reward for the good nor any punishment for the evil is to be expected; I shall here include a passage from his treatise *On Creation* <note>original: *de Creatione*</note>:

> Antigonus used to say that God should not be served for the sake of a reward or payment. By this, however, he intended to suggest nothing other than that we ought to be so disposed in mind that, even without the prospect or expectation of a reward, and solely out of love for the Divine Majesty, we should endure everything and perform all things for His honor and glory. Nevertheless, Zadok and † Boethus <note>original: *Baytos*; traditionally cited as a student of Antigonus and co-founder of the Sadducean/Boethusian movements</note> took this as an opportunity to spread that most foul error: that after this life there remains for us neither reward nor punishment, and consequently, that souls are not immortal at all. <note>original: *Antigonus dicere consvevit...*</note>

† Boethus was Zadok’s fellow student <term>Condiscipulus (a schoolmate or fellow student)</term>, who propagated the same error alongside him, though not as harshly or vehemently as Zadok. And although Rabbi Gedaliah <note>likely Gedaliah ibn Yahya, author of the 16th-century historical work *Shalshelet Ha-Kabbalah*</note> writes that Zadok and Boethus had formed two distinct sects, nonetheless Dr. Johann Benedict Carpzov <note>a prominent 17th-century German Lutheran theologian and Hebraist</note>, in his *Introduction to Jewish Theology* <note>original: *introductione in Theologiam Judaicam*</note>, Chapter II, Section 3, has quite thoroughly proven <note>the word "proven" (bewiesen) is split across this page and the next, indicated by the catchword 'wiesen'</note> the opposite.

<meta>This page transitions from the history of the Mishnic sages (Tannaim) to a detailed definition and etymological analysis of the Gemara, the second major component of the Talmud. It continues the systematic categorization begun on the previous page.</meta>

<header># Gemara.</header>

bo <term>gamar (גְּמַר - to finish)</term> <note>original Latin: *consummavit, perfecit, absolvit, complevit*</note>—to finish, perfect, complete, or fulfill. And perhaps <term>Gemara (גְּמָרָא)</term>, by the power of its letters, might mean: **g** (ג) the turning of the multitude, **m** (מ) from the place of comprehension, into **r** (ר) the activity of going forth, and **a** (א) the Completion. That would then signify a bringing-to-an-end of a work that had not yet been finished. This sounds almost like the definition of a certain learned man:
> The whole Talmud <note>original: *Thalmud totum sumtum*</note> is named after <term>lamad (לָמַד - to teach)</term>; but its higher part is called **Gemara**, as if you were to say, a supplement or completion—namely of the **Mishnah** <note>original: *Michnæ*</note>: or more clearly from <term>gamar (גְּמַר)</term> in the sense of perfection, because the **Talmud**, that vast Body of Jewish Ecclesiastical and Civil Law, received its desired perfection through the **Gemara**.

Rabbi Eliakim Panzi of Rovigo <note>original: *R. Eliakim Panzi Rhodiginus*; Panzi was a 16th-century Italian scholar</note> defined the **Gemara** as follows:
> **Gemara** is the science teaching those things which pertain to the soul of man, specifically how through the proper observation of the commandments and the avoidance of sin, one may reach eternal happiness.

bb. **The Authors.** These are likewise reviewed by Levita <note>Joshua Levita, a 15th-century scholar and author of *Halicoth Olam* ("Paths of the World"), a foundational guide to Talmudic logic</note> in his **Halicoth-Olam**, **Treatise I, Chapter II, § 15.** I wish, however, to set down here only the last two who are said to have closed <note>completed the editing of</note> the **Gemara**. These are called **Ase** <note>Rav Ashi, the primary redactor of the Babylonian Talmud</note> and **Rab. Ina** <note>Rav Ina or Abina, Ashi's successor</note>, regarding whom he explains the passage **Psalm 73:17** in an incomparable way: Un-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the authors of the Gemara, specifically focusing on the final editors, the legendary two versions of the text, and the linguistic style of the work.</meta>

### Gemara Authors

...the marks of these people you find in Psalm 73:17: *Until I went into the Sanctuaries of God; then understood I their end.* <note>original Latin: "Donec ingressus essem in Sanctuaria DEI, animadvertissem finem istorum."</note> Here, "Sanctuaries" <note>original: *Sanctuaria*</note> designates Mar Ashi; the Hebrew word for "I understood" <note>original Latin: *animadvertissem*</note> contains a sign of Rav Ina; the words "their end" <note>original Latin: *finem istorum*</note> designate the end of the authors of the <term>Gemara (the final completion of the Talmudic commentary)</term>. By these men, and in their time, the <term>Gemara</term> was finished and sealed. And in such an absurd manner, the beloved Holy Scripture is misused almost throughout by the foolish Gemarists.

cc. **The Copies.** Regarding this, Levita <note>Joshua Levita, mentioned on the previous page</note> gives this report in the same place: Master Ashi composed two copies of the <term>Gemara</term>, a first and a later *cyclopedia* <note>original Greek: κυκλοπαιδείαν; referring to a complete circle of education or a comprehensive work</note>: one long, the other short. In this, he followed the habit of all authors who usually revise the books they have written by correcting, adding, or taking away. And in this way the <term>Gemara</term> was sealed: so that no one was permitted to add or subtract anything, except for the Teachers called the *Opinators* <note>original Latin: *Opinatores*</note> or *Conjectors* <note>original Latin: *Conjectores*; these refer to the Saboraim, the scholars who followed the Amoraim and finalized the Talmudic text</note> who followed after it and added certain things, just as we have received from those on high. For example, at the beginning of *Kiddushu* <note>original Hebrew: קדשו - "Sanctify," likely referring to a specific tractate or liturgical section</note>. But Master Achai <note>likely Rav Achai Gaon, an 8th-century scholar</note> objects: that this was drawn from their own tradition and opinion.

# Copies, Style, and Versions

...wrote a <term>Key to the Gemara (an introductory guide to the Talmudic commentary)</term> <note>original Latin: *Clavem Gemaricam*</note>, which the learned *Rittmeier* translated into Latin, and which Mr. von *Bashuysen* published along with his *Great Talmudic Key* <note>original Latin: *Clave Talmudica maxima*</note> in the year 1714 in <term>quarto (a book size where the sheet is folded twice to create four leaves)</term>. But this learned man responds to the matter as follows: *Nothing is difficult for the willing; learn in an orderly fashion; choose a skilled and faithful teacher; begin with the treatises translated into the Latin language, of which there are many in the <term>Mishnah (the primary collection of Jewish oral laws)</term>, since we now have the whole work dressed in a Latin garment through the care of Surenhusius—Note Well: truly by the singular grace of GOD.* <note>original Latin: "Volenti nihil est arduum, discas ordine, elige Præceptorem peritum & fidelem, incipe a Tr. versis in linguam latinam, quales in Mischnam plures, quam jam cura Surenhusii totam latina veste indutam habemus, NB. singulari sane beneficio DEI."</note>

ee. **The Translations.** <note>original: "Die Versiones"</note> Of these, very few are to be found. *Coccejus* has published some excerpts from the <term>Sanhedrin (laws concerning the courts)</term> and <term>Makkot (laws regarding corporal punishment)</term>. *Ezardus* translated Chapter 1 of <term>Avodah Zarah (laws concerning foreign worship)</term>. *Königius* translated the beginning of the Tractate <term>Berakhot (prayers and blessings)</term>. *L’Empereur* and *Wagenseil* produced some excerpts, and *Rittmeyer*, as already mentioned, translated the *Key to the Gemara* by Rabbi Eliakim Panzi. For this reason, the learned Mr. von *Bashuysen*—in a public letter to Dr. *Löscher*, as he mentions in the preface to his *System of Minor Hebrew Antiquities* <note>original Latin: *Systemat. Antiquit. Hebr. Min.*</note> published in 1715 on page 23—resolved to translate the <term>Gemara</term> himself. *For a more extensive review of the Talmudic work, with an alphabetical index of its books and chapters, see Buxtorf’s excellent book on Hebrew Abbreviations, page 217 and following.* <note>original Latin: "Prolixiorem operis Thalmudici recensionem, cum ejusdem librorum & capitum alphabetico indice, vide in Buxtorfii peregregio libro de Abbreviaturis Hebraicis, p. 217. seqq."</note>

The Authority of the Talmud among the Jews. <note>original Latin: *Autoritas Thalmudis apud Judæos.*</note>

## a. The High Esteem for the Babylonian Talmud among the Jews.

I will perhaps have done enough here if I show that they hold it higher than the Bible itself. For thus speak their <term>Magistri (the masters or rabbis)</term> in the <term>Tractate Berakhot (the first volume of the Talmud, dealing with blessings and prayers)</term> of the Jerusalem Talmud:
> Know that the words of the Sages are more to be desired than the words of the Law, and the words of the Elders more than the words of the Prophets. <note>original Latin: "Scias, verba Sapientum magis desideranda esse, quam verba legis, & verba Seniorum, quam verba Prophetarum."</note>

Likewise, in <term>Sanhedrin (the volume dealing with the high court and legal procedures)</term>, Chapter 10: One sins more gravely against the words of the Scribes than against the words of the Law. <note>original Latin: "gravius peccatur circa verba Scribarum, quam verba legis."</note> In <term>Masechet Soferim (a minor tractate concerning the rules for writing sacred scrolls)</term>, chapter 12, law 7, it says:
> The Biblical text is like water, the <term>Mishnah (the primary collection of Jewish oral laws)</term> is like wine, and the Talmud (<term>Gemara (the commentary on the Mishnah)</term>) is like spiced wine. <note>original Latin: "Textus Biblicus est similis aquæ, Mischna Vino, & Thalmud (Gemara) Vino condito."</note>

And in law 8:
> Further, the Law is like salt, the Mishnah like pepper, and the Talmud like aromatic spices. <note>original Latin: "Ulterius similis est Lex Sali, Mischna piperi, Thalmud aromatibus."</note>

More on this can be found in Schickard’s *Examination of Commentaries* page 6; Hackspan’s notes on *Nizzachon* page 268; Buxtorf’s *Talmudic Lexicon* page 1171; *Jewish Abbreviations* page 222 following; *The Jewish Synagogue* page 57; in Gerson’s *Talmud* page 213; Witsius’s *The Judaizing Christian* page 171 following; Eisenmenger’s *Judaism Unmasked* Volume 1, pages 239, 330; and in Leusden’s *Philologus Hebræo-Mixtus*, Dissertation 12, page 84 following. <note>These references point to a wide range of 17th-century Christian scholarship on Jewish literature, ranging from linguistic studies to polemical attacks like Eisenmenger's.</note>

Therefore, if anything regarding the **Holy Trinity** and the **Messiah** should be found in the allegorical writings of the Jews, it will not be difficult for them to interpret it as something else entirely. For everyone is free to interpret allegories, just like poetic verses, according to their own pleasure.

# Concerning the Talmud and others

In Chapter 1, Chapter 4, Section 3, number 13, on page 92, one finds:

There are primarily two types of their (the **Rabbis’**) writings. Some are **commentaries on the Old Testament** <note>original: *V. T.* for *Vetus Testamentum*</note>, which are not very difficult to understand for those who understand the context itself and have devoted a moderate effort to them, aided by the **Rabbinic Lexicon of Johann Buxtorf** <note>Johann Buxtorf the Elder (1564–1629) was a pioneering Christian Hebraist; his lexicon was the primary tool used by Christian scholars to decode post-biblical Hebrew and Aramaic.</note>. But since they are by more recent Rabbis, and can in no way be compared to the highly-regarded **Christian interpreters**, who employ far more resources than the race of Rabbis and surpass them greatly in judgment; *I see no reason why they should be valued as highly as they usually are by some; except that they are not commonly read, and it is a fine thing to appear to understand those things which are unknown to others.* 

But if the matter itself is examined, they are in no way to be equated with the more noble Christian grammarians and critics. There are, however, other writings, either **allegorical** or pertaining to **Talmudic Doctrine**, which teach us what the Jews thought several centuries after Christ; but they do little to illustrate the Old Testament. The **allegorical teachers** <note>original: *Allegorici Doctores*</note> invent whatever they wish; the **Talmudists** pursue all rituals with the greatest subtlety and supply from their own brains <note>original: *ex cerebro suo*; a common idiomatic expression in Latin for "making things up" or "inventing from one's own imagination."</note> whatever seems to be missing from the written Law, with almost no regard for grammar or history. This is something students will easily understand, even from the various **Talmudic books**...

# Author’s Advice on Writing, etc.

...more toward these traditions than toward the written Law, allowing themselves to fall into such powerful errors. Indeed, there are those who also set forth and defend the <term>modes of speaking</term> <note>original Latin: *formulas loquendi*</note> found in the New Testament against the blasphemies of the Jews, using the Jews' own writings. This was done with great diligence by the learned Surenhusius <note>Willem Surenhuis (c. 1664–1729), a Dutch scholar famous for his Latin translation of the Mishnah.</note> in his fine book titled *Sefer ha-Mishnah* <note>Hebrew: "The Book of the Mishnah"</note> or *The Book of Reconciliation* <note>original Greek: *βίβλος καταλλαγῆς* (*biblos katallagēs*)</note>, which was published in quarto in the year 1713. And who can thank—or rather, sufficiently thank—these faithful and honest men for such arduous work and effort?

However, I would wish and advise that the errors, fables, lies, deceptions, and blasphemies truly and tangibly found in the <term>Talmud</term> <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah (oral law) and the Gemara (commentary).</note>—of which the late Mr. Dr. Pfeiffer <note>August Pfeiffer (1640–1698), a German Lutheran theologian and orientalist.</note> cited various examples in his *Sacred Criticism* <note>original Latin: *Critica Sacra*</note>, page 503 and following—should not be downplayed, excused, or defended. This is especially important since it is well known that the authors of these Talmudic sayings understood their fables not allegorically—as some more recent Rabbis (see Dr. Scherzer’s <note>Johann Adam Scherzer (1628–1689), a Lutheran theologian and professor at Leipzig.</note> *Trifolium*, page 164 and following) wish them to be understood for the sake of avoiding shame—but <term>literally</term> <note>original Greek: *κατὰ γράμμα* (*kata gramma*)</note> and <term>strictly</term> <note>original Latin: *proprie*</note>. Inasmuch as they went to great trouble to verify these things with Holy Scripture, as Hulfius, Leusdenius <note>Johannes Leusden (1624–1699), a prominent Dutch Hebraist.</note>, and Pfeiffer have sufficiently proven; but rather, as the learned Mr.

# Author’s Advice

...as van Bashuysen <note>Heinrich Jakob van Bashuysen (1679–1750), a German Hanau-based scholar and printer of Hebrew works.</note> has brought a fine example to light in his learned work titled *The Great Talmudic Key* <note>original Latin: *Clavis Thalmudica maxima*</note>, in Dissertation 1, page 68 and following. He wished to call a spade a spade <note>original Latin: *Scapham Scapham nennen*, literally "to call a boat a boat," meaning to speak plainly about the truth.</note> and thus keep his conscience pure before **God** and Christendom. To this category also belongs the exceptional diligence of the late Dr. Wagenseil <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705), a Christian Hebraist whose work *Tela Ignea Satanae* (The Fiery Darts of Satan) compiled Jewish polemics against Christianity.</note> in his *Fiery Darts of Satan*, and of Professor Eisenmenger <note>Johann Andreas Eisenmenger (1654–1704), whose massive work *Entdecktes Judenthum* (Judaism Unmasked) became a controversial source for anti-Jewish critique.</note> in his *Judaism Unmasked*, among many others.

And one more thing. It would perhaps also contribute not a little to the conversion of the Jews if our Christian <term>Talmudists</term> <note>Scholars, often Christian theologians, who studied the Talmud to better understand the roots of Christianity or to engage in mission work.</note> were to apply themselves to the Talmud in such a way that—instead of their previous efforts to illustrate the New Testament for us Christians using the Talmud—they now reversed the process. They should explain the Talmud to the Jews from the Bible, and especially from the New Testament. This should be done in their own Rabbinic or even German language and Jewish style of writing, in the same manner that the late Caspar Calvör <note>Caspar Calvör (1650–1725), a prominent Lutheran theologian.</note> published his *The Glory of Jesus the Messiah* <note>Hebrew: *Kevod Yeshua Mashiach*</note> in quarto in the year 1710. # Review of the Work of Maimonides

*Carpzov* <note>Johann Benedict Carpzov II (1639–1699), a notable Lutheran theologian and Hebraist.</note>, and even *Richard Simon* <note>Richard Simon (1638–1712), a French priest and influential biblical critic who was often skeptical of Rabbinic traditions.</note>, who otherwise bears no goodwill toward the Rabbis, have judged him so. Regarding *Cunaeus* <note>Petrus Cunaeus (1586–1638), author of "The Hebrew Republic," a foundational text on Jewish law and government.</note>, I will say nothing; for he would have "licked his spit" <note>A colorful idiom suggesting that Cunaeus held Maimonides in such high esteem that he would have been desperate to acquire any portion of his work.</note> if he could have only obtained it.

And this work, ***The Strong Hand*** <note>original Latin: *Manus fortis*</note>, which is a fairly large ***Compendium of the Talmud*** <note>original Latin: *Compendium Thalmudis*</note> and written in a neat and pure style, is the very book from which both Christians and Jews writing on Jewish antiquities have taken the most. Therefore, it certainly **deserves** to be **reviewed** here.

And here I wish first to report that this book was most recently published in the year 1702 in **Amsterdam**, in two folio volumes. Furthermore, as for the **title**, the author named it **Hand** <note>original Latin: *Manum*; Hebrew: *Yad* (יד)</note> after the fourteen books of which this work consists <note>In Hebrew, the letters of the word "Yad" (Hand) have a numerical value of 14.</note>, and he added to it the **epithet** **Strong** <note>original Latin: *fortis*; Hebrew: *HaChazakah* (החזקה)</note>.

The book itself has, as mentioned, XIV books and IV parts or **volumes**. The first part contains III books. The first book is called ***The Book of Knowledge*** <note>original Hebrew: *Sepher hamada*; Latin: *liber de Scientia*</note>, and it treats: 1. **on the foundations of the law**; 2. **on various opinions regarding the law of Moses**; 3. **on the study of the law**; 4. **on idolatry**; 5. **on repentance**.

The second book is called ***The Book of Love*** <note>original Hebrew: *Sepher Ahabha*</note>

# The Weakening of the Talmud, #

all offense is avoided, presented in a fine **Latin style** <note>original: *Lateinischen Stilo*</note>: yet how few of those in the **preaching office** <note>The clergy and ministers.</note>—for whom it was primarily written—ever get to see any of it; and the simple and unlearned do not understand it at all. Nevertheless, if these simple people merely walk in a holy life according to the **rule of faith** <note>The basic doctrines of Christianity.</note>, they obtain the end of their faith: the **salvation of their souls** <note>original: *Seelen Seligkeit*</note>. Thus, I do not see what advantage or benefit the Kingdom of God gains from these works, for the sake of which we are supposed to **meditate**, **elaborate**, **publish**, and **propose** <note>original: *meditiren*, *elaboriren*, *divulgiren* and *proponiren*</note> everything.

Indeed, when one fully considers how the writers of **antiquities** <note>Scholars who study the ancient history and customs of a people.</note> frequently **contradict** <note>original: *contradiciren*</note> one another—depending on whether one has followed this **system** <note>original: *Systemati*</note> and another a different one—one finally does not know where they stand. For who does not know how our **Rambam** <term>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and jurist.</term> regards the **Talmud** <term>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing the discussions and rulings of ancient rabbis.</term> in various places as erroneous and **doubtful** <note>original: *dubieus*</note>? In his book ***The Guide for the Perplexed*** <note>original: *More-Nevochim*</note> *Chapter LXXI, page 132*, he expressly writes: that the Jews have lost the knowledge of countless things which would otherwise be very useful for resolving many difficulties in **Holy Scripture** <term>original: *H. Schrifft* (Heilige Schrift).</term>; and this is because they only interpreted the Law **orally** <note>Referring to the Jewish tradition of the Oral Torah.</note>. Therefore, whatever might still remain in the Talmud and other allegorical

### And the Authority of the Talmudists. ###

[...what] stands in the commentaries is nothing certain. And if one subsequently examines Rabbi Jacob ben Asher’s <term>Jacob ben Asher (c. 1269–1343), an influential halakhic authority.</term> *Arba’ah Turim* <note>A foundational code of Jewish law; the title translates as "The Four Rows."</note>, which is likewise an epitome <note>A summary or condensed version.</note> of the **Talmud**, and was printed in folio in Berlin in the year 1703; and notes how often this author critiques **Maimonides**, and also later explains the pronouncements of his **Sages** <note>original: *Sapientum*</note> quite differently than his predecessors; then this is a clear proof that what we have found in the **Talmud** outside of **Holy Scripture** stands on a sandy foundation.

However, I now go further and also report that the learned English professor **Edward Pococke** <note>An influential 17th-century English Orientalist and biblical scholar.</note> published a book in quarto at Oxford in the year 1655, which he titled ***Bab Musa*** <term>The Gate of Moses; a collection of Maimonides' Arabic prefaces to his commentary on the Mishnah.</term> <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *באב מוסי Portam Mosis*</note>: this contains various dissertations which this Rabbi Moses ben Maimon placed before his commentaries on the **Mishnah** <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions.</note>, in which almost all Jewish disciplines are revealed.

I wish also to add, for the sake of abundance—which I should have noted earlier—that when reading such things that come from the Jews, one must always keep in mind what **Origen** <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD).</note> already wrote in his time in *Against Celsus* <note>original: *contra Celsum*</note>, Book II, regarding these people: 
> Everything of the Jews now is myths and nonsense. <note>original Greek: *πάντα μὲν τὰ Ιεδαίων τῶν νῦν μῦθοι καὶ λῆρος*</note>

That all their things, in this regard, are only poems and fables, which,

### The Undermined Authority of the Talmudists. ###

-things <note>Completing the word from the previous page.</note>, as **Grotius** <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch jurist and theologian.</note>, who otherwise was very much inclined toward Jewish views <note>original: *gejudenhet*</note>, says with truth in his annotations on Matthew 24:24: *they are the worst masters of history, for since the time they were expelled from their homeland, all history among them has been tainted with gross errors and fables; therefore, nothing they say is to be believed unless other witnesses are added.* <note>original Latin: *pessimi Historiæ Magistri, nam ex quo patria expulsi sunt, omnis apud illos historia crassis erroribus & fabulis est inquinata, quibus & proinde nihil credendum est, nisi alii testes accedant.*</note> And as **Isaac Casaubon** <note>A renowned classical scholar (1559–1614).</note> also critiques them with this sharp rebuke in his *Exercises against Baronius* <note>A work critiquing the *Annales Ecclesiastici* of Cardinal Caesar Baronius.</note> XVI. 5. 8: *To the Rabbis, when the Hebrew language is discussed, and the specific property of a word, or some Talmudic institution, Christians rightly attribute not a little; but where one moves from words to things, or to history,* (**Glassius** <note>Salomon Glassius (1593–1656), a Lutheran theologian.</note> also added in parentheses, *as also to the sense of Holy Scripture, especially in those places which deal with the Messiah,*) *or the explanation of the ancient affairs of the old people, unless we wish to be misled and deceived, one must hold no faith in them at all. I could easily prove this with six hundred arguments, if that were my purpose now.* <note>original Latin: *Rabbinis, ubi de lingva Hebraica agitur... vel rerum antiquarum veteris populi explicationem, nisi falli & decipi volumus, nihil admodum esse illis fidei habendum. Who have treated the *Jewish Antiquities* <note>original: *Antiquitates Judaicas*</note>, are found in considerable number in the sixth volume of the *English Critical Work* <note>Likely referring to the *Critici Sacri*, a massive 17th-century collection of biblical commentaries published in London.</note>. ## II. I intend to present the Jewish Commentaries in three periods.

A. The first period shall be from the Year of the World 3488 (500 years before the birth of Christ) until the Year of Christ 500—a span of 1000 years. In this period, following the Babylonian Captivity, the Levites first began to translate the Holy Scripture for the common people from the Hebrew language into their then-current Aramaic dialect, which they had heard for a full 70 years in Chaldea. They showed the people the literal meaning of the text. This was the <term>Metaphrastic Method</term> <note>A literal, word-for-word translation style aimed at making the text accessible to those who no longer spoke the original Hebrew.</note>, as seen from the Book of Nehemiah 

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: chapter</meta>

->Of the Jews<-

to be seen in chapter VIII, 8 <note>Referring to the Book of Nehemiah, chapter 8, verse 8, describing how the Levites helped the people understand the Law.</note>; where it is expressly stated: **paraphrastically by adding the sense, and thus they provided understanding through the Scripture itself** <note>original: "Metaphrastice apponendo sensum, & sic dederunt intelligentiam per Scripturam ipsam"</note>; or, as *Junius* and *Tremellius* <note>Franciszek Junis and Immanuel Tremellius were 16th-century Protestant scholars famous for their Latin translation of the Bible.</note> have translated it: **for they read that book, the law of GOD, clearly, and by explaining the sense they provided understanding through the scripture itself.** <note>original: "legebant enim librum illum, legem DEI explanate, & exponendo sensum dabant intelligentiam per scripturam ipsam."</note>

But when they subsequently, just like their fathers in the wilderness, became weary of this their native manna <note>A metaphor for the plain, literal word of God, referencing the Israelites' dissatisfaction with the miraculous food provided during the Exodus.</note> and grew lustful for the Greek language, behold, they also began to **imitate** the Greek **Philosophers**, and fell into purely allegorical explanations, just as the Christian **Fathers** <note>The "Church Fathers," early influential Christian theologians.</note> did later on.

If the book **Zohar** <term>Zohar: The foundational work of Jewish mysticism or Kabbalah, written in Aramaic.</term> were as old as it was previously held to be (the Jews would like to attribute it to **Rabbi Shimeon, son of Jochai**, who perished in the year 120 A.D. **in Hadrian's war against the Jews** <note>The Bar Kokhba revolt (132–136 AD), though the author cites 120 AD.</note>: but **Buxtorfius** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder, a celebrated Hebraist.</note> writes in his *Abbreviations* that it first appeared more than 1000 years later. **See pages 190, 192.**), then one could cite many examples of this from it. **See Buddeus's Introduction to the History of Hebrew Philosophy, §. 29 p. 107.** But even assuming that it were even younger than the **Talmud** <term>Talmud: The central compendium of Jewish law and tradition, compiled between the 3rd and 6th centuries.</term>, as **Joseph Scaliger** writes in **Book III, Letter 243, p. 481** to the elder **Buxtorfius**, nonetheless the **Talmud** itself, the **Bereshit** <gloss>Genesis</gloss> Rab. <note>Catchword for "Bereshit Rabbah," a midrashic commentary on Genesis.</note>

### Commentaries.

The *Genesis Rabbah* <term>Genesis Rabbah: An early collection of homiletic interpretations (midrash) on the Book of Genesis, often attributed to Rabbi Oshaya.</term> of Rabbi Oshaya, the *Baal HaTurim* <term>Baal HaTurim: A commentary by Jacob ben Asher (c. 1269–1343) that often uses Gematria—the calculation of the numerical value of Hebrew letters—to find hidden meanings.</term> of Rabbi Jacob ben Asher—which Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629) was a famous Christian Hebraist whose "Rabbinic Bible" was a landmark of scholarship.</note> included in the margins of his beautiful Bible alongside the oldest rabbis—and many other similar writings belonging to this period, provide sufficient testimony that **Jewish Exegesis** <term>Exegesis: The critical explanation or interpretation of a text, especially scripture.</term> was then more **Pythagorean** <note>Here meaning mystical or focused on number-symbolism, like the secret teachings of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, rather than literal or historical analysis.</note> than **critical**; just as the learned Mr. Reimmann <note>Jakob Friedrich Reimmann (1668–1743), a polymath and historian of literature.</note>—whose fine diligence I have also made use of here—has very well noted from **Raymond Martini’s *Dagger of Faith*** <note>original: *Raymundi Martini Pugione fidei*. This was a 13th-century work intended to use Jewish texts to defend Christianity.</note>.

(As a side note <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐν παρόδῳ</note>, I only wish to mention here that the beautiful book by **Raymond Martini** cited above—a learned **Dominican** monk who lived around the year of Christ 1278—was first copied by the **Carthusian Salvatico Porcheto** (who lived in 1315) in his *Victory Against the Impious Jews* <note>original: *Victoria adversus impios Judæos*</note>. Afterwards, it was copied from him by the **Franciscan Petrus Galatinus** (who lived at the beginning of the 16th century) in his book *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth Against the Jews* <note>original: *de Arcanis catholicæ veritatis contra Judæos*</note>. It was finally brought to print by **Joseph de Voisin** in Paris, 1651, **with observations**, and most recently by **Doctor Johann Benedict Carpzov**, **with an Introduction to Jewish Theology**, in Leipzig, 1687, in large folio format.)

B. The second period, from 500 to 1100 A.D., looked fairly decent at the beginning; after-

### Jewish Exegetical Studies.

Later, however, through **grammatical, critical, and Masoretic studies** <term>Masoretic: Referring to the Masoretes, Jewish scholar-scribes between the 6th and 10th centuries who compiled a system of pronunciation and grammatical guides to preserve the Hebrew Bible.</term>, it would become more pedantic than edifying, until finally—**until the 11th century** <note>original Latin: *usque ad Seculum XI*</note>—it was of no use at all. Indeed, it would have been nearly all over for the Jews and their **exegesis** <term>Exegesis: The critical explanation or interpretation of a text, especially scripture.</term> if the **Arabs** <note>The author refers to the Golden Age of Jewish culture in Islamic Spain, where contact with Arabic science and philosophy revitalized Jewish scholarship.</note> had not awakened them to it again in the **11th Century** <note>original Latin: *Seculo XI.*</note>. From that time on, various individuals were found among them who—though mainly out of **hatred for Christians** <note>original Latin: *in odium Christianorum*</note>—took greater pains to unroll the holy parchment and explain it according to their **prejudices** <note>original: *Præjudiciis*</note>; these we must therefore place into the third period.

**C. The third period, from 1100 CE until our own times**, can best be divided again into three classes. And these are:

**α.** In the first class: the **Karaites** <term>Karaites: A Jewish movement characterized by the recognition of the Tanakh (Hebrew Bible) alone as its supreme authority in Halakha (Jewish law), rejecting the Oral Torah (the Talmud) and the Rabbinic tradition.</term>. Because there are so few of them compared to the **Rabbanites** <term>Rabbanites: The mainstream form of Judaism that follows the rabbinic lineage and the Oral Torah (Talmud), as opposed to the Karaites.</term>, and because none are to be found in **Germany**, and they are also not accustomed to writing **commentaries** <note>original Latin: *Commentarios*</note>; one can easily imagine that not much can be reported about them specifically, nor about their books and interpretations of the **Holy Scripture**. In my work ***Onesimus*** <note>Likely referring to a specific publication by the author, possibly *Onesimus, oder der an seine Pflicht erinnerte Christ*.</note>, I indicated on page 116 and following where one might find some information regarding this Jewish sect; however, here I still wish to show:

# Karaites.

a. What is written regarding their origin. There are mainly two opinions found on this matter, although Mr. Reimann has compiled a great many others.

aa. Jo. Morinus <note>Jean Morin (1591–1659), a French theologian and scholar.</note>, a Father of the Oratory in Paris who converted from the Reformed to the Roman Church, writes in his *Biblical Exercises* <note>original Latin: *Exercitationibus Biblicis*</note>, which were printed there in folio format in the years 1633 and 1686, that the Karaites were first made into a specific sect in the 8th Century <note>original Latin: *Seculo VIII.*</note> by the famous Rabbi Aaron. This opinion was also adopted by Richard Simon—as he did with almost all his other views—as is evident from his *Critical History of the Old Testament* <note>original Latin: *Historia Critica V. T.*</note> as well as from his *Selected Letters* <note>original Latin: *Epistolis Selectis*</note>.

bb. Joseph Scaliger, however, as well as Jacobus Triglandius in his learned treatise *On the Sect of the Karaites* <note>original Latin: *de Secta Karrhæorum*</note>, which was published in quarto at Delft in the year 1703, have proven with much better reasons that the aforementioned Rabbi Aaron was not the author of this sect, but only its restorer <note>original Latin: *Restaurator*</note>. They argue that the Karaites were already known before the time of Christ—though, as far as I understand it, not yet as a formal sect—who at that time, as a small group of the right-believing, opposed the decrees of the Rabbanites (who were the Pharisees). Because of this, they were called Karaites, or "Scripturalists" <term>Schriftler: A German term for those who adhere strictly to the written text (Scripture) rather than oral tradition; a literal translation of the Hebrew 'Karaites'.</term> by them; these people, however, afterwards, because they...

# On Origin and Exegesis

...[they] did not want to convert to Christ, and so fell into many errors in which they remain even to this day; from that time onward, they are to be regarded as a sect. On this matter, one may also consult Dr. Buddeus’s *Introduction to the History of Hebrew Philosophy* <note>original Latin: *Introd. ad Philos. Hebr.
