Also, see my own *The Fates of Holy Scripture* <note>original Latin: *Fata S. Scripturæ*</note>, which will perhaps be sent to the press very soon.

### b. How they deal with Holy Scripture.
It is well known concerning them that they adhere solely to the literal text <note>original German: *Buchstaben*, literally "the letters"</note> of Holy Scripture and do not accept any Talmud <term>Talmud: The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah and the Gemara, which the Karaites reject in favor of the written Torah.</term>, neither in the Mishnah <term>Mishnah: The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions.</term> nor the Gemara <term>Gemara: The section of the Talmud containing rabbinical commentary and analysis of the Mishnah.</term>. And although I, like many thousands of other theologians, cannot boast of having seen a single original writing of these people, I can nonetheless provide this much information about them from Literary History <note>original Latin: *ex Historia Literaria*</note>: that they, as already mentioned, remain uniquely and solely—though in a Jewish manner—within the literal understanding. Rabbi Ibn Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a major Spanish Jewish scholar and biblical exegete.</note> himself bears witness to this. See Hottinger’s *Heidelberg Firstlings* <note>original Latin: *Primitias Heidelbergenses*</note>, Disputation I, page 23. This is also found in the little book by the Karaite Rabbi Judah ben Eliezer, who is also called Poki, entitled *The Gate of Judah* <note>original Latin: *Porta Judæ*</note>; it was printed in 1582 in Constantinople and was lent for only a short time to John Selden <note>John Selden (1584–1654), a famous English jurist and polymath known for his studies of ancient laws.</note> by a learned youth in Cambridge, [and also to] Dr. Johann Benedict

...[Rabbi Ibn Ezra, mentioned on the previous page]. However, **Carpzov of Leipzig** <note>Johann Benedict Carpzov II (1639–1699), a noted Hebrew scholar and theologian at the University of Leipzig.</note> was presented with a manuscript by a Polish Jew from Krakow as a particular rarity. This work deals throughout with **incest and illicit intercourse** <note>original Latin: *de incestu & concubitu illicito*</note>. It is said to be explicitly stated in the preface: it is the custom among them that every individual interprets the Holy Scripture according to his own understanding, and nothing more.

This gave some people the opportunity to attempt to see whether **Hebraism** <note>The study of the "pure" or original state of the Hebrew language and its biblical roots.</note> could be restored from the writings and reports of the Karaites. To this end, the father of the current Queen of Sweden, **Ulrika Eleonora**—namely **Charles XI** <note>Carolus XI (1655–1697), King of Sweden.</note> of most glorious memory—was persuaded by the Uppsala professor **Gustav Peringer** <note>Gustav Peringer Lillieblad (1651–1710), an Orientalist and professor of Hebrew who traveled to Eastern Europe to study Karaite communities.</note> to send him to the Karaites living in Poland and Russia <note>The original German "Reussen" refers to Ruthenia, the Eastern territories of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth.</note> to search for good manuscripts. However, despite the great joy initially felt over the writings he brought back, it was ultimately discovered that the Karaites completely agreed with the **Rabbanites** <term>Rabbanites: The mainstream branch of Judaism that follows the oral law and the Talmud, which the Karaites explicitly reject.</term> in their grammar, even down to the **vowel points** <note>original Latin: *puncta*. Christian scholars hoped the Karaites might have preserved an older system of reading Hebrew, but found they used the same Masoretic vowel system as other Jews.</note>. The learned **Mr. Reimann** <note>Jacob Friedrich Reimann (1668–1743), a polymath and historian of Jewish literature.</note> has provided an outline of their theology in his *Attempt at an Introduction to the History of Jewish Theology*, which see <note>original Latin: *quem vide*</note> on page 801 and following.

### The Samaritans, <note>original Latin: *Samaritani*</note>

Here I must speak for once about:
**a.** What concerns them personally. Regarding this, toward the end of the previous **century** <note>original Latin: *Seculi*</note>, the Samaritans gave the following account of themselves in two letters written in response to the excellent **Mr. Ludolf** <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a preeminent German orientalist and the founder of Ethiopian studies.</note>. These letters were facilitated by a **Hebronite** Jew who had come to **Amsterdam** in the **year** <note>original Latin abbreviation: *Ao.*</note> 1684 to collect alms. In these writings, they gave this account of themselves: They claimed to be true Israelites, descendants of the patriarch **Joseph**, from the tribes of **Manasseh** and **Ephraim**. They stated they had their own High Priest from the descendants of **Aaron**, specifically from the grandson of **Phinehas**, descending from him in an **unbroken succession** <note>original German: *unverrückter Ordnung*</note>. They possessed and observed the **Law of Moses**, which they alone—to the exclusion of all other remaining Jewish writings <note>The Samaritans reject the Prophets and the Writings of the Hebrew Bible, accepting only the Pentateuch.</note>—regarded as divine. They claimed to be stricter than the Jews, especially regarding the **Sabbath**, the festivals, circumcision, ritual purification, and similar matters.

The name of **GOD** <term>YHWH <gloss>The four-letter name of God, known as the Tetragrammaton; the text notes the Samaritans used a different pronunciation than the Jews.</gloss></term> was also pronounced by them quite differently than by the Jews; and they performed their **divine service** according to ancient custom on **Mount Gerizim**, toward which they were also accustomed to turn during their prayers. Mr. Ludolf replied to them regarding these matters; however, it has not become known whether they wrote anything back in response.

It should also not be forgotten here that the famous **Joseph Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was a preeminent French scholar known for expanding the scope of historical chronology to include non-Greco-Roman history.</note> was the first person from Europe to write to these people. However, he did not live to see their response, which they sent in the **year** 1590, because it was delayed for a very long time in transit—though it is not known where or by whose hindrance.

Subsequently, **Morinus** <note>Jean Morin (1591–1659), a French theologian and scholar who was a pioneer in the study of the Samaritan Pentateuch.</note> received it from **Peiresc** (who is not to be found in the *Learned Lexicon*, even though his life was written by **Pierre Gassendi** and printed in **The Hague** in 1655 in **quarto** <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves; Peiresc was a famous polymath and patron of the sciences.</note>). He translated it into Latin, but this version was only printed after his death along with other works under the title *Antiquities of the Eastern Church* <note>original Latin: *Antiquitatum Ecclesiæ Orientalis*</note>, first in England and later also in **Leipzig** in the **year** 1683 in **duodecimo** <note>A small book format where each sheet is folded into twelve leaves.</note>.

Since the aforementioned small treatise *Antiquities of the Eastern Church* <note>original Latin: *Antiquitates Ecclesiæ Orientalis*</note> may not be particularly common, I will, as a favor to those for whom I write this, include here those two letters which the Samaritans wrote to Scaliger from that same work.

->### Samaritans,<-

Here I must speak for once of,
**a.** What concerns them themselves. Regarding this, toward the end of the last **century** <note>original Latin: *Seculi*</note>, the Samaritans gave this account of themselves in two letters of response to the excellent **Mr. Ludolf** <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704) was a preeminent German orientalist and linguist, often considered the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe.</note>. These letters were written to him by way of a Jew from **Hebron**, who had come to **Amsterdam** in the **year 1684** <note>original Latin: *Ao. 1684*</note> to collect alms. They claimed that they were true Israelites, descendants of the Patriarch Joseph, from the tribes of **Manasseh** and **Ephraim**. They stated they had their High Priest from the descendants of **Aaron**, specifically from the grandson of **Phinehas** <note>In biblical tradition, Phinehas was the son of Eleazar and grandson of Aaron, promised a "covenant of perpetual priesthood" for his zeal.</note>, descending from him in an unbroken line. They possessed and kept the Law of **Moses** <note>The Pentateuch, or first five books of the Bible.</note>, which they alone—excluding all other remaining writings of the Jews—regarded as divine; they claimed to keep it more strictly than the Jews, especially regarding the Sabbath, the festivals, circumcision, ritual purification, and similar matters.

The **Name of God** <note>original Hebrew: יהוה — The Tetragrammaton, the four-letter sacred name of God.</note> they also pronounced quite differently than the Jews; and they performed their **divine service**, according to ancient custom, on **Mount Gerizim**, toward which they also were accustomed to turn in their prayers. **Mr. Ludolf** replied to them again regarding these things; however, it has not become known whether they wrote anything back in return.

# Letter of the Samaritans <note>original Latin header: *Epistola Samaritanorum*</note>

In the name of the Lord GOD, great, powerful, and awesome, who shows no partiality and accepts no bribes. <note>This opening invokes the language of Deuteronomy 10:17.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the name of the strong Lord, the GOD of Israel, to whom belongs all Power: may He grant me Power that I might answer your inquiries, Joseph, son of Scaliger. <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger, the scholar to whom this letter was addressed.</note> If you ask about the observance of the Sabbath, we observe it just as the Lord said: *Let no one go out of his place on the Sabbath day; and the people rested on the seventh day.* <note>Quoting Exodus 16:29–30.</note> But in the house of God, for the sake of prayer, only praises and the mighty works <note>original: *magnalia*</note> of God are sung, and a reading of the holy law is held. For we do no work on that day; we also do not sleep with our wives, nor do we light any fire. But the Jews go out outside the city, and they light fires, and sleep with their wives on the very night of the Sabbath, nor do they wash themselves to cleanse any kinds of ritual uncleanness. <note>The Samaritans here highlight their stricter interpretation of the law compared to the Rabbinic Jews of the time.</note> But we pray the whole day, evening and morning, as it is said: *One lamb you shall sacrifice in the morning, and the other lamb you shall sacrifice between the evenings;* <note>Quoting Exodus 29:39 or Numbers 28:4.</note> and we prostrate ourselves on the ground and worship the Lord.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You ask about the observance of the Festivals; there are seven days of holy convocations: the head of these is the Festival of Passover. <note>original: *Festum Paschæ*</note> We sacrifice the offering for it in the evening when the sun sets, as the Lord said: *You shall eat it roasted with fire, with unleavened bread* <note>original: *azymis*</note> *and bitter herbs.* <note>Quoting Exodus 12:8.</note>

### To Joseph Scaliger, the first [letter] <note>original Latin: *ad Joseph. Scaliger. prima.*</note>

Furthermore, we do not perform that sacrifice <note>Referring to the Passover lamb.</note> except in Shechem, in the region of Mount Gerizim, in the first month, on the fourteenth day between the evenings. How could we do this, however, unless we knew whether it coincided with the first day of Nisan (*) of the Romans?

(*) One should read "Greeks" instead; see the observations on this matter in the *Life of Morin*. <note>Jean Morin (1591–1659) was the scholar who eventually published these letters; the note reflects later scholarly commentary on the Samaritan calendar.</note>

This feast is the Feast of the Harvest <note>This refers to the festival known as Shavuot or Pentecost. The Samaritans count the fifty days from the Sunday (the day after the Sabbath) occurring during Passover week, a point of sectarian dispute with Rabbinic Jews.</note>. But the Jews count from the day after the solemnity of the Passover. During the solemnity of the Harvest festival, we also keep holiday for seven days, just as in the Feast of Unleavened Bread.

We pray and sing songs throughout the entire night and day, from evening until evening, and we fast—both we and our children, adults and little ones, except for nursing infants. # Letter of the Samaritans
<note>original Latin: *Epistola Samaritanorum*</note>

->In the name of the Lord GOD, great and powerful and terrible, who does not show favoritism nor accept a bribe.<- <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 10:17.</note>

In the name of the strong Lord, the GOD of Israel, whose is the Power, may he grant me Power to answer your requests, Joseph, son of Scaliger. <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was a foundational figure in modern chronology and oriental studies who reached out to the Samaritans in Nablus to compare their ancient traditions with Rabbinic Judaism.</note> If you ask about the observation of the **Sabbath**, we observe it just as the Lord said: *No one shall go out from his place on the Sabbath day*; and the people rested on the seventh day. <note>Quoting Exodus 16:29–30.</note> But in the house of GOD, for the sake of prayer, only praises and the great works of GOD are sung, and a reading of the holy law is held.

For we do not perform any work on it; we also do not sleep with women, nor do we light a fire. <note>The Samaritans maintain a very strict interpretation of "kindling a fire," meaning they do not allow any fire to burn in their homes during the Sabbath, even if lit beforehand—a major point of difference from Jewish practice.</note> But the Jews go out outside the city, and they light a fire, and they sleep with women on the Sabbath night itself, nor do they wash themselves because of any kinds of pollution. <note>The Samaritans consider the Jews' more lenient interpretations to be a violation of the sanctity of the day.</note> But we pray the whole day, evening and morning, as it is said: *One lamb you shall sacrifice in the morning, and the other lamb you shall sacrifice between the evenings*, <note>Quoting Numbers 28:4.</note> and we cast ourselves down on the earth and worship the Lord.

You ask about the observation of the **Festivals**; there are seven days of holy convocations: their head is the **Feast of Passover**. <note>original Latin: *Pascha*</note> We sacrifice the offering on it in the evening, when the sun sets, just as the Lord said: *You shall eat it roasted with fire with unleavened bread and bitter herbs.* <note>Quoting Exodus 12:8. The Samaritans are one of the few groups that continue the literal practice of the Passover animal sacrifice to this day.</note>

# To Joseph Scaliger, the first letter.
<note>original Latin: *ad Joseph. Scaliger. prima.*</note>

Furthermore, we do not perform that sacrifice <note>Referring to the Passover lamb.</note> except in Shechem <note>Modern-day Nablus.</note> over against Mount Gerizim, in the first month, on the fourteenth day at twilight. How could we perform it, however, unless we knew whether it coincided with the first day of Nisan of the Romans? <note>original Latin: *an attigerit diem unum Nisan Romani*. The text asks how they could coordinate their lunar calendar with the solar calendars used by European scholars.</note>

<margin>It should be replaced with "Greeks"; read what was observed regarding that matter in the *Life of Morin*. <note>original Latin: *Reponendum Græci; lege quid ea de re animadversum in Vita Morini*. This is a scholarly gloss referring to Jean Morin (1591–1659), a French priest who published the Samaritan Pentateuch and wrote about these letters.</note></margin>

The Feast of Unleavened Bread <term>Azymorum festum; the Samaritans follow the biblical command to eat matzah for seven days</term> truly does not extend beyond seven days, and then we celebrate the festival at Mount Gerizim. We ascend there in the morning with a reading [of the Law], and from that time Israel occupies itself with the Law, and afterward we pray. Once the prayers are finished, the High Priest blesses the head of Israel with a great blessing. Then we count fifty days from the day after the Sabbath—which is one of the seven days of unleavened bread—until the day after the seventh Sabbath. This festival is the Feast of Harvest <term>Shavuot or Pentecost</term>. However, the Jews count from the day after the solemnity of the Passover. <note>This identifies a major theological rift: Samaritans begin the 50-day countdown to Pentecost from the Sunday occurring during Passover week, whereas Rabbinic Jews begin counting from the second day of Passover regardless of the day of the week.</note> In the solemnity of the Feast of Harvest, we also observe a holiday for seven days, just as in the Feast of Unleavened Bread.

We observe, moreover, the seventh month; its tenth day is called the Day of Atonement <term>Yom Kippur</term> in the Law. We pray and sing hymns the entire night and day, from evening until evening, and we fast—both ourselves and our children, adults and little ones, except for nursing infants. But the children of the Jews do not fast, who

### Letter of the Samaritans
<note>original Latin: *Epistola Samaritanorum*</note>

...they have not reached the seventh year. On the fifteenth day of that month is the solemnity of the **Feast of Tabernacles** <term>Sukkot; a biblical pilgrimage festival where booths are built to remember the Israelites' journey in the wilderness</term>, which is celebrated on Mount Gerizim <note>The holy site of the Samaritans near Shechem/Nablus</note>, as before.

We make Tabernacles, just as the Lord said: *and you shall take for yourselves on the first day the fruit of a precious tree, branches of palms, and twigs of a thick tree, and willows of the brook,* <note>original: Leviticus 23:40</note> and the eighth day of this feast is a festival [in itself], because that feast is the last of all [the pilgrimage festivals]. How then do we celebrate Passover in Shechem, except by observing and keeping, in every city in which we live, the purifications and those things which are clean? We separate ourselves from a woman suffering from her monthly period on the third day and the seventh, and a woman remains in her state of impurity for seven days; but she who has given birth to a female child remains [secluded] for eighty days on account of the female. <note>These rules for ritual isolation follow a strict, literal interpretation of the Book of Leviticus.</note>

Moreover, we circumcise the foreskin of a male on the eighth day, and we do not delay even for one day, just as the Lord said: *He whose flesh of the foreskin has not been circumcised on the eighth day, that soul shall be cut off from his people.* <note>original: Genesis 17:14</note> The Jews, however, postpone it by one day or more <note>The Samaritans often criticized the Jews for allowing the Sabbath or other holidays to delay a circumcision, whereas Samaritans performed it on the eighth day regardless.</note>. We also wash ourselves because of nocturnal emissions <note>original Latin: *nocturna ludibria*</note> and sexual intercourse, and we touch no vessels among those that can be defiled until we have washed ourselves with water. We also remove the fat from the sacrifices, and we give to the priest the shoulder, the jaws, and the stomach; nor indeed—

...[we do not] marry <note>The Latin text reads "iam" (now), but given the context of Samaritan law which strictly forbids niece-marriage, this is likely a transcription error for "non" (not).</note> the daughter of a brother; but the Jews marry the daughters of their brothers and sisters as they please.

We do nothing except according to the commands of the Lord in the holy law [given] by the hand of Moses—upon whom be peace forever—but the Jews desert what the Lord says in the law and instead do exactly as their wise men and elders tell them. <note>This highlights the fundamental split between the Samaritans, who follow a literal interpretation of the Torah, and the Jews, who follow the "Oral Law" or Rabbinic tradition.</note>

There is, however, among us a High Priest from the seed of Phinehas, son of Eleazar, son of Aaron, son of Amram, son of Kohath, son of Levi, son of Jacob Israel, son of Isaac, son of Abraham the righteous—upon whom be peace forever. <note>The Samaritans maintain a meticulously documented genealogy to prove their priests are direct descendants of the biblical Aaron.</note> The name of this Priest is Lord Eleazar, who has a son, just like him, a High Priest performing the duties of the priesthood in the presence of his father; his name is Lord Phinehas. They live perpetually in the holy place and do not depart from it, but stand before the Lord and serve the Great Name and the Holy Inscription. <term>Holy Inscription; likely referring to the *Tzitz*, the golden plate worn on the High Priest's forehead inscribed with "Holy to the Lord."</term> All matters and business are brought to them from all the cities; they judge among all the people with justice, they do not show favoritism, nor do they accept bribes; and according to their decree [matters] are settled...

The Jews, however, do not have priests from the line of Phinehas. (*In a confused manner.*) They call us Cutheans <term>Cutheans: A derogatory term used by Rabbinic Jews for Samaritans, claiming they were descendants of settlers from Cuthah (in modern-day Iraq) rather than true Israelites.</term>, but they lie in their words. The Jews hate us and curse us, but the Lord said in the holy law: *Blessed shall they be who bless you, and cursed shall they be who curse you.* <note>A reference to the blessings of Abraham and Israel found in Genesis 12:3 and Numbers 24:9.</note>

...regarding your words, which you spoke concerning the King who led us away from Samaria <note>Likely a reference to the Assyrian exile or a historical misunderstanding in Scaliger's previous letter</note>: if you are of those people [who seek us] and wish to test us, behold, from whom are we descended if not from the tribe of Joseph the Just <note>original: "Josephus Justus"; a traditional epithet for the patriarch Joseph</note>, out of Ephraim?

In turn, we ask of you by the Lord, and adjure you by His holy name, that you do not send back our request empty. Explain to us what is truly your law and the language which you speak; what is the name of the city in which you live, who is the king, and what is his law. Tell us if there are among you priests from the line of Levi, or Aaron, or Phinehas <note>Phinehas was the grandson of Aaron and a key figure in the Samaritan line of High Priests</note>, or indeed, whether there are priests among you at all.

I adjure you by the Lord and by Moses, and by the Law <note>The Torah</note>, that you do not explain or relate to us anything except that which is true, so that we may hear who you are, and your Sabbath rites, and their explanation, and the traditions that follow them. Let it be reported to us who among your people rules, and how we may know whether that word is true or not; for you now know that the scripture is Hebrew, of the holy language.

You sent to us a letter which is truthful, as far as we can tell. Indeed, your letter, which you had sent to Egypt, reached us at Gaza and Sichem <note>Modern-day Nablus, the center of Samaritan life</note>, and from it many ancient matters were known. ### Letter II.

A Letter from the Samaritans dwelling in Egypt to the same Joseph Scaliger, regarding the same subject, and by the same interpreter, Jean Morin <note>Jean Morin was a 17th-century French Oratorian priest and biblical scholar who played a key role in bringing Samaritan texts to the attention of European academics</note>.

->*In the Name of the Lord, great and mighty and terrible.*<-

May the peace of the Lord be upon Moses, son of Amram, the Prophet of all ages, exalted in mercy, prayers, fasting, and abstinence. May the Lord of life sustain and help you, and hold fast to you as He held fast to your fathers; may He hate those who hate you, and may our God be exalted over all that which is exalted over us and over you, because of the labor of our righteous fathers and your fathers, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, upon whom be peace.

<header>To Joseph Scaliger, Second [Letter]</header>
<sig>E 4</sig>

This is the Letter of the Entire Congregation of Israel that is in Egypt—worshipping toward Mount Gerizim <note>The sacred mountain of the Samaritans, located near Nablus, which they believe to be the chosen place of God rather than Jerusalem</note>, the house of the Lord—to our Lord, a man of the finest reputation, the pillar of the congregation and prince of princes among those who keep the law, who loves our law, **Joseph Scaliger, a French Christian**; may the Lord preserve him and adorn him with those things that are remembered for good. *Amen.*

Your letter was brought to us, which contained various questions. We send to you what occurs to us regarding them, based on the law and custom and the Rabbi of our Synagogue—the Synagogue of Moses, son of Amram, which believes in the Lord and in Moses the Prophet (the son of Amram), and in the Holy Law, and in Mount Gerizim, the house of God.

Our Law consists of five books <note>The Samaritan Pentateuch, which contains the first five books of the Bible with specific variations from the Jewish Masoretic text</note>. Moreover, it happens that the Passover is celebrated every year on Mount Gerizim by the hands of the High Priest—Eleazar, son of Phinehas, of the lineage of Phinehas, son of Aaron the Priest—on the fourteenth day of the first month. Afterward, we celebrate the Feast of Weeks <note>Known as Shavuot in Hebrew or Pentecost</note>, which is the conclusion and the seal of the count of fifty days; and the Sabbath of the Amalekites <note>original Latin: "Sabbathum Amalekitarum"; this likely refers to a specific Sabbath in the Samaritan liturgy where the victory over the Amalekites is commemorated</note>, and not of the congregation, and the days of reading. We also celebrate the Feast of Tabernacles <note>Sukkot</note> in the seventh month, on the fifteenth day, and again on the eighth day; but the Day of Expiation <note>Yom Kippur</note> occurs on the tenth day—

## Letter of the Samaritans

...of the same month; then for ten days there occurs the sealing of sins <note>Referring to the period of repentance leading to the Day of Atonement, or Yom Kippur</note>, and the binding of the priests <note>original Latin: "ligatio sacerdotum"; likely a reference to the specific consecration or liturgical duties of the priests during this holy season</note> on the seventh day, just as the Lord said. And on that day we perform no work, as the Lord said: *Keep the Sabbath day.* On that day we do not kindle a fire, as the Lord said: *You shall not kindle a fire in all your dwellings on the Sabbath day.*

We also perform the covenant of circumcision on the eighth day, as the Lord said: *when a woman has conceived, then on the eighth day you shall circumcise the flesh of his foreskin.* Our Synagogue and our Judgments <note>legal rulings or religious traditions</note> are similar to the Synagogue and the customs of the Jews; however, the scripture of the Jews is the scripture of Ezra <note>Ezra the Scribe; the Samaritans believed Ezra corrupted the original Hebrew text and changed the script from the ancient Paleo-Hebrew to the square Aramaic script</note>, which is cursed forever. 

Furthermore, we have sent your letters to the High Priest Eleazar—may the Lord hear his prayers—to the City of Sichem <note>Modern-day Nablus, the spiritual center of the Samaritans</note>, so that he may give us his judgment concerning these matters: what your congregation is, what your faith is, and what your city is. Send us your letters every year, or let one of you come to us and celebrate the Solemnity, and set aside a gift at Mount Gerizim, at the City of Sichem in the Province of Samaria. 

Moreover, we have in our hands the Book of Abisha <note>The Abisha Scroll; a famous Torah scroll which the Samaritans claim was written by Abishua, the great-grandson of Aaron, thirteen years after the entry into Canaan</note>, the son of Phinehas, which he wrote in the "favored days" <note>original: "diebus benevolis"; this refers to the 'Rahuta,' a period of divine favor in Samaritan theology before the era of divine displeasure</note> in the thirteenth year.

The Lord bless you and keep you, and make His face shine upon you, and have mer—

...which are held as indubitable by all, through their mixture, to use their interpretation. <note>original Latin: "quæ ut indubitatæ ab omnibus habentur, commixtione, earum interpretatione uti." And because a certain <term>polyhistor<gloss>a person of great and varied learning; an encyclopedic scholar</gloss></term> wonders why Castellus <note>Edmund Castell (1606–1685), an English Hebraist who contributed significantly to the London Polyglot Bible.</note> did not bring to light the remarkable things promised from his *Samaritan Commentary* and other such manuscripts; it should be known that this Commentary—along with 300 copies of his *Lexicon* (over which he had studied himself nearly blind) and many other borrowed manuscripts—was destroyed by fire in London, after which he did not live much longer. <note>This refers to the Great Fire of London in 1666, which devastated the publishing industry and destroyed much of Castell's life's work, leaving him in financial ruin.</note>

At the very least, one can see from the cited extract that the Samaritans are very closely related to the <term>Karaites<gloss>a Jewish movement that recognizes only the written Tanakh as binding, rejecting the Oral Law or Talmud</gloss></term> and the <term>Rabbanites<gloss>the mainstream Jewish tradition that follows the Rabbinic Oral Law and the Talmud</gloss></term> in their <term>exegesis<gloss>the critical explanation or interpretation of a text, especially scripture</gloss></term>. And although they differ from one another <term>ratione librorum & methodi<gloss>in respect to their books and methods</gloss></term>, they all eventually come together in their ultimate aim.

c. In the third class: the Rabbanites.
Here is found a very large number of commentators; I will, however, only list the most eminent and best known of them, whose writings are most frequently used, organized according to the centuries in which they lived.

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

<meta>The page begins mid-sentence, continuing the biographical description of Rashi.</meta>

[A] Frenchman, and born in the year of our Lord 1030 in Troyes in Champagne; he died, however, in the year 1105, at the age of 75. <note>Modern scholarship generally places Rashi's birth in 1040, but the author here cites 1030.</note> Among all the Rabbis, he was the first to have written commentaries on the entire Old Testament; and these are held in such high regard by the Jews that they call the author a "prince among interpreters" for this reason. He is also called par excellence <note>original Greek: "κατ᾽ ἐξοχὴν"</note> **Parshandatha** <note>Hebrew: "פרשנדתא"; a name from the Book of Esther traditionally interpreted as a title for Rashi</note>, which means **Interpreter of the Law** <note>Hebrew: "מפרש הדת"; Latin: "Interpres legis"</note>. He wrote, however, in such a concise and obscure style that several other Rabbis after him—among whom Samuel Zarza is the most prominent—wrote **Explanations** <note>Hebrew: "ביאורים"; Latin: "Elucidationes"</note> regarding Rashi. The learned Court Counselor of the High Princely House of Gotha, Mr. Breithaupt <note>Johann Friedrich Breithaupt (1639–1713), a Christian Hebraist who made Rashi's work accessible to Latin-reading scholars</note>, translated his commentaries into Latin and published them with scholarly notes in 1713, in <term>quarto<gloss>a book size created by folding a printed sheet twice to form four leaves</gloss></term>, at Gotha. He also wrote on the Talmud and died during the work on the final tractate **Makkot** <note>Hebrew: "מכות"; literally 'stripes,' the section of the Talmud dealing with corporal punishment and legal penalties</note>, which Rabbi Judah, son of Rabbi Nathan <note>Rabbi Judah ben Nathan (the Rivan), Rashi's son-in-law</note>, then completed. Because he held too zealously to ancestral traditions <note>original: "Patroparadoris," likely a corruption of the Greek "patroparadosis," meaning traditions handed down from the fathers</note>, according to the judgment of Richard Simon <note>Richard Simon (1638–1712), a prominent French priest and influential biblical critic</note>, he serves the Jews more than the Christians.

His son <note>historically, Rashi had three daughters but no sons; the "son" mentioned here likely refers to his son-in-law or a student</note> Rabbi Judah transcribed a very accurate <term>codex<gloss>an ancient manuscript in book form</gloss></term> for Rabbi Ibn Ezra ben Meir and his children, which Emperor Maximilian presented to Capnion <note>The Greek-style pseudonym for Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a famous German humanist and pioneer of Hebrew studies among Christians</note> or Reuch-

# Rabbis of the 12th Century

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, likely referring to the scholar Johannes Reuchlin, who was closely associated with Pforzheim.</meta>

...honored [Reuchlin], but he, upon his death, bequeathed it to the **Senate and People of Pforzheim** <note>original Latin: "S. P. Q. Phorcensi," an abbreviation of *Senatus Populusque Phorcensis*, modeled on the Roman SPQR</note>, as the younger Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664), a member of a famous family of Christian Hebraists</note> notes in his *Miscellanies* <note>original Latin: "Catalectis"</note> on page 152; it is now preserved as a precious treasure in the **Margravial Library of Baden**.

Although Jarchius <note>A name commonly used by Christian scholars for the Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki, also known as Rashi</note> was a Frenchman, he also understood the German language, as can be seen from the German words <note>Rashi often used vernacular "loazim" (foreign terms) to explain difficult Hebrew words; while these were usually Old French, scholars of this era often identified them as German or related dialects</note> he allowed to flow into his commentaries. And Schickard <note>Wilhelm Schickard (1592–1635), a polymath who wrote one of the first Hebrew grammars for German students</note> writes in his *Examination of Commentaries* <note>original Hebrew/Latin: "Bechinat Happeruschim"</note>: *Rabbi Solomon Jarchi was French by nationality, and from the city of Troyes, yet he spent a certain part of his life in Germany, staying for some time at Worms on the Rhine, in whose synagogue the Jews even now point out traces of his presence.* <note>original Latin: "R. Sal. Jarchi natione Gallus erat, & patria Trecensis, contrivit tamen aliquam aetatis partem in Germania, commoratus aliquamdiu Wormatiae ad Rhenum, cujus Synagogae Judaei etiamnum ipsius praesentiae monstrant vestigia."</note>

2. **Rabbi Ibn Ezra**, known by the abbreviation **RaBA** <note>Hebrew: ראב"ע; an acronym for Rabbi Abraham Ben Ezra</note> according to his proper name, **Rabbi Abraham bar Rabbi Meir ben Ezra**, was born in Toledo in Spain. He lived—according to Buxtorf—around the year 1145, though according to Schickard, around the year 1157; both may be correct. He was a good **Philosopher**, **Physician**, **Poet**, **Grammarian**, and **Astronomer**, and spent most of his life traveling. After he had traveled through England, France, Italy, Greece, etc.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Rashi to the life and scholarship of Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), another towering figure of medieval Jewish thought. The previous page concluded with Rashi's influence in Germany; this page begins with the end of Ibn Ezra's travels.</meta>

# Various Methods of the Rabbis

...[having] traveled, he finally died on the island of Rhodes in the 75th year of his age.

Like Jarchi <note>A common name for Rashi (Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki)</note>, and with a similarly obscure style—which Richard Simon <note>Richard Simon (1638–1712) was a French priest and a pioneer of modern biblical criticism</note> compares to that of Sallust <note>original Latin: "Salustii"; Sallust was a Roman historian known for his concise, abrupt, and sometimes difficult Latin style</note>—he wrote commentaries on the entire Old Testament, upon which other Rabbis have also prepared clarifications <note>These are "supercommentaries," or explanations written to explain the primary commentary</note>. Otherwise, one must grant him the fame of having been one of the Jewish interpreters who withdrew from the Talmudic and Kabbalistic explanations of Scripture and endeavored to discover the simple, literal meaning <note>This refers to the "Peshat," the plain or direct sense of the biblical text, as opposed to allegorical or mystical interpretations</note>. To this end, he also identified five types of explanations, which I will set down here as Richard Simon has reviewed them.

a. **First**, he says, there are some who dwell extensively on a single word, use many digressions, and mix all sorts of things into their commentaries that they have learned in philosophy and mathematics. This can be seen from Rabbi Isaac’s <note>Likely referring to Isaac Israeli or another philosophical commentator</note> two books on the first two chapters of Genesis <note>original Latin: "Capita Geneseos"</note>, from Saadia Gaon <note>A prominent 10th-century Jewish philosopher and exegete</note>, and others who have written large, rambling books on physics, mathematics, and Kabbalah based on the suggestion of one word or another. Aben Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra</note> rejects this method because one must stick to the literal letter and treat what belongs to other arts and sciences separately.

<meta>catchword: b. Her</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a brief biography of Abraham ibn Ezra, noting his death on Rhodes and his commitment to the literal "Peshat" interpretation of the Bible. It introduces his critique of five common rabbinic exegetical methods, starting with those who include irrelevant scientific and philosophical digressions.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham ibn Ezra, Rashi, Richard Simon, Sallust, biblical commentary, literal sense, Peshat, Saadia Gaon, Rhodes, Jewish exegesis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Abraham ibn Ezra’s five methods of biblical interpretation, as categorized by the scholar Richard Simon. # [Methods] of Explaining Holy Scripture

b. Next, others are found who measure everything according to their own reason, without any other authority; he criticizes this approach in the **Karaites** <note>A Jewish movement that recognizes the Hebrew Bible as the sole authority in Halakha (Jewish law), rejecting the Oral Torah/Talmud</note> and **Sadducees**. This method seems dangerous to him because many parts of religion remain hidden from reason. Yet, he passes an unfair judgment on the Karaites and Christians for both rejecting the Talmudic statutes, especially since he himself, in his *Commentaries* <note>original Latin: "Commentariis"</note>, followed the teaching style of the Karaites more than that of the old **Rabbanites** <note>Mainstream Jews who follow the rabbinic tradition and the Talmud</note>.

c. In the third **class**, he places those who play with pure allegories and seek to discover and reveal secrets everywhere. He likewise considers this a dangerous matter, although he does not deny that Holy Scripture contains some teachings that possess a mystical meaning; such as when it speaks, **for example** <note>original Latin abbreviation: "e. g."</note>, of the circumcision of the heart.

> However, this hidden meaning is
> then precisely the actual **literal sense** <note>original Latin: "Sensus literalis"</note>:
> which I have dealt with extensively
> under the title **Literal Sense**.

d. The fourth rank is held by the **Kabbalists**, who twist Holy Scripture with their foolish whims and ridiculous secrets—which they seek in letters, numbers, and other such things; Ibn Ezra likewise rejects them, though not entirely...

<meta>catchword: throws [German: wirfft]</meta>

It highlights his surprising affinity for Karaite methodology despite his formal criticism of them.</summary>

# Various Rabbinic Methods of Explaining Holy Scripture 79

throws [them out], although in his heart he seems to be convinced of their vanity. For he makes a distinction between the old and the new Kabbalah <note>A tradition of mystical interpretation of the Hebrew Bible</note>, of which he approves of the former, but rejects the latter as something merely patched onto it. Nevertheless, he also states once more that one cannot rely too much even on the old Kabbalah, because it often has no basis in Holy Scripture.

> And so the poor man himself does not know what he should say for fear of the Jews.

e. Finally, the fifth place is held by those who examine the actual meaning of every word exactly and, as much as possible, arrange their explanation according to the literal meaning. They do not bind themselves strictly to the **Masorah** <note>The body of tradition regarding the correct transmission and pronunciation of the Hebrew biblical text</note>, because most *variant readings* <note>original Latin: "Variantes lectiones"</note>

> have arisen not so much from the **Masorah** itself as from the scribes.

And **Abraham ibn Ezra** <note>Also known as Aben Ezra; a famous 12th-century Jewish polymath and Bible commentator</note> belongs to this category, as the one who, above all other Jews, explained Holy Scripture according to the literal sense of the words with great skill.

> Yet, he does so only as a Jew who still stands outside the veil, and who has blunted his teeth by biting on the shell <note>A common historical metaphor suggesting that Jewish commentators stayed on the "outer shell" or literal meaning of the text without reaching what Christian writers considered the "inner kernel" or spiritual truth</note>. *See his Rhythmic Work on the Hebrew Methods of Interpreting the Law by Joseph de Voisin, with his notes. Paris, 1635.* <note>original Latin: "Vid. Opus ejus Rhythm. de modis Hebr. Interpret. legem. per Jos. de Voysin, cum ejus nott. Paris. 1635."</note>

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

Mr. Dr. Abicht <note>Johann Georg Abicht (1672–1740), a German theologian and Orientalist</note> has incorporated [these works] into his *Selected Rabbinic-Philological Works* <note>original Latin: "Selectis Rabbinico Philol."</note>, which he published in Leipzig in the year 1705.

3. **Rabbi Moses**, son of Maimon, abbreviated as **Rambam** <note>An acronym for Rabbi Mosheh ben Maimon</note>. This learned Jew was born in the year 1135 in Córdoba, Spain, but was raised and educated in Egypt. Because of this, he was also later called **Moses the Egyptian** <note>original Latin: "Mosen Ægyptium"</note>. At only 23 years of age, he began writing his *Commentary on the Mishnah* <note>original: "Commentarium in Mischnajoth"; the Mishnah is the primary collection of Jewish oral law</note>, and he completed it in his 30th year. In his 42nd year, he brought the great work **Jad Hachazakah** <note>The "Strong Hand," also known as the Mishneh Torah, his monumental code of Jewish law</note> to its conclusion. After this, he prepared the book *On the Foundations of the Law* <note>original Latin: "de fundamentis legis"</note>, and in his 50th year, he wrote the book **Moreh Nevukhim** <note>The "Guide for the Perplexed," his famous work of religious philosophy</note> in the Arabic language.

He understood the Hebrew, Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note>, Arabic, Turkish, Median, and Greek languages; he was also very well-versed in medicine and philosophy, and especially in mathematics <note>original Latin: "Mathesi"</note>. And although he initially appeared very suspicious to his fellow believers because of his *Guide for the Perplexed*; they nevertheless held him in such high esteem afterward—particularly because of his *Catechism* <note>likely referring to his Thirteen Principles of Faith</note>, which they immediately and universally accepted with joy—that they even used this proverb about him:

> **From Moses unto Moses, there has none arisen like Moses.**
> <note>original Hebrew: "ממשה עד משה לא היה כמשה." original Latin: "A Mose usque ad Mosen non fuit sicut Moses." This proverb compares Maimonides to the biblical Prophet Moses.</note>

<meta>catchword: With</meta>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Moses Maimonides (Rambam), picking up from the list of his linguistic and scientific proficiencies. The previous page detailed his early life in Spain and Egypt and his major works including the Commentary on the Mishnah and the Mishneh Torah.</meta>

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

<center>F</center>

With these words I wish to mention, in passing <note>original Greek: "ὡς ἐν παρόδῳ"</note>, that the famous Rabbi Elias Levita <note>a renowned Hebrew grammarian of the Renaissance</note> bestowed this eulogy, with the necessary changes <note>original Latin: "mutatis mutandis"</note>, upon the famous Paul Fagius <note>a 16th-century German scholar of Hebrew</note> with these words: "And just as we say of our Master Moses ben Maimon: from Moses unto Moses, there has not arisen one like Moses; so also let them say of him, from Paul unto Paul, there has not arisen one like Paul" <note>This is a play on the famous Jewish proverb comparing Maimonides to the biblical Moses, suggesting that Fagius was as peerless among Christians as Maimonides was among Jews</note>.

Finally, Rabbi Moses died in the year of Christ 1205, at the age of 70, in the Promised Land, and has bequeathed the following books to posterity, written in a style almost like that of Curtius <note>referring to the elegant and clear Latin style of the Roman historian Quintus Curtius Rufus</note>, in addition to those already mentioned.

1. ***The Guide for the Perplexed*** <note>Hebrew: *Moreh Nevukhim*</note>, illustrated with notes by Johannes Buxtorf the Younger <note>a prominent Christian Hebraist</note>, published in Basel in quarto. Rabbi Samuel ibn Tibbon translated it from Arabic into Hebrew.

The learned Witsius <note>Herman Witsius (1636–1708), a Dutch theologian</note> passed the following judgment on this book: "Maimonides composed a book in which he boasts of being the 'Teacher of the Perplexed,' for he gave the book this sufficiently proud title. And he primarily concerns himself with investigating the reasons for the Mosaic laws. Nor can it be denied that things occasionally occur within it which are by no means unpleasant to read. Where, however, it comes to the point that the secrets of those external ceremonies..." <note>The text cuts off here, as the sentence continues on the following page</note>

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

It cannot be expressed how this doctor [Maimonides] remains stuck in such perplexity, and into what futile trifles his entire commentary descends when the meanings of "dog" <note>original Latin: "canis"</note> are to be explained. See *Sacred Miscellany* <note>original Latin: "Miscellan. S."</note> Volume II, page 881. It should therefore be understood with a grain of salt <note>original Latin: "cum grano salis"; a phrase meaning to view something with skepticism</note> when Spencer <note>John Spencer (1630–1693), an English theologian and Hebraist</note> calls this book a **"golden book"** <note>original Latin: "librum aureum"</note>.

2. ***Laws of the Foundations of the Law*** <note>original Latin: "Constitutiones de fundamentis legis"</note>, translated into Latin by Willem Vorst: with added notes and Isaac Abarbanel's <note>a Portuguese Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> writing on the article of faith. Amsterdam, 1638, in quarto.
3. ***On the Fasts of the Hebrews*** <note>original Latin: "De Hebræorum Jejuniis"</note>, with the interpretation of Johann Benedict Carpzov. Leipzig, 1662, in quarto.
4. ***On the Rights of the Poor and the Stranger among the Jews*** <note>original Latin: "De Jure pauperis & peregrini apud Judæos"</note>, translated into Latin and illustrated with notes by Humphrey Prideaux <note>an English clergyman and Orientalist</note>. Oxford, 1679, in quarto.
5. ***On the Sanhedrin and Punishments*** <note>original Latin: "De Synedriis & poenis"</note> translated into Latin and illustrated by Henricus Houting. Amsterdam, 1695, in quarto.
6. ***On the Study of the Law*** <note>original Latin: "De Studio legis"</note> by Johann Ulman. Strasbourg <note>original Latin: "Argent." for Argentoratum</note>, 1708, in octavo, etc.

Witsius writes in *The Christianizing Jew* <note>original Latin: "Judæo Chriſtianizante"</note> page 54, as well as in *Sacred Miscellany* Volume II, page 881: "Maimonides, who by a certain fate and the chance of his birth was the first and only one among those people who understood what it meant not to be foolish." The most eloquent Cunaeus <note>Petrus Cunaeus (1586–1638), author of a famous work on the Hebrew state</note> adorned him with this worthy praise in *On the Hebrew Republic* <note>original Latin: "de Republica Hebræorum"</note> Book I, Chapter II; and Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the Renaissance</note> says: "Maimonides alone was the first among the Jews to cease being delirious."

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

->F 2<-

4. **Rabbi David Kimchi**. 1190. abbreviated as **רד״ק Radak**, was also a Spaniard, the son of **Rabbi Joseph Kimchi**, and the brother of **Rabbi Moses Kimchi**. He earned a great name for himself through his learning, which he demonstrated particularly in his Hebrew *Grammar* and *Book of Roots* <note>original Latin: "libro radicum"; this was a major Hebrew dictionary that organized the language by its three-letter roots</note>, as well as in the *Commentaries* he prepared for the entire **Old Testament**. Among the Jews, he was the first to truly understand the *foundations of grammar* <note>original Latin: "fundamenta Grammaticalia"</note>; for this reason, the Jews also call him with the definite article (ה) **The Radak** <note>original Hebrew: "Ha-Radak"; the addition of the Hebrew letter 'He' functions like the word 'The', signaling his status as the preeminent authority</note>. **Hottinger** <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian</note>, in his *Philological Treasury* <note>original Latin: "Thes. Philol."</note> page 200, calls him the **"Prince of Grammarians"** <note>original Latin: "Grammaticorum Principem"</note>.

In this regard, however, one must distinguish him clearly from his brother, because the brother also wrote a *Grammar* as well as a *Journey to Knowledge* <note>original Greek: "hodoiporian"; used here to mean a guidebook or introduction to the science of language</note>. The latter was published with the *exposition* <note>meaning a detailed explanation or commentary</note> of **Master Elijah** <note>Elijah Levita (1469–1549), perhaps the most influential Hebrew grammarian of the Renaissance</note> by **L'Empereur** <note>Constantijn L'Empereur (1591–1648), a prominent Dutch scholar of Hebrew</note> in the year 1631 in octavo at **Leiden**.

Although he departs significantly from Talmudic and Kabbalistic dreams <note>the author uses "dreams" dismissively to refer to mystical or non-literal interpretations he considers unfounded</note>, he has, in contrast, allowed far too many grammatical trivialities to flow into his *Commentaries*, in which he frequently cites his father's work.

His *Commentaries on the Psalms of David* were translated into Latin by **Ambroise Janvier** <note>a French Benedictine scholar and editor</note> and published in quarto in the year 1666 in **Paris**.

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

...how the meanings of the grey hair <note>original Latin: "cani significatus"; likely referring to Maimonides' allegorical interpretations of biblical descriptions of age or the "Ancient of Days"</note> are to be explained, it cannot be said how entangled this doctor remains in confusion here, and into what futile trifles his entire commentary dissolves. See *Sacred Miscellanies* <note>original Latin: "Miscellan. S." refers to the "Miscellanea Sacra" by Hermann Witsius</note> Volume II, page 881. It is therefore to be taken with a grain of salt when Spencer <note>John Spencer (1630–1693), an English theologian who pioneered the comparative study of religion</note> calls this book a "golden book."

2. *Constitutions on the Foundations of the Law* <note>original Latin: "Constitutiones de fundamentis legis"</note>, translated into Latin by **Willem Vorst**; with added notes and **Isaac Abarbanel's** writing on the article of faith <note>original Latin: "de fidei capite"; a reference to Abarbanel's "Rosh Amanah"</note>. 5. *On Sanhedrins and Punishments* <note>original Latin: "De Synedriis & pœnis"</note> translated into Latin and illustrated by **Henricus Houting**. Amsterdam 1695, quarto.

**Witsius** writes in *The Judaizing Christian* page 54, as well as in *Sacred Miscellanies* Volume II, page 881: "**Maimonides**, who by a certain fate and luck of birth was the first and only one among those people to understand what it means not to talk nonsense." With which praise the most eloquent **Cunaeus** worthily honored him in *On the Hebrew Republic* Book I, Chapter II; and **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a towering figure of Renaissance scholarship</note> says: "**Maimonides** alone was the first among the Jews to cease raving."

### Rabbis of the 12th Century

<note>original Latin: Rabbini Seculi XII.</note>

->F 2<-

4. *Rabbi David Kimchi*. 1190. Abbreviated as רד"ק **Radak** <note>An acronym for "Rabbi David Kimchi"</note>, is also a Spaniard, the son of *Rabbi Joseph Kimchi* and the brother of *Rabbi Moses Kimchi*. He has made a great name for himself through his scholarship, which he demonstrated particularly in his *Hebrew Grammar* and *Book of Roots* <note>original Latin: "libro radicum"; a famous Hebrew dictionary and study of root words</note>, as well as in the commentaries he produced for the entire Old Testament. Among the Jews, he was the first to truly understand the grammatical foundations; for this reason, the Jews refer to him with the definite article as *Ha-Radak* <note>The Radak</note>, while Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian</note>, in his *Philological Treasure* <note>original Latin: "Thes. Philol." referring to "Thesaurus Philologicus"</note>, page 200, calls him the **Prince of Grammarians**.

In this regard, however, he must be clearly distinguished from his brother, because his brother, like him, also wrote a Grammar as well as a *Path to Knowledge* <note>original Greek/Latin: "ὁδοιπορίαν ad scientiam"</note>, the latter of which was published with the exposition of **Master Elijah** <note>Elijah Levita (1469–1549), a major Renaissance-era Hebrew grammarian</note> by **L’Empereur** <note>Constantine L’Empereur (1591–1648), a Dutch orientalist</note> in 1631 in octavo at Leiden.

Although he departs significantly from Talmudic and Kabbalistic dreams <note>referring to Kimchi's preference for literal and linguistic analysis over mystical or folkloric interpretations</note>, he has, on the other hand, allowed too many grammatical minutiae to flow into his commentaries, in which he frequently cites his father's work.

His commentaries on the **Psalms of David** <note>original Latin: "Psalmos Davidis"</note> were translated into Latin by **Ambrose Janvier** <note>Ambroise Janvier (1613–1682), a French Benedictine scholar and linguist</note> and published in 1666 in quarto at Paris.

Finally, it remains to be reported of him that

### 13th Century

<note>original Latin: Seculo XIII.</note>

**Rabbi Moshe ben Nachman**, abbreviated as רמב״ן **Ramban** <note>An acronym for "Rabbi Moses ben Nachman," also known in the West as Nachmanides</note>, born in 1212 in Girona, Catalonia. He wrote a *Midrash* <note>A method of exegesis or a commentary</note> or commentary on the Pentateuch <note>The Five Books of Moses</note>; however, he is so preoccupied with Kabbalistic <term>prejudices<gloss>In this context, the author uses "prejudices" to mean "pre-conceived mystical frameworks" that differ from the literal or rationalist interpretations preferred by some Christian scholars of the era.</gloss></term> that Christians can make little use of him. The same is true of his other writings, such as: *The Book of Hope* <note>original Latin: "Liber Spei"</note>, *Observations* <note>original Latin: "Animadversiones"</note>, *The Book of Merits* <note>original Latin: "liber meritorum"</note>, *The Disposition of Man* <note>original Latin: "dispositio hominis"</note>, etc.; for this reason, he is held in little esteem.

### 14th Century

<note>original Latin: Seculo XIV.</note>

**Rabbi Levi ben Gerson**, abbreviated as רלב״ג **Ralbag** <note>An acronym for "Rabbi Levi ben Gerson," also known as Gersonides</note>, from Bagnols in Catalonia <note>While the text says Catalonia, Bagnols is historically in Provence, France; the author may be grouping these regions together</note>, died in the year 1370. He obscured his commentaries on the entire Old Testament with philosophical subtleties and, moreover, as a secret naturalist <note>Someone who seeks natural rather than supernatural explanations for the world</note>, showed how the miracles in Holy Scripture could be explained away as a mere heap of natural occurrences. He also wrote the book *Milchamos Adonai* <note>original Hebrew: מלחמות יהוה</note>, *The Book of the Wars of the Lord* <note>original Latin: "librum bellorum Domini"</note>, which some Jews call *Milchumeth Ha-Shem* <note>original phonetic: Milchumeth Alschem</note>, which is to say, "War against the Lord" <note>This is a pun by his critics, suggesting his rationalist philosophy was actually an attack on God</note>.

<summary>This page catalogs two influential medieval Jewish scholars: Nachmanides (Ramban), noted for his mystical Kabbalistic approach, and Gersonides (Ralbag), criticized by the author for his extreme rationalism and naturalistic interpretation of biblical miracles.</summary>

# 14th and 15th Century Rabbis

It <note>Refers to Levi ben Gerson's commentary mentioned on the previous page</note> was printed in folio in the year of Christ 1560. He completed his *Commentary on the Law* <note>original Latin: *Commentarium in legem*; referring to the Pentateuch</note>, into which he incorporated *Ethics*, in the year 1330; however, he finished the *Commentaries* on the books of Samuel in the year of Christ 1338, and finally died in Perpignan in the year 1370.

### 15th Century

<note>original Latin: *Seculo XV.*</note>

1. **Rabbi Isaac Abarbanel**, or **Abrabanel**, also **Abravanel**, and **Abarbinel**, אברבנאל, was born in Lisbon in the year of Christ 1437, and as is generally believed, from a family said to descend from King David. He resided partly in Castile, partly in Naples, and in other places in Italy, and finally died in Venice at 71 years of age.

He is one of the best and most useful interpreters among those who "sit in darkness and the shadow of death" <note>This is a biblical allusion (Psalm 107:10) frequently used by Christian authors of this period to describe Jewish scholars who had not converted to Christianity</note>; he writes in a Rabbinic style as pure and elegant as Cicero wrote in Latin, although he is quite prolix and more oratorical than exegetical <note>Meaning he focuses more on eloquent speech and rhetoric than on the strict line-by-line interpretation of the text</note>. He also cites the *expositions* <note>original Latin: *Expositiones*</note> of other Rabbis and does not shy away from frequently criticizing them sharply. However, the many questions he poses in his *Commentaries* on Moses, Joshua, Judges, Samuel, and Kings produce more annoyance and distaste in the reader than an appetite to read them.

Through his *Perush* <note>original Hebrew: *Perusch* (פירוש), meaning "commentary"</note>, or commentary on the Former and Latter Prophets,

The best edition of his *Commentaries on the Pentateuch of Moses* <note>original Latin: *Commentariis in Pentateuchum Mosis*; the first five books of the Bible</note> is the one published through the *effort and labor* <note>original Latin: *studio & labore*</note> of Mr. von *Bashuysen* at Hanau *in folio* <note>A large book format where the printing sheets are folded only once</note>.

# 15th-Century Rabbis

Israel <note>Likely referring to Menasseh ben Israel (1604–1657), a famous Portuguese rabbi and publisher</note> writes: *If anyone desires to see all the controversies of the Hebrews that are moved among them concerning the explanation of the prophecies read in Daniel, let him consult Abarbanel's Treatise* **Springs of Salvation** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'ayanei HaYeshu'ah*; original Latin: *Fontes salutis*</note> (which I understand that most learned man Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf II</note> has translated into Latin). He also wrote **Sacrifices of the Passover** <note>Hebrew: *Zevach Pesach*; original Latin: *Sacrificia Paschæ*</note>, **Inheritance of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Nachalat Avot*; original Latin: *Hæreditatem Patrum*</note>, **Chariot of the Mishnah** <note>Hebrew: *Merkavat HaMishnah*; original Latin: *Currum Mischnæ*</note>, **Crown of the Learned** <note>Hebrew: *Ateret Zekenim*; original Latin: *Coronam Eruditorum*</note>, and **Assembly of the Prophets** <note>Hebrew: *Lahaqat HaNevi'im*; original Latin: *Collegium Prophetarum*</note>.

Mr. Dr. J. H. Majus <note>Johann Heinrich Majus (1653–1719), a German Hebraist and theologian</note> described his biography and had it printed in quarto <note>A book size created by folding a printed sheet twice</note> in Altdorf in the year 1708. His youngest son, it is said, converted to the Christian faith in *Ferrara* and was given the name *Alphonso*; the eldest, however, *Judas*, became a *physician* and a famous *poet*.

2. *Rabbi Isaac* <gloss>Nathan</gloss> or *Rabbi Mordecai Nathan*, <gloss>Nathan</gloss> in the year 1438, a son of *Eliezer*, a Jew from Constantinople. (He is not to be confused with *Nathan of Hordana*, who also lived around this time and wrote a *Perush* <note>A commentary</note> on *Rashi* and *Rabbi Isaac of Düren* <note>Original Latin: *R. Isaac Medura*; author of the legal work *Sha'arei Dura*</note>; nor with *Nathan ben Jehiel*, who wrote a *Talmudic and Aramaic dictionary* <note>The *Arukh*</note> and died in *1106 AD*). He published not only a *Commentary* on the *Five Books of Moses* <note>Pentateuch</note>, in which he ex-pressed <note>The German text ends mid-word with "expri-"</note> the *literal sense* <note>original Latin: *sensum literalem*</note> quite well, though as a Jew—

<summary>This page details the major works of Isaac Abarbanel, his family's history, and begins a biographical entry for Rabbi Isaac (Mordecai) Nathan, distinguishing him from other famous scholars named Nathan.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Abarbanel, Johannes Buxtorf, Johann Heinrich Majus, Mordecai Nathan, Nathan ben Jehiel, Jewish-Christian conversion, Bible commentary, Hebrew scholarship</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the entry for Rabbi Isaac Nathan, highlighting his pioneering work on Hebrew concordances, and begins the 16th-century section with Rabbi Solomon ben Melech.</meta>

printed; but he also wrote the first Hebrew Concordance of the Old Testament, through which he cast a great light upon **exegetical theology** <note>The branch of theology focused on the systematic interpretation of religious texts; original Latin: *Theologiæ Exegeticæ*</note>, by showing a new way in which the **literal sense** <note>The plain, historical meaning of the text; original Latin: *Sensum literalem*</note> could be understood much more thoroughly through **parallelism** <note>The practice of comparing similar passages of Scripture to clarify meaning; original Latin: *Parallelismo*</note>.

### 16th Century <note>original Latin: Seculo XVI.</note>

Rabbi Solomon ben, or Aben Melech, <gloss>active around</gloss> the year 1550. He compiled a **Commentary on selected passages and more difficult words and matters** <note>original Latin: *Commentarium in loca selecta vocesque & res difficiliores*</note>, briefly drawn from the commentaries of Rabbi Judah <note>Judah Hayyuj</note>, Jonah <note>Jonah ibn Janah</note>, Ibn Ezra, and Kimchi. To this work he gave the name **Michlal Yophi** <note>Hebrew: מכלל יופי</note>, which means **Perfect Beauty**, taken from **Psalm 50:2**.

Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf, a famous Christian scholar of Hebrew</note> prefers him over **Rabbi Kimchi** and believes that this **literal and historical commentary** <note>original Latin: *Commentarius literalis & Historicus*</note> could serve both Jews and Christians well. Furthermore, the Professors at Leiden—**Abraham Heidanus, Jacob Golius, Johannes Cocceius,** and **Alardus Uchtmann**—have explicitly written in their specific letters (which were included in this work when it was published in Amsterdam in 1684 as a very elegant folio <note>A large book format made by folding printed sheets only once</note> volume): "**The author has collected in it excellent notes** <note>original Greek: ὑπομνήματα (*hypomnemata*)</note> **on the more difficult and selected passages and words of Old Testament Scripture, and has clarified them using the best interpretations from diverse sources.**" <note>original Latin: *Autorem in eo egregia ὑπομνήματα in difficiliora selectaque scripturæ V. T. loca & verba congessisse, eque diversorum optimis interpretationibus illustrasse.*</note>

# Bibliotheca Rabbinica <note>Latin: Rabbinical Library</note>

These are, then, the most distinguished and important **exegetes** <note>Scholars who provide critical interpretation of scripture</note>, or commentators, among the blinded and hardened people of the Jews: to whom I will also add the following Jewish books, as a small **Jewish Library** <note>original Latin: *Bibliothecam Judaicam*</note>, arranged in alphabetical order.

->A.<-

**Abarbanel’s Commentary on the Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible; original Latin: *Abarbanelis Commentarius in Pentateuchum Mofis*</note>. Hanau, published through the study and labor of van Bashuysen.
- **Commentary on the Former Prophets** <note>The biblical books of Joshua, Judges, Samuel, and Kings; original Latin: *Commentarius in Prophet. priores*</note>. Leipzig, 1686, folio. Also, Hamburg, with the commentary of Rabbi Jacob Fidanque, 1687.
- **Commentary on the Latter Prophets** <note>The books of Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, and the twelve minor prophets; original Latin: *Commentar. in Prophetas posteriores*</note>. Amsterdam, 1641, folio.
- **Zevach Pesach** <note>Hebrew: "Passover Sacrifice"</note>, which is a **commentary on the Haggadah**, or the historical reading of the Passover festival. Venice, 1545, quarto. Also, Cremona, quarto.
- **Ma’ayanei Yeshu’ah** <note>Hebrew: "Fountains of Salvation"</note>. A **Commentary on Daniel**. Amsterdam, 1652, quarto. The title is taken from **Isaiah 12:3**: *the fountains of salvation* <note>original Latin: *fontes salutis*</note>.
- **Merkevet HaMishneh** <note>Hebrew: "The Second Chariot"</note>. A **Commentary on the Five Books of Moses**. Sabbioneta, 1551, folio. The title is taken from **Genesis 41:43**: *the second chariot* <note>original Latin: *Currus fecundanus*</note>.
- **Nachalat Avot** <note>Hebrew: "Inheritance of the Fathers"</note>, from **Proverbs 18:14**. Venice, quarto.

### Bibliotheca Rabbinica <note>Latin: Rabbinical Library</note>

**Ateret Zekenim** <note>Hebrew: "The Crown of the Aged"</note>, titled after Proverbs 17:6. Sabbioneta, in the year of Christ 1557, quarto.

- **Mashmi'a Yeshu’ah** <note>Hebrew: "The Herald of Salvation"</note>, titled after Isaiah 52:7. It deals with the prophets, specifically those who discuss the Jews' supposed future redemption. Amsterdam, 1647, and Frankfurt, quarto, including a Latin translation.
- **Miph’alot Elohim** <note>Hebrew: "The Works of God"</note>, titled after Psalm 66:5. This work treats the creation of the world, arguing against **Aristotle** and others who maintain the eternity of the world <note>The philosophical belief that the universe has no beginning, which contradicts the biblical account of creation</note>. Venice, 1592, quarto.
- **Adam Sikhli** <note>Hebrew: "The Intellectual Man"</note>. Published in Freiburg im Breisgau, 1560. Following the words of David in Psalm 29:2, it strongly urges a holy life.
- **Rosh Amanah** <note>Hebrew: "The Pinnacle of Faith"</note>, titled after Song of Songs 4:8. It concerns the principal articles of the Jewish faith. # Bibliotheca Rabbinica <note>Latin: Rabbinical Library</note>

by Rabbi Machir <note>original Hebrew: ר' מכיר</note>. This treatise is divided into three books. The first deals with the things which the Jews call *Chevlei Mashiach* <note>Hebrew: "Birth pangs of the Messiah"</note>, or "the sorrows of the Messiah" <note>original Latin: *dolores Messiæ*</note>, such as the signs, wonders, wars, and tribulations that shall occur before the coming of the Messiah. The second deals with the soul and its departure from the body, as well as its destination; likewise <note>original Latin: *it.*</note> of its condition in Hell <note>Gehenna</note>, and what Hell is. Similarly, it treats the time to come and eternal life, as well as the resurrection of the dead. The third deals with the matters they call the "Tradition of Moses from Mount Sinai" <note>original Latin: *Traditionem Mosis de monte Sinai*</note>, such as the formation of man, the number of his limbs, and various matters of Gematria <note>original Latin: *Gematricis*; a system of Jewish numerology where letters represent numbers</note> and Kabbalah <note>original Latin: *Kabbalisticis*; Jewish mystical tradition</note> derived from the Law. Venice, in the year of Christ 1597. **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf, the elder</note> possessed a manuscript <note>original Latin: *MSSt.*</note> of it.

**Akedat Yitzchak** <note>Hebrew: "The Binding of Isaac"</note>, or the "Binding of Isaac" <note>original Latin: *Ligatio Isaaci*</note>. *A Commentary on the Law and the Five Scrolls* <note>The Torah and the five biblical books of Song of Songs, Ruth, Lamentations, Ecclesiastes, and Esther</note> by Rabbi Isaac ben Arama, or Armáu. He likewise refutes **Aristotle** regarding the eternity of the world <note>The philosophical argument that the universe has no beginning, which conflicts with the biblical creation story</note>. It is a truly learned work. Venice, in the year of Christ 1573, folio.

**Alphesi** <note>The *Sefer Ha-Halakhot* of Isaac Alfasi</note> is an abridgment of the Talmud, which takes its name from its author, Rav Alfasi, and is reviewed <note>original Latin/German: *recensiret*</note> by **Buxtorf** in his *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*, page 312 and following.

# Bibliotheca Rabbinica <note>Latin: Rabbinical Library</note>

**Amanah**, or the **Book of Faith** <note>original Hebrew: *Sepher amanah*; original Latin: *Liber fidei*</note>. This is a Christian book, written by a converted Jew, in which he defends the Christian faith against the objections of the Jews. **Paulus Fagius** <note>a prominent 16th-century German Renaissance scholar and Hebraist</note> translated it into Latin. Printed in Isny <note>original Latin: *Isnæ*; a town in Germany</note> in the year of Christ 1542, in quarto format.

**Pillars of the Exile** <note>original Hebrew: *Ammúde Gólah*; original Latin: *Columnæ exilii*</note> by **Rabbi Isaac ben Joseph of Corbeil**. This work deals with the 613 commandments of the Jews <note>a central count of all religious laws in the Torah</note> and is commonly called by the Jews **SMaK** <note>an acronym for *Sefer Mitzvot Katan*</note>, that is, the **Small Book of Commandments** <note>original Latin: *liber præceptorum parvus*</note>. This is in contrast to the work written by **Rabbi Moses of Coucy** <note>original German: *Moses Kotzensis*</note>, the **Great Book of Commandments** <note>original Hebrew: *Sepher mitzvoth gádol*; original Latin: *librum præceptorum Magnum*</note>.

**Its Seven Pillars** <note>original Hebrew: *Ammudéha Schibha*</note>, or *Shifa* <note>likely a variant transcription of the Hebrew</note>, by **Rabbi Bezaleel**. This is an allegorical interpretation of various verses of the Old Testament. Printed in Dyhernfurth <note>original German: *Dihrenfort*; modern-day Brzeg Dolny, Poland</note> in the year of Christ 1693, in quarto format.

**Four Rows** <note>original Hebrew: *Arba túrim*; original Latin: *Quatuor ordines*</note>, by **Rabbi Jacob ben Asher**. This work deals with the ceremonies and laws of the Jews. Printed in Kraków, 1615, in folio format. This book is held in very high regard among the Jews, and its concordances <note>cross-references</note> are placed in the margins throughout the entire **Talmud**. The four "orders" <note>divisions</note> into which it is divided are these:
1. **The Path of Life** <note>original Hebrew: *Orach chajim*; original Latin: *Via vitæ*</note>. 
2. **Teaching Knowledge** <note>original Hebrew: *Jore Deah*; original Latin: *docens scientiam*</note>. 
3. **The Breastplate of Judgment** <note>original Hebrew: *Choshen hammischpat* (OCR reads "Chofen"); original Latin: *pectorale judicii*</note>. 
4. **The Stone of Help** <note>original Hebrew: *Ebben haëzer*; original Latin: *Lapis auxilii*</note>. 
Subsequently, several large compendia <note>summaries or manuals of law</note> were made from this book, such as the—

# Bibliotheca Rabbinica <note>Latin: Rabbinical Library</note>

**The Prepared Table** <note>original Hebrew: *Shulchan Aruch*; a fundamental code of Jewish law</note>, concerning which more will be said in its proper place, under the letter **S**.

**Aruch**, a **Talmudic-Chaldaic Lexicon** <note>a dictionary for the Aramaic and Hebrew terms used in the Talmud</note>, by **Rabbi Nathan** <note>Nathan ben Jehiel of Rome, an 11th-century lexicographer</note>. It was first printed in **Venice**, and later in **Basel**, at the expense of the Jewish community, in folio format. This version is referred to as the **Great Aruch**. However, there was also later printed:

The **Shortened Aruch** <note>original Hebrew: *Aruch Ha-katzur*; original Latin: *Aruch breve*</note>, serving as an **abridgment of the larger Aruch** <note>original Latin: *Compendium majoris Aruch*</note>, which is much better for beginners than the former. This is because the author, whose name is not listed, often provides much better explanations than those found in the Great Aruch, and frequently explains things in German. Printed in **Krakow** in the year of Christ **1592**.

**Holy Service** <note>original Hebrew: *Avodat Ha-kodesh*; original Latin: *Ministerium Sanctum*</note>, by **Rabbi Meir ben Gabbai**. Printed in **Krakow** in **1577**, in folio format. **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder, a prominent Christian Hebraist</note> reports in his *Bibliotheca Rabbinica* (page 390) that a good friend wrote to him regarding this book, claiming he had found many remarkable things concerning the mystery of the **Holy Trinity** within it.

->B.<-

**The Great Genesis** <note>original Hebrew: *Bereshit Rabbah*</note>, by **Rabbi bar Nachmoni**. *Bereshit* is the first word of **Genesis**, and here it signifies the First Book of **Moses**; *Rabbah*, however, means **Great Commentary** <note>original Latin: *Glossa magna*</note>. This book is therefore an explanation of the First Book of Moses, though it is not literal, but rather allegorical and historical, written in the **Jerusalem language** <note>Jewish Palestinian Aramaic</note>, with many inter- <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "ter" suggests the next word is "intermixed"</note>

**Exodus Rabbah** <note>original Hebrew: *Shemoth rabba*</note>, the **Great Gloss of Exodus** <note>original Latin: *Glossa Exodi magna*</note>, etc. Wilmersdorf. 1673. folio <note>a large book format made by folding sheets of paper once</note>.

**Bechayi’s Commentary on the Five Books of Moses** <note>original Latin: *Bechaji Commentarius in V. libb. Mosis*</note>, Venice. 1546. folio. There are also the **Explanations of Bechayi** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Biuri Bechaji*, or *Elucidationes Bechaji*</note>, concerning difficult matters, especially Kabbalistic <note>relating to Jewish mysticism</note> ones, which occur in the works of Bechayi <note>referring to Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century Spanish scholar</note>, that have been published.

**Bachur**, or the **Book of the Chosen** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Sepher Bachur*, *liber electus*</note>, is the well-known grammatical book by **Rabbi Elias Levita the German**, which **Münster** <note>Sebastian Münster, a 16th-century German cartographer and Hebraist</note> translated into Latin and which was printed in Basel.

**Son of Knowledge** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Ben da’at*, *filius scientiæ*</note>, by **Rabbi Axelrad**. A commentary on the Psalms. Hanau. 1616. quarto <note>a medium-sized book format</note>.

**The Well of the Exile** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Béer ha-golah*, *Puteus*, or *fons Captivitatis*</note>.
It is an explanation of those Talmudic <note>relating to the Talmud, the central text of Jewish civil and ceremonial law</note> sayings that at first seem completely absurd. To tell the truth, however, these explanations are often much more absurd and foolish than the Talmudic whims themselves. Prague. 1598. folio.

**The Well of Moses** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Beer Mosche*, *Puteus Mosis*</note>. Prague 1604.
This is a German explanation of the difficult words that occur in the books of Moses.
