<meta>This page continues the alphabetical catalog of Hebrew and Rabbinic works, following the previous page's entry for "The Well of the Exile" (Be'er ha-Golah). It focuses on entries starting with the letter B, specifically liturgical songs, mystical texts, and moral philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>Rabbinical Library <note>Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note></header>

**In Prosperity** <note>original Hebrew: *Bakosharot* (literally "with chains"); Latin: *Baccofcharoth, cum compedibus*</note>, "with chains," from Psalm 68:7. By Rabbi Ephraim, son of Rabbi Joseph. Cracow, 1608. Quarto <note>a medium-sized book format</note>. A Hebrew songbook, in which joyful psalms and songs of praise are found in the first part; in the other, however, lamentations and funeral songs are to be found.

**The Radiant** <note>original Hebrew: *Bahir*, or *Sepher habbahir*</note>, or the illustrious book, a "light-giver," and Kabbalistic <note>relating to Jewish mysticism</note>. Attributed to Rabbi Nehunia ben ha-Kanah, who is said to have lived with Jonathan the Paraphrast <note>the author of the Aramaic translation of the Prophets</note> approximately 30 or 40 years before the birth of Christ. It is thus the very oldest book that the Jews possess. Various profound sayings are attributed to the author, some of which can be found in the *Rabbinical Library* of **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder, a famous Christian Hebraist</note>, on page 320.

**Examination of the World** <note>original Hebrew/Latin: *Bechinath olam*, *Probatio*, or *Examen Mundi*</note>. This is a very famous book, both regarding the wording and the subject matter itself: it deals with the contemptible vanity of the world and the seeking of the Kingdom of God. The beautiful style and pure manner of writing are especially recommended to those who intend to practice Rabbinic literature <note>the specific Hebrew style used by medieval and early modern scholars</note>. Prague, 1598.

**Ben Sira** <note>The Alphabet of Ben Sira</note> is an old book containing very useful moral maxims; it was translated and published by **Paul Fagius** <note>a 16th-century German scholar and reformer</note>. Isny, 1542. Quarto. **Johannes Drusius** <note>a Flemish scholar of the Bible</note> has [pub]-
<meta>The text cuts off mid-word ("her-") at the end of the page, leading into "herausgegeben" (published/edited) on the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page lists significant Rabbinic texts, including the liturgical songbook Baccofcharoth, the foundational Kabbalistic work Bahir, the moral-philosophical Bechinath Olam, and the ethical maxims of Ben Sira.</summary>
<keywords>Bakosharot, Sefer ha-Bahir, Bechinat Olam, Alphabet of Ben Sira, Kabbalah, Nehunia ben ha-Kanah, Paul Fagius, Rabbinic literature, liturgy</keywords>

later revised and illustrated <note>meaning explained or annotated</note> anew, and printed with a fine supply of Hebrew **proverbs** <note>Latin: *Adagiorum*</note> in **Franeker** in **1597 A.D.** <note>Latin: *A.C. (Anno Christi)*</note>. **See by all means what Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder, a prominent Christian scholar of Hebrew</note> **relates from this book in his *Rabbinical Library*, page 324.** Finally, it was also republished in **Amsterdam** in **1697 A.D.**

**Joseph is a Fruitful Bough** <note>Hebrew: *Ben porath Joseph*; Latin: *Ramus fœcundus Joſephi*</note>, **from Genesis 49:22.** It is a disputation by **Juan Meza** <note>Latin: *Joannis Micæ*</note>, who converted from the Christian religion to the Jewish unbelief <note>reflecting the perspective of the author's era regarding conversion to Judaism</note>, and, as an apostate, had himself called **Joseph Nasi**.

**Blessings for Food** <note>Hebrew: *Birchoth hammaſon*; Latin: *Precationes Cibi*</note>. This is called the **Bensch-book** <note>from the Yiddish *benshen*, "to bless," likely derived from the Latin *benedicere*; refers to the Birkat Hamazon or Grace after Meals</note> by the Jews and contains all sorts of prayers, especially those to be used at the table. It is available in Hebrew and also in German <note>often referring to Yiddish, the vernacular of Central European Jews</note>.

**Covenant of Rest** <note>Hebrew: *Berith menúchah*; Latin: *fœdus quietis*</note> or **Covenant of Peace** <note>Latin: *pacis*</note>. **By Rabbi Abraham Merimon** <note>Abraham ben Isaac of Granada</note>. A Kabbalistic <note>Jewish mystical</note> book. **Amsterdam, 1648. Quarto** <note>a book size made by folding a sheet twice</note>.

### C.

These are **theological commonplaces** <note>Latin: *loci communes*; foundational topics of faith</note> arranged in **alphabetical order**. **Lublin.**

It reads quite pleasantly indeed, but a great deception lies behind it, because the author attempts to explain the utterly reckless, fabricated fables and foolish presentations of the Talmudic adventures—of which the Jews themselves are ashamed—in a different, flowery manner. This is done so that their idol, the Talmud, may maintain its dignity and so that Christians may be more subtly deceived.

**The Five Fifths of the Torah** <note>Hebrew: *Chamishah Chumshei Torah*; Latin: *quinque quiniones Legis*</note>, that is, the **Pentateuch of Moses**, with the **Targum** <note>an ancient Aramaic translation or paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note>, a double **Masora** <note>the traditional system of notes on the text of the Hebrew Bible regarding its correct pronunciation and spelling</note>, and a fourfold **commentary**. Published in **Wilhermsdorf** in **1713 A.D.** <note>Latin: *A.C. (Anno Christi)*</note> in royal folio format. This book is reviewed in the *Innocent News* <note>German: *unschuldigen Nachrichten*, a prominent 18th-century Lutheran theological journal</note> of the year 1714, page 87 and following.

**The Pious**, or **The Book of the Pious** <note>Hebrew: *Sepher Chasidim*; Latin: *liber piorum* or *Sanctorum*</note>. This is a theological book of morals and provides **precepts for living well** <note>Latin: *praecepta bene vivendi*</note>, which are illustrated with various examples and stories. The actual author is **Rabbi Samuel**, who flourished around the year of Christ 1180; however, it is commonly published under the name of his son, **Rabbi Judah the Pious** <note>Latin: *R. Jehudae pii*</note>. **Basel, 1581.**

**The Desirable Vessel** <note>Hebrew: *Keli Chemda*; Latin: *Vas desiderabile*</note>, titled from **Jeremiah 25:34**. By **Rabbi Samuel Laniado** <note>Latin: *R. Samuelis Laniadi*</note>. **Venice**, in folio.

**Chumash** <term>Chumash: Derived from the Hebrew word for "five," this is the common Jewish term for the Five Books of Moses</term>, or the Pentateuch <note>Latin: *Pentateuchus*</note>. ## D.

**Good Words** <note>Hebrew: *Devarim Tovim*; Latin: *Verba bona*</note>, by **Rabbi Moshe Alshich**. Venice. A commentary on Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs <note>Latin: *Canticum Canticorum*</note>.

**Words of the Days** <note>Hebrew: *Divrei HaYamim*; Latin: *Verba dierum*</note>. Words, or History, or Annals of the Kings of Israel under the Second House, or the Second Temple. **The Way of Life** <note>Hebrew: *Derech Chajim*; Latin: *Via Vitæ*</note>. This is also a very common title under which many books are published.

**Sermons** <note>Hebrew: *Derashot*; Latin: *Homiliæ*</note>. Likewise. This is a title that they commonly place over their collections of sermons.

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>Latin original: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

**The Daughter of Rabbi Solomon** <note>Latin: *Filia R. Salomonis*; likely referring to a work by Solomon Luria or a similar chronicle</note>. Krakow, 1606. This is a historical book, covering the period from Adam until the departure of the children of Israel from Egypt. It was also printed in Prague in the year 1668, in quarto <note>a book size where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>.

**The Passover Story** <note>Hebrew: *Haggadah Shel Pesach*; Latin: *Historia Paschæ*, or *Lectio festi Paschæ*</note>. Venice, 1609. This is a Hebrew-German book <note>likely written in Judeo-German or Yiddish</note>, containing everything pertaining to the preparation and celebration of the Passover festival. However, it is to be understood as reflecting current times, and thus does not belong among the works of antiquity, even though one part or another of it originates from ancient times.

**Glosses on Maimonides** <note>Hebrew: *Hagahot Maimoniyot*; Latin: *Haggehoth Majemoni*</note>, meaning Corrections or Meditations on Maimonides, by **Rabbi Meir of Rothenburg** <note>a major 13th-century German Rabbi and poet</note>.

**Logic** <note>Hebrew: *Higgayon*</note>, the Logic of **Rabbi Simeon**, translated into Latin and illustrated with vowel points by **Sebastian Münster** <note>a famous 16th-century Christian Hebraist</note>. Basel, 1527. From this book, one can see how the logic of the Jews is structured quite differently from our own; consequently, they also have a completely different method for disputation than we do.

**The Glory of the Elders** <note>Hebrew: *Hadrat Zekenim*; Latin: *Honor Senum*</note>, by **Rabbi Aza-**

And I am of the same opinion, especially since one can find this book in the excellent *Greater Key to the Talmud* <note>Latin: *Clavi Thalmudica maxima*</note> by the highly renowned Dr. van Bashuysen <note>Heinrich Jakob van Bashuysen, a prominent 18th-century Christian Hebraist</note>. The author of this work is **Rabbi Joshua Halevi**, or Levita, a Spaniard who lived in Toledo around the year of Christ 1467. The learned L’Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur, a Dutch scholar of Hebrew</note> translated it into Latin and published it under the title *The Talmudic Key, encompassing the formulas, dialectical, and rhetorical passages of the ancient Jews* <note>original Latin: *Clavis Thalmudica, complectens Formulas, loca Dialectica, & Rhetorica priscorum Judæorum*</note>. Venice, in folio <note>a large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once</note>. These are legal decisions regarding various cases, "according to the canons of Talmudic law" <note>Latin: *juxta juris Thalmudici canones*</note>. From this book one can clearly see how these stubborn people spent the first millennium after the birth of Christ with nothing but Talmudic whimsies <note>the author is expressing a common 18th-century Christian prejudice, dismissing complex Jewish legal debate as "whimsies" or "fancies"</note>, leaving the Bible entirely aside.

**Decided Rulings** <note>Hebrew: *Hilchoth pesukoth*; Latin: *Sententiæ decisæ*</note>, by **Rabbi Yehudai bar Nahman** <note>an 8th-century scholar and head of the academy at Sura</note>. This is a compendium <note>a brief summary or abridgment</note> of the previous work.

**The Regimen of Health** <note>Hebrew: *Hanhagath habberiuth*; Latin: *Regimen Sanitatis*</note>, by **Rabbi Moses, son of Maimon** <note>Moses Maimonides, the famous medieval philosopher and physician</note>. This work deals with the preservation of health and demonstrates that **Rambam** <note>an acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon</note> was also a skilled physician <note>Latin: *Medicus*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Book of Formation" (Sefer Yetzirah), a foundational work of Jewish mysticism. The previous page ended with a mention of the book's title, and this page continues a critical assessment of its supposed antiquity and authorship, traditionally attributed to the patriarch Abraham.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>Rabbinical Library <note>Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

God is also called the "God of *Israel*" within it; because, as is well known, *Abraham* had already been dead for a long time when his grandson, *Jacob*, was named *Israel*. And although the Jews, when **Scipio Sgambati** <note>Scipio Sgambati (1595–1652) was a Jesuit theologian and scholar known for his polemical works</note> pointed this out to them, gave the answer: 1. that *Abraham* was a prophet and could have known this beforehand, and 2. that *Abraham* himself was also called *Israel*; one can clearly see that these are merely feeble excuses <note>Literally "rotten fish" (*faule Fische*), a German idiom for worthless arguments or evasions</note>. For, although *Abraham* was a prophet in one respect or another, he could not therefore see and know everything in the future; and as for the claim that *Abraham* was also called *Israel*, that may well be written—as they say—in their deceitful **Genesis Rabbah** <note>The author uses the Hebrew title *Bereshit Rabbah*; he is dismissive of this collection of ancient rabbinic interpretations of the Book of Genesis</note>; but let them show it to us in the Bible. Who would not conclude from this upon what shifting sands the **Talmudic Kabbalah** <note>The *Kabbalah* refers to Jewish mystical tradition; the author views it skeptically here as a later invention built on shaky historical claims</note> is built, since this fabricated book laid the first cornerstone for it? Nevertheless, I wish to remind the reader that this book contains some fine testimonies for us against the Jews, and we can therefore use it quite well against them <note>It was common for Christian scholars of this era to use Jewish texts to argue for Christian truths, a practice known as Christian Kabbalah</note>. 

But that the Jews actually consider *Abraham* to be the author is testified by **Rabbi Judah Halevi the Spaniard** <note>Judah Halevi (c. 1075–1141) was a famous Jewish physician, poet, and philosopher</note> in his famous book **The Kuzari** <note>A foundational work of Jewish philosophy</note>, Discourse IV, Number 25, with these words: **The Book of Formation** <note>Original Latin: *Liber Jetzirah*; Hebrew: *Sefer Yetzirah*</note>...

<summary>This page presents a critical Christian perspective on the Sefer Yetzirah, arguing that its internal references to "Israel" prove it could not have been written by the patriarch Abraham, while still acknowledging its utility in theological debate.</summary>

<keywords>Sefer Yetzirah, Abraham, Scipio Sgambati, Genesis Rabbah, Kabbalah, Judah Halevi, The Kuzari, polemics, biblical history</keywords>

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

...the book is of our Father Abraham, and it is profound; its explanation is to be sought from afar, and it teaches the Divinity of GOD and His unity. <note>Original Latin: *liber est Abrahami Patris nostri, estque profundus, & Explicatio ejus longe arcessenda, ac docet Deitatem DEI, illiusque unitatem.*</note>

The newest edition of this book is the one which was published in the year 1642 in Amsterdam, in quarto <note>A book size where the paper is folded twice to create four leaves</note> under this title: *The Book of Formation* <note>Original title: *Liber Jetzirah*</note>, which is attributed to the Patriarch Abraham, together with the Commentary of Rabbi Abraham ben Dior <note>Also known as Abraham ben David or Ravad III, a 12th-century French rabbi</note> on the 32 Paths of Wisdom, with which the *Book of Formation* begins: translated and illustrated with notes by **Johann Stephan Rittangel**, Extraordinary Professor of Oriental Languages at the Electoral Academy of Königsberg <note>Original Latin: *Elect. Acad. Regiom.*</note>.

**The Epistle of the Animals** <note>Hebrew: *Iggereth baale Chajim*. This work is a famous philosophical fable about animals bringing a court case against humanity.</note>. These are conversations of man with all kinds of wild animals, crawling things, virtues, and vices. It is said that this book was first written in Greek, and afterwards someone translated it into Arabic; however, Rabbi Kalonymus translated it from Arabic into Hebrew. Published at Mantua in octavo <note>A book size where the paper is folded three times to create eight leaves</note> in the year of Christ 1557.

**The Epistle to the South** <note>Hebrew: *Iggereth Teman*, often called the *Letter to Yemen*</note>. The Southern Epistle, from Rabbi Moses Maimonides to Rabbi Nathanael and the other Jews dwelling in the South <note>Referring to Yemen</note>. Amsterdam, 1660, in quarto. In this letter, **Rambam** <note>An acronym for Rabbi Moshe ben Maimon, the common Jewish name for Maimonides</note> exhorts his aforementioned coreligionists to constancy in faith and to steadfast endurance under persecutions. ---

[Kalony]mus <note>Referring to Rabbi Kalonymus ben Kalonymus from the previous page</note> translated it from Arabic into Hebrew, and called it *The Gate of Hope* <note>Original Latin: *ostiam spei*</note>.

Note: *Epistle* <note>Hebrew: *Iggereth*; Latin: *Epistola*</note> is a title under which very many books are published, which one can look up at the very least in Buxtorf's *Rabbinical Library* <note>Referring to Johannes Buxtorf the Elder's *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*, a famous 17th-century bibliography of Jewish books</note>.

***Principles*** <note>Hebrew: *Ikkarim*; Latin: *fundamenta*, namely of the faith</note>, by **Rabbi Joseph Albo** of Spain <note>A 15th-century Jewish philosopher</note>, whom the Jews call the "Divine Philosopher." Published in Lublin, Poland, 1597, in quarto <note>A book size where the paper is folded twice to create four leaves</note>. This book, in which the Jewish articles of faith are explained and the Christians are often attacked, is held in very high regard by the Jews.

***Words of Understanding*** <note>Hebrew: *Imre binah*; Latin: *Verba Intelligentiæ*</note>, from Proverbs 1:2. By **Rabbi Isaschar Bähr**, son of Rabbi Moshe Petachya. Published in Prague, 1611. This book explains the foreign and difficult words of the Book of *Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism, the Kabbalah</note>, and the book *The Source of Wisdom* <note>Hebrew: *Mekor Chochma*; Latin: *fons Sapientiæ*</note>, from Proverbs 18:4, belongs to it as a second part; in the latter, the matters found in the *Zohar* are explained briefly and clearly.

***Words of the Desperate*** <note>Hebrew: *Imre noash*; Latin: *Verba desperati*</note>, from Job 6:26. By **Rabbi Solomon Piera**. It deals with Hebrew rhythms or the abundance of poetic words.

***The Rain of Sinners*** <note>Hebrew: *Yoreh chataim*; Latin: *pluvia peccatorum*</note>. Published in 1641, in duodecimo <note>A small book format where each sheet of paper is folded into 12 leaves</note>. It deals with sins.

***Lineages*** <note>Hebrew: *Juchasin*</note>, or *The Book of Lineages* <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Juchasin*; Latin: *Liber prosapiarum*</note>—

<meta>The catchword "rum" at the bottom of the page completes the Latin word "prosapiarum," meaning "of lineages" or "of families."</meta>

[Genealogies] <note>original: "rum", concluding the Latin title *Sefer Juchasin* or *Liber Genealogiarum*</note>. **Rabbi Abraham Zacuto**. Cracow. 1580. In quarto. **Sacred and Jewish History**, from the beginning of the world until the year of Christ 1500, is treated therein. <note>Abraham Zacuto was a famous 15th-century astronomer and historian whose charts were used by Christopher Columbus.</note>

***Prohibited and Permitted*** <note>Hebrew: *Issur ve-heter*; Latin: *vetitum & licitum*</note>, by **Rabbi Isaac of Düren**. In quarto. It deals almost entirely with the distinction of foods <note>Jewish dietary laws or Kashrut</note>, and is commonly called by the Jews, after the author's surname, the ***Gates of Düren*** <note>Hebrew: *Sha'are Dura*; Latin: *Portae Durae*</note>. **Rabbi Solomon Luria** <note>A preeminent 16th-century Polish rabbi known as the Maharshal</note> has illustrated this book with a **commentary**.

***City of the Mighty*** <note>Hebrew: *Ir Gibborim*; Latin: *Civitas fortium*</note>, from Proverbs 21:22. **Rabbi Ephraim**. Basel. 1580. These are ***Darshanim*** <note>Hebrew: preachers</note>, or sermons on the **Five Books of Moses**. The author was **Head of the Synagogue** <note>Latin: *Caput Synagogæ*</note> in Reuss, Lemberg <note>Now Lviv, Ukraine</note>.

### K.

***Kabbalah***, or ***The Book of Tradition*** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer ha-Kabbalah*; Latin: *Liber Kabbalae*</note>. **Rabbi Abraham ibn Daud** <note>Written here as Abraham bar Dior, a 12th-century philosopher and historian</note>. Venice. 1545. In quarto. This is a chronological-historical book, from the beginning of the world until 1161 AD.

***The Silver Bowl*** <note>Hebrew: *Ka'arat Kesef*; Latin: *Scutella argentea*</note>, by **Rabbi Joseph Ezobi** <note>Latinized as Hyssopeus, a 13th-century liturgical poet</note>, with a Latin translation by **Jean Mercier**. Paris, 1559. In octavo. It is a small book, but it contains many beautiful **moral maxims**.

***Acquire Understanding*** <note>Hebrew: *Kenah Binah*; Latin: *compara Scientiam*</note>, from Proverbs...

many different kinds of books are to be found there. See Buxtorf’s *Rabbinical Library* <note>Referring to the bibliographic work by Johannes Buxtorf</note>.
***Desire for Every Matter*** <note>Hebrew: *Lechol chephetz*; Latin: *omnis rei desiderium*</note>. Venice, 1552. In quarto <note>A book size where the printer's sheet is folded twice to make four leaves</note>. These are legal templates <term>formularia</term> for a variety of cases.
***Good Doctrine*** <note>Hebrew: *Lekach tobh*; Latin: *Doctrina bona*, from Proverbs 4:1</note>. This title is used for several different books, such as:
1. The Jews' Catechism, which has been printed several times in Amsterdam: which Witsius, in the preface to his *Sacred Miscellany* <note>Latin: *Miscellanea Sacra*</note>, Volume 1, Section 4, calls an "elegant catechism."
2. A Hebrew-German Lexicon <note>dictionary</note> of the entire Bible, with scattered Hebrew annotations <gloss>glosses</gloss> added for the more difficult passages, following the order of the biblical books. Prague. In quarto. One can use this book in place of a literal commentary.
3. An exposition on the Book of Esther, by **Rabbi Yom Tov Salon**. Constantinople.
4. An exposition on the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible</note>, by **Rabbi Moses Nager**. Constantinople.
***Levi ben Gerson***, also known as ***Ralbag*** <note>An acronym for Rabbi Levi ben Gerson, a prominent 14th-century philosopher and commentator</note>, a celebrated commentary on the Law, that is, the Pentateuch of Moses. Venice, 1547. In folio <note>A large book size where the sheet is folded only once</note>.
***Royal Garment*** <note>Hebrew: *Lebhusch Malchuth*; Latin: *Indumentum regium*, from Esther 6:8</note>. **Rabbi Mordecai**, called "the Fair" <note>Referring to Mordecai Yoffe, known for his beauty and wisdom</note>. Prague, 1610. In folio. It deals with Jewish customs and laws, and consists of 10 parts, each of which bears the title of a specific garment: such as "Garment..." <note>The author, Yoffe, titled his works "Lebushim" (Garments) as a pun on his name, which means "fair" or "beautiful," referencing the "royal garments" Mordecai wore in the Book of Esther</note>.

## M.

***The Orderer of Orders*** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'arikh ha-Ma'arakhot*; Latin: *Digerens digesta*</note>.
This is a dictionary <term>dictionarium</term> in which all the **Hebrew, Chaldean** <note>Aramaic</note>, **Talmudic-Rabbinic** words and **Hebrew abbreviations** are to be found. It was edited by **Philippe d'Aquin** <note>Latin: *Philippus Aquinas*</note>, a baptized Jew who was previously named **Michael**, and who afterwards became Professor of the **Hebrew** language in **Lyon**, France. Published in **Paris** <note>Latin: *Lutetia Parisiorum*</note>, 1629, in **folio** <note>a large book size</note>.
***The Arrangement of Divinity*** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'arekhet ha-Elohut*; Latin: *Dispositio Divinitatis*</note>. **Rabbi Perez**. **Mantua** and **Ferrara**, in quarto <note>in 4.</note>. This is a famous Kabbalistic <note>Jewish mystical</note> book, upon which **Rabbi Judah Hayyat** has written a commentary.
***The Stations***, or ***The Book of the Stations*** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer ha-Ma'amadot*; Latin: *Ordo stationum sacrarum*</note>. **Venice**, with a brief commentary, 1598, in quarto; and in **Verona**, in **German**. From this book one can see: first, what the nature and arrangement of the sacrifices for daily sins were,

...held, while the Temple still stood: namely, how the one who sinned at night brought his sacrifice in the morning; and the one who sinned during the day brought his sacrifice in the evening; whereupon the priest **absolved** him. Afterwards, [the text describes] how they, after the destruction of the Temple and the priesthood, **introduced** the "sacrifice of the lips"—as they call it <note>This refers to the concept that prayer (*Avodat Sefatayim*) serves as a substitute for animal sacrifices</note>—instead of actual sacrifices, along with the remembrance of the former sacrifices. For they firmly and steadfastly imagine that the current remembrance of the former sacrifices is just as valid as if they were still actually sacrificing. To this end, they have also increased their daily **Bible readings** <note>Latin: *Lectiones Biblicas*</note> in such a way that those who wish to be particularly pious and holy read one [portion] from the Bible, one from the **Mishnah** <term>Mishnah</term> <note>the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions</note>, and one from the **Gemara** <term>Gemara</term> <note>the component of the Talmud containing rabbinical analysis and commentary on the Mishnah</note>, and accompany such **readings** with this **commendation** <note>Latin: *elogio*</note>: 

-> *Whoever recites these daily can be certain that he is a child of eternal life.* <note>Original Latin: *Quicunque dicit ista quotidie, persuasum ipsi sit, se esse filium vitæ æternæ.*</note> <-

***The Fountain of Gardens*** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'ayan Gannim*; Latin: *Fons hortorum*</note>, **from the Song of Solomon 4:8** <note>Though the text cites chapter 4 verse 8, the phrase "Fountain of Gardens" traditionally appears in Song of Solomon 4:15</note>. 2. ***The Book of Responses*** <note>Latin: *Liber responsionum*</note> **to certain mathematical questions of Joseph del Medico** <note>A famous 17th-century Jewish physician, mathematician, and philosopher</note>, **which he proposed in the book** ***Elim*** <note>Hebrew: *Eilim* (אילם), referring to the biblical oasis with seventy palm trees, symbolizing the seventy questions in his book</note>. **Amsterdam, in quarto** <note>4.</note>.

### Spring of the Gardens <note>Hebrew: *Ma'ayan Gannim*; Latin: *Fons hortorum*</note>, from the Song of Solomon 4:15 <note>The source text cites chapter 4, verse 8, but the phrase "Spring of the Gardens" appears in verse 15.</note>.

Two books in particular are known under this title.

1. *A Book of Epistles* <note>Latin: *Liber Epistolicus*</note>, which has 25 letters and as many answers. Published in Venice, 1553, in octavo <term>octavo</term> <note>A book size resulting from folding a printed sheet into eight leaves.</note>.
2. *A Book of Responses to certain Mathematical questions* of **Joseph del Medico** <note>Joseph Solomon Delmedigo (1591–1655), a rabbi, physician, and mathematician.</note>, which he proposed in the book ***Elim*** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Elim*, a work on mathematics, astronomy, and physics.</note>. Published in Amsterdam, in quarto <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size resulting from folding a printed sheet into four leaves.</note>.

*Springs of Salvation* <note>Hebrew: *Ma'ayanei ha-Yeshuah*; Latin: *fontes salutis*</note>, taken from Isaiah 12:3. By Rabbi Isaac Abarbanel. This is a commentary on the Book of Daniel, which one could call beautiful if it were not directed against Christians. Published in the year 1551 in quarto <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>; it is rare.

*Cycle* <note>Hebrew: *Machzor*; Latin: *Cyclus, Circulus, Reditus in circulum*</note>. This is yet another title used for many different books. However, I will only mention the one here which is the Jews' well-known prayer book, which they use on their festival days. Nearly all the prayers are written in rhyme and are very intricately composed, so that probably very few Jews actually understand them; for this reason, some have even written commentaries upon it. One can obtain this book in all kinds of formats and also in German, although printed with Rabbinical letters <note>This refers to Old Yiddish, which was written using the Hebrew alphabet.</note>. It contains many things directed against Christians and the Roman Empire, for which reason it ought rightly to be censored <note>The original German uses the term *castriren* (to castrate), a common early modern term for the removal of "offensive" passages by censors.</note> in our lands, just as the Venetians have done. Although these stubborn people immediately write the censored passages back into the margins.

*Shield* <note>Hebrew: *Magen*; Latin: *Clypeus* or *Scutum*</note>. Again, a very common title for many and various books. Among these, the following is particularly well-known:

*Shield of the Fathers* <note>Hebrew: *Magen Avot*; Latin: *Scutum patrum*</note>. By Rabbi Simeon bar Zemach, of the Duran family, a descendant <note>Latin: *nepos*</note> of Ralbag <term>Ralbag</term> <note>The acronym for Rabbi Levi ben Gershom, also known as Gersonides, a famous 14th-century philosopher.</note>. It is a disputation against the Muhamme- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "Muhammedans" or Muslims.</note>

Rabbi Benjamin <note>Benjamin of Tudela, a famous 12th-century Jewish traveler.</note>, who traveled through nearly the entire world, described in this treatise everything noteworthy that he either saw himself or heard from other trustworthy persons, as he tells it. It was first translated into Latin by Benito Arias Montano and printed in Antwerp; however, later the learned L’Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur, a prominent 17th-century Dutch scholar of Hebrew.</note> illustrated it with a new version and learned notes and, as mentioned, had it printed in Leiden.

**The Tradition of Tradition** <note>Hebrew: *Masoreth Ha-Masoreth*</note>, by Rabbi Elijah Levita. It is a Masoretic key <note>The Masorah refers to the traditional system of notes and rules for the precise writing and pronunciation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, in which the logic of the entire Masorah is explained.

**The Tradition of the Jerusalem Talmud** <note>Hebrew: *Masoreth Talmud Yerushalmi*</note>. By Rabbi Judah of Thessaloniki. Constantinople, 1573, in quarto. This is an index of all matters found within the Jerusalem Talmud <note>The Jerusalem Talmud is a collection of Rabbinic commentaries on Jewish law compiled in the Land of Israel, distinct from the Babylonian Talmud.</note>.

**The Work of the Lord** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'aseh Adonai*; Latin: *Opus Domini*</note>. It is adorned with a beautiful preface, and the Jews say that he once turned away from their religion but later returned to it again <note>Duran was a forced convert to Christianity who eventually fled and reclaimed his Jewish identity</note>. Because of this book, he is commonly called "Ephodaeus."

**The Story, or Book of Stories** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'aseh*, or *Sefer ha-Ma'aseh*; Latin: *Liber Historiarum*</note>. Basel, in quarto. It is a Hebrew-German book <note>Yiddish</note> that tells various amusing Talmudic tales.

A commentary on the Latter Prophets. By the author Moses Alshech <note>A significant 16th-century rabbi and biblical commentator in Safed</note>.

**The Vision of the Priest** <note>Hebrew: *Mar'eh Kohen*; Latin: *Visio sacerdotis*</note>. Krakow, in quarto. It is an index of the most prominent chapters <note>Latin: *capita*</note> of the Book of Zohar <note>The primary work of Jewish mysticism or Kabbalah</note>.

**The Compositions of Ithiel** <note>Hebrew: *Mechaberoth Ithiel*; Latin: *Collectiones* or *compositiones*</note>, **by Rabbi Ithiel Charizi** <note>Judah al-Harizi (1165–1225), a Spanish Hebrew poet and translator</note>. The author first produced this book in the Arabic language, and indeed very elegantly according to the **style of the Quran** <note>original: *Alkoranischen Schreib-Art*</note>; however, **Charizi** subsequently translated it into an equally beautiful Hebrew, so that **Immanuel** <note>Immanuel of Rome (c. 1261–1335), an Italian Hebrew poet</note> himself calls him the most distinguished and incomparable **poet** for that reason.

**Mechilta.** This is an ancient and famous **commentary on Exodus** <note>Latin: *Commentarius in Exodum*. The Mechilta is a legal and homiletical interpretation of the second book of the Bible</note>. However, the Jews themselves do not know for certain who composed it.

By Rabbi Moses, son of Rabbi Israel Isserles <note>Moses Isserles (1530–1572), known as the Rema, a major Polish Ashkenazi legal authority</note>. Cremona, 1559, in quarto <term>quarto</term>. A **commentary** on the Book of **Esther**.

**Supporting the Infirm** <note>Hebrew: *Mechalkel machalah*; Latin: *Sustentans infirmitatem*</note>. By Rabbi Abraham Caslari. A medical book <note>Latin: *Liber Medicus*</note>.

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>
<meta>catchword: Me-</meta>

1. **Midrash on the Torah** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch hattorah*</note>. A homiletical commentary <note>Latin: *Commentarius homologeticus*</note> on the Law <note>the Five Books of Moses</note>.
2. **Midrash on the Five Scrolls** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Chamesch Megilloth*</note>. An allegorical commentary <note>Latin: *Comment. allegoricus*</note> on the five small books <note>The books of Ruth, Song of Songs, Ecclesiastes, Lamentations, and Esther</note>.
3. **Midrash on the Song of Songs and Lamentations** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Schir haschirim veecha*</note>. An allegorical commentary on the **Song of Songs** and **Lamentations**, taken from the **Zohar** <note>The central work of Jewish mysticism</note>.
4. **Midrash on the Psalms** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Tehillim*</note>. An allegorical commentary on the Psalms.
5. **Midrash on Proverbs** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Mischle*</note>. An allegorical commentary on the Book of Proverbs.
6. **Midrash on Samuel** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Schmuel*</note>. An allegorical commentary on the Book of Samuel. Thus one also finds:
7. **Midrash of the House of Elijah** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch debeth Eliahu*; Latin: *Medrasch Domus Eliæ*</note>, which consist of little stories or fables from the **Talmud**.
8. **Midrash of Wisdom** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch hachocmah*; Latin: *Medrasch Sapientiæ*</note> regarding **Aristotle's Physics**, by **Rabbi Judah**, priest of Toledo <note>Judah ben Solomon ha-Kohen (c. 1215–1274), a Spanish Jewish philosopher and scientist</note>.
9. **Midrash Samuel on the Chapters of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Medrasch Schmuel al Pirke abhoth*</note>. An allegorical commentary on the **Ethics of the Fathers** <note>Latin: *capitula Patrum*; a collection of ethical and moral teachings of the Mishnaic Rabbis</note>.

**The Scroll of the Book** <note>Hebrew: *Megillath Sepher*; Latin: *Volumen libri*</note>. These are those letters <note>epistles</note> which **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664), a renowned Christian Hebraist</note> had printed almost in their entirety under the title: *Instruction in Hebrew Letter-Writing* <note>Latin: *Institutio Epistolaris Hebraica*</note> in the year 1649 at Basel in **octavo** <term>octavo</term>.

**The Scroll of Secrets** <note>Hebrew: *Megillath Setharim*; Latin: *Volumen occultorum*</note>, by **Rabbi Samuel Motot** <note>A 14th-century Spanish philosopher and commentator</note>. Published in Venice. It is an explanation <note>Latin: *Expositio*</note> of **Ibn Ezra** <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a major medieval biblical commentator</note> regarding the **Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible</note>.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical bibliographic listing of Hebrew works under the letter "M," moving from the various Midrashic commentaries on page 128 to works concerning the Masorah (textual tradition) and the "Meir" (Illuminating) series of commentaries.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

**Mesaroth**, or **The Book of Mesaroth** <note>Hebrew: *Sepher Mesaroth*</note>, the *Book of the Masorahs* by Rabbi Joseph, son of Rabbi Samuel ben Raj, the King <note>likely referring to a family title or lineage</note>. Venice, quarto, 1607. In this book, it is demonstrated and illustrated with many examples that the words and phrases which occur so frequently in the Masorah <note>the traditional system of notes used to preserve the exact text of the Hebrew Bible</note> are not placed there by chance or in vain, but rather for significant reasons and with careful deliberation.

**The Mountains of Leopards** <note>Hebrew: *Meharre nemarim*; a title taken from Song of Songs 4:8</note>. Venice, quarto. An explanation of certain properties of speech and debate in the Talmud <note>the central text of Jewish civil and ceremonial law</note>. The Jews are so malicious that, against their own better knowledge and conscience, they exert themselves day and night to put a favorable "coat of paint" <note>an idiom for a deceptive or flattering gloss</note> upon the clumsy, foolish, and deceitful sayings of their supposed Sages <note>Latin: *Sapientum*</note>, so that one might understand and interpret them differently than the authors ever intended. And such books are the most harmful of all, having led many admirers of Rabbinical books astray.

**Meir**, meaning "illuminating" <note>Latin: *illuminans*</note>, is a title that the Jews are accustomed to use when they wish to explain and clarify either certain biblical books or other subjects. For example:

1. **Meir Jiob**, which *Illuminates Job* <note>Latin: *Illuminans Jobum*</note>. This is a mostly philosophical explanation of the Book of Job.

<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: 2. Meir</meta>

<summary>This page lists several significant Hebrew works, including a treatise on the Masorah's textual precision and the start of the "Meir" series of illuminating commentaries. The entry on Talmudic debate is notably characterized by the Christian author's polemical and suspicious view of Jewish interpretive methods.</summary>
<keywords>Masorah, Sepher Mesaroth, Rabbi Joseph ben Samuel, Talmud, Song of Songs, polemics, Meir Jiob, Book of Job, Jewish Sages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Hebrew titles beginning with the word "Meir" (Illuminating), following the entry for "Meir Jiob" (The Illuminator of Job) on the previous page. It then transitions into other titles beginning with the Hebrew word "Mekor" (Source or Fountain).</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

2. **The Illuminator of Psalms** <note>Hebrew: *Meir tehilloth*</note>. Illuminating the Psalms <note>original Latin: *Illuminans Psalmos*</note>. Written in the very same manner <note>as the preceding entry</note>.

3. **The Illuminator of the Path** <note>Hebrew: *Meir nathibh*</note>, illuminating the way <note>original Latin: *illuminans viam*</note>. Published in Venice, 1524, and Basel, 1581, in quarto <note>a book size where the sheet is folded twice to form four leaves</note>. This is the same Hebrew Concordance <note>an alphabetical index of words used in the Bible</note> which **Buxtorf the Elder**, though following a different and better method, also edited and published in an expanded version, which was printed in the year 1631.

**The Lighting of the Eyes** <note>Hebrew: *Meirath enajim*</note>, or the Illumination of the eyes <note>original Latin: *Illuminatio oculorum*</note>. Under this title, three books in particular are to be noted:

1. A brief and summary review <note>original Latin: *Recensio brevis & summaria*</note> of all 613 commandments <note>referring to the *Mitzvot*, the total number of biblical laws in Jewish tradition</note>. Prague, in octavo <note>a small book size</note>.
2. A ritual and legal book <note>original Latin: *Liber ritualis & Juridicus*</note>. Kraków.
3. An exposition of the Kabbalistic secrets <note>referring to *Kabbalah*, the Jewish tradition of mystical interpretation</note> which occur in the Commentaries on the Law <note>the Torah</note> by **Rabbi Moses Nachmanides**.

**Buying and Selling** <note>Hebrew: *Mekach Umemkar*</note>. Purchase and Sale <note>original Latin: *Emptio & Venditio*</note>. By **Rabbi Samuel the Priest**, son of **Chophni**. Note: Other authors have also written their books under this heading.

**The Fountain of Life** <note>Hebrew: *Mekor chajim*</note>, from the "fountain of life" mentioned in Psalm 36:10 <note>original Latin: *fons vitæ, ex Psal. XXXVI. 10*</note>. By **Rabbi Samuel Sirsa**. Mantua, in folio <note>a large book format</note>. This is a commentary upon the commentary of **Aben Ezra** <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a celebrated 12th-century scholar</note>.

**The Fountain of Wisdom** <note>Hebrew: *Mekor chochma*</note>, from the "fountain of wisdom" mentioned in Proverbs 18:4 <note>original Latin: *fons Sapientiæ, ex Prov. XVIII. 4*</note>. By **Rabbi Issachar Bär**, son of **Rabbi Moshe Petachja**. This is the second part of the book **Words of Understanding** <note>Hebrew: *Imre binah*; Latin: *Verba Intelligentiæ*</note>, which will be discussed in its proper place in this catalog.

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

**The Work of Arithmetic** <note>Hebrew: *Melecheth hammispar*; original Latin: *Opus arithmeticum*</note>, by **Rabbi Elijah Mizrachi**, also known as the Orientalist.

**Menachem**, a Rabbi of Recanati, has published the following books:
1. **Recanati**, or **The Explanation of the Law** <note>Hebrew: *Biur ha-Torah*</note>, a Commentary on the Law <note>the Torah</note>, or on the secrets of the Law, which **Rambam** <note>Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, known as Maimonides</note> wrote. Venice, 1545, in quarto <note>a book size where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>.
2. **The Reasons for the Commandments** <note>Hebrew: *Ta'ame mitzvot*; original Latin: *Rationes præceptorum*</note>. Basel, 1581, in quarto. In this work, the reasons for the commandments of Moses are provided.
3. **Decisions of the Law** <note>Hebrew: *Piske halachot*; original Latin: *Decisiones juris*</note>. Legal rulings.

**Menachem**, a Rabbi with the surname **Tzijoni**, published a Kabbalistic <note>relating to Jewish mysticism</note> explanation of the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible</note> under this title: **Tzijoni on the Law** <note>Hebrew: *Tzijoni al ha-Torah*; original Latin: *Tzijonius super Legem*</note>. Cremona, 1560, in quarto.

**The Candelabrum of Light** <note>Hebrew: *Menorat ha-Maor*; original Latin: *Candelabrum lucis*</note>, by **Rabbi Isaac Aboab**. Venice, 1544. A Talmudic book containing the <term>Aggadoth</term> <note>non-legal narrative and ethical teachings</note> and <term>Midrashim</term> <note>interpretive stories or homilies</note> of the entire Talmud, intended for the guidance of man and the shaping of his morals. It was also printed at Frankfurt am Mayn, 1687, in folio <note>a large book size where the sheet is folded only once</note>.

**The Golden Candelabrum** <note>Hebrew: *Menorat ha-Zahav*; original Latin: *Candelabrum aureum*</note>, which according to the opinion of some is by the same **Aboab**; but according to **Buxtorf**, it is by **Rabbi Solomon Luria**. A Kabbalistic book.

**The Light of the Eyes** <note>Hebrew: *Me'or Einayim*; original Latin: *Lumen oculorum*</note>. By **Rabbi Azariah dei Rossi** <note>referred to here by the Latin *Edomæi*, meaning "The Red," a play on his Italian surname</note>. Mantua, 1576, in quarto. This is the book from which it is chiefly to be seen,

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>.</header>

...how diligently the <term>Rabbis</term> read not only pagan but also Christian books: since one encounters many passages here from <term>Origen</term>, <term>Jerome</term>, and <term>Augustine</term> <note>three major early Christian Church Fathers</note>, as well as from <term>Aristeas</term>, <term>Cicero</term>, and <term>Plutarch</term> <note>noted classical Greek and Roman authors</note>. The book itself is divided into three parts. The first is called **The Voice of God** <note>Hebrew: *Kol Elohim*; Latin: *Vox DEI*</note> and deals with the earthquakes that occurred in **Ferrara** on November 18, 1571. The second, **The Glory of the Elders** <note>Hebrew: *Hadrath Zekenim*; Latin: *Honor, s. Decor Senum*</note>, see under the letter H. The third, **Words of Intelligence** <note>Hebrew: *Imre Binah*; Latin: *Verba Intelligentiae*</note>, see under J.

**Light of the Poor** <note>Hebrew: *Meor Aniyim*; Latin: *Lumen Pauperum*</note>.

**The Book of Luminaries** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Me'orot*; Latin: *Liber Luminarium*</note>. **Rabbi Zerachiah**. Scholarly commentary <note>original Latin: *Scholia*</note> on the **Rif** <note>The legal code of Rabbi Isaac Alfasi, often referred to as *Rabh Alphes*</note>.

**The Ancient Proverb** <note>Hebrew: *Meshal ha-Kadmoni*; Latin: *Proverbium Antiqui*</note>. By **Rabbi Isaac son of Solomon**, in quarto <note>a book size where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>. A rare book consisting of verses or rhymes and having five parts. The first deals with the praise of intelligence and wisdom <note>original Latin: *de laude Intelligentiae & Sapientiae*</note>. The second with the praises of repentance <note>original Latin: *de laudibus Poenitentiae*</note>. The third with counsel and prudence <note>original Latin: *de Consilio & prudentia*</note>. The fourth with humility <note>original Latin: *de humilitate*</note>, and the fifth with the fear [of God] <note>original Latin: *de Timore*</note>. It is, however, almost entirely presented in the form of allegories and fables.

**The Book of Uprightness** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Mesharim*; Latin: *Liber Rectitudinum*</note>, by **Rabbi Jeroham**. Venice, 1553, in folio <note>a large book size where the sheet is folded only once</note>.

**The Looser of Knots** <note>Aramaic: *Meshare Kitrin*; Latin translation provided: *solvens nodos*</note>, from **Daniel** 5:12. **Rabbi Abraham Levi**. Constantinople.

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

...deals with the calculation of the seventy weeks of Daniel <note>A reference to the prophecy in Daniel 9:24-27 concerning the timing of the Messiah</note>.

**The Servant of Moses** <note>Hebrew: *Meschareth Mosche*; Latin: *Minister Mosis*</note>, by Rabbi Kalonymus. These are sermons, or orations, in favor of <term>Rabbi Moses the Egyptian</term> <note>The famous philosopher and physician Maimonides, also known as Rambam</note>.

**The Restorer of Paths** <note>Hebrew: *Meschobhebh nethibhoth*; Latin: *restituens semitas*</note>, taken from Isaiah 58:12. By Rabbi Samuel Motot.

**The Seeker of the Lord** <note>Hebrew: *Mebhakhesch Adonai*; Latin: *Quærens Dominum*</note>, by Rabbi Jaari Gerson. Published in Venice. These are sermons.

**The Interpreter** <note>Hebrew: *Mesurgeman*; Latin: *Interpres*</note>. A Chaldaic Lexicon <note>A dictionary for Aramaic, then often called "Chaldaic," used in the Targums and the Talmud</note> by **Elijah Levita**. Published in Cologne, 1560.

**The Choice of Pearls** <note>Hebrew: *Mibhehar happeninim*; Latin: *Selectæ Margaritæ*</note>, by Rabbi Solomon ben Gabirol. This is a famous book containing ethical and moral precepts, which the author excerpted from all the Greek and Arabic philosophers and composed in the Arabic language. Rabbi Judah ibn Tibbon translated it into Hebrew, and <term>Drusius</term> <note>Johannes Drusius, a prominent 16th-century Christian Hebraist</note> reviewed it in his *Apophthegms of the Hebrews and Arabs*.

**The Perfection of Beauty** <note>Hebrew: *Michlal Jophi*; Latin: *perfectio pulchritudinis*</note>, from Psalm 50:2. By Rabbi Solomon ben Melech. Look to the history of the Rabbis of the 16th century.

**Perfection** <note>Hebrew: *Michlol*; Latin: *Perfectio*</note>. The Grammar of Rabbi David Kimchi <note>Known as the Radak, a foundational Hebrew grammarian</note>. Venice, 1545, in <term>folio</term> <note>a large book size where the printing sheet is folded only once</note>.

**A Golden Poem of David** <note>Hebrew: *Michtam leDavid*; Latin: *Aureolum, carmen Davidis*</note>. By Rabbi David Vidal. Venice, 1546, in <term>octavo</term> <note>a smaller book size where the sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves</note>. It is an explanation of the thirteen Jewish articles of faith <note>The fundamental principles of Judaism as formulated by Maimonides</note>.

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**The Desert of Judah** <note>Hebrew: *Midbar Jehudah*; Latin: *Desertum Judææ*</note>. A Book of Sermons <note>original Latin: *Liber Concionum*</note>. Venice, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>.

**Ethics**, or **The Book of Ethics** <note>Hebrew: *Middoth*, or *Sepher hammiddoth*; Latin: *Liber morum*</note>. These are the *Books of Ethics* by **Aristotle**, which Don Meir—a physician to the King of Castile—translated into the Hebrew language. It is illustrated with a commentary by Rabbi Joseph, the son of Rabbi Shem Tob.

**Midrash**, see **Medrash**. <note>A cross-reference to another spelling of the term for rabbinic biblical interpretation</note>

**The Tower of David** <note>Hebrew: *Migdal David*; Latin: *Turris Davidis*</note>, named from Song of Solomon 4:4. Various books belong to this title, such as:
1. A work by **Rabbi Mordecai Dato**, in which he shows the important reasons the Jews have for waiting both for the redemption from their current exile and for the rebuilding of the Temple.
2. The sermons on the Law <note>original Latin: *conciones in Legem*</note> by **Rabbi David Stella**.
3. A grammar by **Rabbi David Provincialis**.

**The Strong Tower** <note>Hebrew: *Migdal Odz*; Latin: *Turris robusta*</note>. By Rabbi Yom Tob bar Abraham, who is called by the abbreviation **Ritba**. <note>Ritba is a famous 13th-14th century Spanish medieval rabbi and commentator</note>

**The Possession of Abraham** <note>Hebrew: *Mikne Abraham*; Latin: *Peculium Abrahami*</note>. A grammar by **Abraham de Balmis**, a Doctor of Arts and Medicine, including a Latin version. Venice, 1523, in quarto. The book itself turned out as poorly as the translation, and it is to be recommended to no one. <note>A scathing review by the bibliographer regarding the quality of de Balmis's Hebrew-Latin grammar</note>

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

The scope and intention of all festivals, laws, and rites are explained allegorically.

**The Small Sanctuary** <note>Hebrew: *Mikdash me’at*; Latin: *Sanctuarium parvum*</note>, named from Ezekiel 11:16. <note>In this verse, God promises to be a "small sanctuary" for the exiled Israelites; the term is often used by Jewish authors to refer to the synagogue or to works of study.</note> An exposition of certain Talmudic ceremonies.

**The Hope of Israel** <note>Hebrew: *Mikveh Yisrael*; Latin: *Exspectatio*, or *Spes Israëlis*</note>, from Jeremiah 14:8. By **Rabbi Menasseh ben Israel**. *It treats the future redemption of the Jews. Amsterdam, 1698, in <term>sextodecimo</term> <note>A small book format where each printing sheet is folded into 16 leaves</note>. Rabbi Judah ben Shemaiah, a Kabbalist, has also written a Kabbalistic book on Jewish customs under this title.*

**The Wars of the Lord** <note>Hebrew: *Milchamoth Adonai*; Latin: *Liber bellorum Domini*</note>. By **Ralbag** <note>An acronym for Rabbi Levi ben Gerson, also known as Gersonides (1288–1344), a famous philosopher and astronomer</note>. Riva di Trento, 1560, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size where the printing sheet is folded only once</note>. A theological-metaphysical book, collected from the writings of **Aristotle** and other philosophers. *Under this same title, Rabbi Jacob bar Reuben wrote an ungodly disputation against Christians.* <note>The author of this bibliography views the polemic against Christianity as "godless," reflecting the perspective of 17th/18th-century Christian scholars.</note>

**The War of Wisdom and Wealth** <note>Hebrew: *Milchamath hachochmah veha’osher*; Latin: *Bellum Sapientiæ & Divitiarum*</note>. Kraków, in <term>octavo</term>.

**The Obligatory War** <note>Hebrew: *Milchamath mitzvah*; Latin: *Bellum Præcepti*</note>. By **Rabbi David Kimchi**. <note>Kimchi, known as the Radak (1160–1235), was a grammarian and biblical commentator</note> A disputation against Christians. Also, an exposition of the Book of Esther by **Rabbi Solomon bar Zemach**.

**Fillings**, or **The Book of Fillings** <note>Hebrew: *Milluim*, or *Sefer hamilluim*; Latin: *Liber Supplementorum*, or *Impletionum*</note>. *Such as: A Kabbalistic book by Rabbi Azariah, son of the holy Rabbi Menachem.*

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**Rabbi Isaac Israeli’s Supplement** to his own book, **The Foundation of the World** <note>original title: *Jesod Olam*</note>.

**Rabbi Joseph Ezobioni**, <gloss>son of Hyssop</gloss>. <note>The name Ezobi is a Hebrew family name derived from the word for the hyssop plant.</note>

**Words of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Milli de-avot*; Latin: *Verba Patrum*</note>, by **Rabbi Joseph Hivan**. Cracow. In <term>quarto</term> <note>A book format where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>. A commentary on the **Sayings of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Pirkei Avot*, a collection of ethical teachings from the Mishnaic period</note>.

**Customs of the Karaites** <note>Hebrew: *Minhage Karraim*; Latin: *Ritus Karraitar*</note>. Venice. <note>The Karaites are a Jewish movement characterized by the recognition of the written Torah alone as its supreme authority in legal matters and theology, rejecting the Oral Torah/Talmud.</note>

**The Offering of Jealousy** <note>Hebrew: *Minchath Kenaoth*; Latin: *Oblatio Zelotypiarum*</note>, from Numbers 5. By **Rabbi Abraham Mari bar Moses**.
It is a refutation of **Rabbi Solomon ben Adret** <note>A towering 13th-century legal authority known as the Rashba</note> and his disciples, who excommunicated those who began to study philosophy before their 30th year; it includes the letters collected for this controversy.

**Customs** <note>Hebrew: *Minhagim*; Latin: *Ritus*</note>, or Rituals and Prayer Books.
Very many authors have written such books. However, they do not deal with ancient customs, but rather with the current practices of the Jews; therefore, they cannot be used for the explanation of antiquities <note>The author distinguishes between contemporary Jewish life and the historical "antiquities" of the Biblical era</note>.

**A Mixed Offering** <note>Hebrew: *Mincha Beluah*; Latin: *Oblatio mixta*</note>, from Leviticus 9:4. By **Rabbi Abraham Menachem**. Verona. In <term>quarto</term>. A commentary on the Law <note>The Torah</note>.

**The Offering of Judah** <note>Hebrew: *Minchath Jehudah*; Latin: *Oblatio Jehudae*</note>. By **Rabbi Judah Chayat**. It is a commentary on the book **The Order of Divinity** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'arekhet ha-Elohut*; Latin: *Dispositio Divinitatis*</note>.

**The Offering of the Priest** <note>Hebrew: *Minchath Kohen*; Latin: *Oblatio sacerdotis*</note>. By **Rabbi Joseph the Priest** <gloss>Hacohen</gloss>, son of Rabbi Shinia the Priest.

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note></header>

[Published in] Constantinople, 1598, in <term>octavo</term> <note>A book size where the printing sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note>. These are **Masoretic collections** <note>Studies and notes on the traditional Hebrew text of the Bible, intended to preserve its exact wording and pronunciation</note> on the **Pentateuch of Moses** <note>The first five books of the Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note>.

**The Works of God** <note>Hebrew: *Miphaloth Elohim*; Latin: *Opera Dei*</note>, a title taken from Psalm 66:5. By **Don Isaac Abarbanel** <note>A celebrated 15th-century Jewish statesman, philosopher, and Bible commentator.</note>. Venice, 1592, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book format where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>. Regarding the **Creation of the World** in opposition to the views of Aristotle.

**The Proverbs of Solomon** <note>Hebrew: *Mishle Shlomoh*; Latin: *Proverbia Salomonis*</note>. By **Rabbi Gedaliah of Imola** in Italy, 1557. This is an explanation of all types of dreams.

**Fox Parables** <note>Hebrew: *Mishle Shualim*; Latin: *Adagia Vulpium* or *Fabulæ Vulpeculæ*</note>. By **Rabbi Berechiah ha-Nakdan** <note>Known as "the Punctuator" or "the Grammarian."</note>. Mantua, 1557. This work is of a similar nature to the previous book and is very rare. The title also demonstrates how the word *Mishle* <note>Proverbs or Parables</note> is understood and used by the Jews in various ways.

**The Mishnah** <note>The first major written collection of the Jewish oral traditions</note>, or the **Book of Mishnayot** <note>Hebrew: *Sepher hammi-shnajoth*; Latin: *Liber Traditionum* or *Legis secundæ oraliter traditæ*</note>, the Book of Traditions, or the Second Law handed down orally. This is the same as the text of the **Talmud**. See the entry for "Mishnah."

**The Repetition of the Law** <note>Hebrew: *Mishneh Torah*; Latin: *Repetitio Legis*</note>. By **Joseph Karo**. Venice, 1577, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book format where the printing sheet is folded only once.</note>. This book is held in very high regard by the Jews and contains Talmudic traditions. <note>The *Mishneh Torah* was originally the code of law by Maimonides; Joseph Karo authored a major commentary upon it.</note>

**The Judgments of Samuel** <note>Hebrew: *Mishpate Shemuel*; Latin: *Judicia Samuelis*</note>. By **Rabbi Samuel Alcali**. Venice. In this work, several passages from the Talmud are explained.

**The Scales of Weight** <note>Hebrew: *Modzne Mishkal*; Latin: *Statera ponderis*</note>, a title taken from Ezekiel 5:1. By **Rabbi Jacob Romanus of Constantinople**. It is a work on **Hebrew prosody** <note>The study of poetic meters and the patterns of sounds in poetry.</note>, in which 1,348 types of verse are found.

...well drawn from both the Bible and ancient writings.

**Instruction of the Intellect** <note>Hebrew: *Musar Ha-Sekhel*; Latin: *Eruditio intellectus*</note>, a title taken from Proverbs 1:3. By **Rabbi Hai Gaon** <note>The last of the great heads of the Talmudic academy in Pumbedita (modern Iraq), active in the early 11th century.</note>. This is a small book in <term>octavo</term> and treats very beautiful moral maxims that lead toward virtue and godliness, written in verse. **Jean Mercier** <note>A prominent 16th-century French Hebraist.</note> translated it into Latin, and it was printed in Paris in the year 1561.

## N.

**The Inheritance of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Nachalat Avot*; Latin: *Haereditas Patrum*</note>, a title taken from Proverbs 18:14 <note>The verse "Houses and riches are the inheritance of fathers" is usually found at Proverbs 19:14.</note>. The book *Ethics of the Fathers* <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Avot*; a compilation of the ethical teachings and maxims of the Rabbis from the Mishnaic period.</note>, with commentaries by **Rambam** <note>Moses Maimonides, the famous 12th-century philosopher.</note> and **Don Isaac Abarbanel** <note>A prominent 15th-century Jewish statesman, philosopher, and biblical commentator.</note>. Venice, in <term>quarto</term>.

**The Consolation of Boethius** <note>Hebrew: *Nechamat Boethio*</note>. This is Boethius’s book *The Consolation of Philosophy*, which **Rabbi Samuel ben Banshat** has elegantly translated into Hebrew.

**Silver Points** <note>Hebrew: *Nekudot Ha-Kesef*; Latin: *Puncta argentea*</note>, a title taken from the Song of Solomon 1:11. An explanation of the Song of Songs. By **Rabbi Abraham Laniado**. Venice.

**The River Pishon** <note>Hebrew: *Nehar Pishon*; Latin: *Fluvius Pishon*</note>. Sermons by **Rabbi Isaac Aboab**. Constantinople.

**The Wise Soul** <note>Hebrew: *Nefesh Ha-Chakhamah*; Latin: *Anima sapiens*</note>. On the soul, its faculties, and its state after this life. Basel, in <term>quarto</term>.

**The Dispersions of Judah** <note>Hebrew: *Nefuzot Yehudah*; Latin: *Dispersiones Jehudae*</note>, a title taken from Isaiah 11:12. Fifty-two sermons by **Rabbi Joseph Moscato**. Venice, in <term>quarto</term>.

<header>Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note></header>

**The Lamp of the Commandment** <note>Hebrew: *Ner Mitzvah*; original Latin: *Lumen præceptum*</note>, a title taken from Proverbs 6:23. By **Rabbi Samuel Cohen**. These are also sermons. There is also an index of Talmudic books <note>The Talmud is the central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah and the Gemara.</note> available under this same name.

**The Eternity of Israel** <note>Hebrew: *Netzach Yisrael*; original Latin: *Æternitas Israelis*</note>, a title taken from 1 Samuel 15:29. By **Rabbi Judah Loew ben Bezalel** <note>Commonly known as the Maharal of Prague (c. 1520–1609), a leading communal leader and philosopher.</note>. Printed in Prague, 1599, in <term>folio</term>. It treats the certainty and necessity of the Jewish redemption from their current captivity; also <note>original Latin: *It.*, abbreviation of *Item*</note> the future of their supposed Messiah, the great dignity of the Jews, and their perpetual preservation. Through such books, the Jews are quite bewitched, so that they cannot be converted to Christ. <note>The author here reflects the common 17th-century Christian Hebraist view that Jewish philosophical works acted as barriers to conversion.</note>

**The Abode of Peace** <note>Hebrew: *Neveh Shalom*; original Latin: *Habitaculum pacis*</note>, a title taken from Isaiah 32:18. By **Rabbi Abraham Shalom**. Venice, 1575, in <term>quarto</term>. The author treats all kinds of sciences, both spiritual and secular, and examines and refutes an immense number of questions from **Aristotle**.

**Pointing** <note>Hebrew: *Nikkud*</note>, or *The Book of Vowel Pointing* <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Ha-Nikkud*; original Latin: *Liber punctationis*</note>, or regarding the art of vowel pointing <note>The system of dots and dashes used in Hebrew to indicate vowel sounds, which are not part of the standard alphabet.</note>. It belongs to the book *The Book of Rest* <note>Likely *Sefer Ha-Nuach*</note> and is the third part of it. There is also another book existing under this name which the famous Kabbalist <note>A follower of Kabbalah, the tradition of Jewish mysticism.</note> **Rabbi Aaron** wrote.

**Joseph’s Reasonings** <note>Hebrew: *Nimukei Yosef*; original Latin: *Observationes* or *Annotationes Josephi*</note>, the observations or annotations of Joseph, by **Rabbi Joseph Habib**, on

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**Rabbi Alfasi** <note>Isaac ben Jacob Alfasi (1013–1103), a foundational North African scholar whose work condensed the legal portions of the Talmud.</note>. Most items pertaining to this author are still in manuscript <note>original Latin abbreviation: *MSStis*</note>.

**The Soul of Adam** <note>Hebrew: *Nishmat Adam*; original Latin: *Anima hominis*</note>, a title taken from Proverbs 20:27. It treats the human soul, reward, and punishment. Hanau. 1617. <term>Quarto</term> <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>.

**The Breath of Life** <note>Hebrew: *Nishmat Hayyim*; original Latin: *Anima Vitæ*</note>. By **Rabbi Manasseh ben Israel** <note>A prominent Portuguese rabbi and diplomat in Amsterdam (1604–1657).</note>. Regarding the immortality of the soul. Amsterdam. 1652. <term>Quarto</term>.

**Victory** <note>Hebrew: *Nizzachon*; original Latin: *Victoria*</note> and **The Book of Victory** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Ha-Nizzachon*; original Latin: *Liber Victoriæ*</note> is a title that the Jews like to use when they write against us Christians and the history of the Gospels. From this, one can observe how stubbornly and firmly they must imagine themselves to have a just cause—so much so that they think it impossible to refute them, and therefore "sing triumph before the victory" <note>original Latin: *triumphum ante victoriam singen*—a proverb for celebrating success before it is earned.</note>. The following in particular are well known:

**The Victory of Rabbi Mattathias**, which is the oldest.

**The Victory of Rabbi Lippmann of Mühlhausen**. It is explicitly directed against the history of the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels.</note> and is a truly godless book <note>The author's harsh language reflects the intense religious polemics of the 17th century, where Christian Hebraists studied Jewish texts both to learn from them and to find arguments for conversion.</note>.

**The Book of Victory by Rabbi Joseph Kimchi**, father of Rabbi David <note>Joseph Kimchi (c. 1105–1170) was a medieval Spanish grammarian and polemicist; his son David, or "Radak," became one of history's most influential Bible commentators.</note>.

**Awesome in Praises** <note>Hebrew: *Nora Tehillot*; original Latin: *Venerandus laudibus*</note>, from Exodus 15:11. By **Rabbi Joel ibn Shoaib**. A commentary on the Psalms.

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**Nuach**, or *The Book of Rest* <note>Hebrew: *Sepher ha-Nuach*; Latin: *Liber quietis*</note>.
This is a book on grammar and consists of four parts:
1) This present *Book of Rest*, which deals with the "quiescent letters" <note>Hebrew letters (Aleph, He, Vav, Yod) that can sometimes be silent or act as vowels.</note>.
2) *The Book of Doubling* <note>Hebrew: *Sepher ha-Kephel*; Latin: *Liber duplicationis*</note>, which deals with "doubled verbs" <note>Verbs where the second and third letters of the root are the same.</note>.
3) *The Book of Vowel Pointing* <note>Hebrew: *Sepher ha-Nikkud*; Latin: *Liber punctationis*</note>.
4) *The Book of Preparation* <note>Hebrew: *Sepher ha-Rikhah*; Latin: *Liber condimenti* (Book of Seasoning)</note>.
The author of this grammar is **Rabbi Judah ben David Hayyuj**, an Arab from Fez <note>A 10th-century Moroccan grammarian who revolutionized Hebrew linguistics by discovering that all Hebrew roots consist of three letters.</note>, surnamed **Chijug**.

->O.<-

**Lover of Justice** <note>Hebrew: *Ohebh Mishpat*; Latin: *Diligens judicium*</note>, by **Rabbi Simon Duran**. It is a commentary on the Book of Job.

**The Tent of Jacob** <note>Hebrew: *Ohel Yaakobh*; Latin: *Tentorium Jacobi*</note>, by **Rabbi Jacob**, son of Samuel Capellmann of Breisach. <term>Quarto</term>. An explanation of the book *Ikkarim* <note>The *Sefer Ha-Ikkarim* (Book of Principles) by Joseph Albo, a famous 15th-century work on the fundamental dogmas of Judaism.</note>.

**The Tent of Meeting** <note>Hebrew: *Ohel Moed*; Latin: *Tentorium conventus*</note>. By **Solomon of Urbino**. Venice, 1548. This is a book of Hebrew synonyms gathered from the Bible, which provides examples for each individual word and the specific location where they are found. An alphabetical index of all words has been added.
There is also another book existing under this title regarding the articles of the Jewish faith. 
Likewise, a Kabbalistic book by **Rabbi Bahya** <note>Likely Bahya ben Asher, a medieval Spanish scholar known for incorporating mystical traditions into his biblical commentaries.</note>.

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**Tents of Kedar** <note>Hebrew: *Oholei Kedar*; Latin: *Tentoria Kedar*</note>. By **Rabbi Solomon of Jerusalem** <note>Rabbi Shlomo al-Qustantini</note>. An Arabic Calendar.

**The Burnt Offering of Isaac** <note>Hebrew: *Olath Yitzchak*; Latin: *Holocaustum Isaaci*</note>. A book of legal decisions. Prague, 1608. <term>Quarto</term>.

**The Burnt Offering of the Sabbath** <note>Hebrew: *Olath Shabbat*; Latin: *Holocaustum Sabbathi*</note>, based on Numbers 28:10. By **Rabbi [Joel] ben Shuiv** of Constantinople. A commentary on the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note>.

**The Continual Burnt Offering** <note>Hebrew: *Olath Tamid*; Latin: *Holocaustum Juge*</note>. By **Rabbi Moses Albelda**. Venice, 1526, <term>Folio</term>. A philosophical commentary on the entire Law <note>The Torah.</note>.

**The Small World** <note>Hebrew: *Olam Katon*; Latin: *Mundus parvus*, or *Microcosmus*</note>. An ancient philosophical work by **Rabbi Joseph** <note>Likely Joseph ibn Tsaddik, a 12th-century Spanish philosopher and poet.</note>.

**The Gleanings of Ephraim** <note>Hebrew: *Olellot Ephraim*; Latin: *Racemationes Ephraimi*</note>, based on Judges 8:2. By **Rabbi Ephraim** <note>Ephraim Luntschitz, a famous 16th-century preacher from Prague.</note>. This is a book of sermons.

**The Depth of the Path** <note>Hebrew: *Omeq Halakha*; Latin: *profunditas ambulans* (the walking depth)</note>. Amsterdam, 1710. <term>Folio</term>. This book contains all those passages of the Talmud which involve mathematics <note>original German: *Mathesin*</note> or are otherwise based on specific geometric figures <note>original German: *Figuration*</note>. For the most part, these passages are not understood by Jews or Christians who are not well-versed in this field of study. The author follows the Talmudic order of the six **Orders** <note>The *Sedarim*, which are the primary divisions of the Mishnah and Talmud.</note> and presents everything very clearly, along with the relevant diagrams. **Adam Andreas Knollen** <note>Latin: *Adamus Andreas Cnollenius*</note>, who died in 1714 while serving as a deacon in Fürth near Nuremberg, reviewed this work, and one can find this review in the...

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

...the so-called *Innocent News* <note>original German: *unschuldigen Nachrichten*, referring to the periodical *Unschuldige Nachrichten von alten und neuen theologischen Sachen*, a famous 18th-century theological journal</note> of the year 1714, page 267 and following.

**Light** <note>Hebrew: *Or*; Latin: *Lux*</note>. This is once again a very common title for many and various books. I wish to list the following here:

1. **Light of the Nations** <note>Hebrew: *Or Ammim*; Latin: *Lux populorum*</note>, by **Rabbi Obadiah [Sforno] the Physician**, Bologna, 1550. A medical book. <note>While Sforno was a physician, this specific work is typically known as a philosophical treatise defending Judaism against Aristotelianism; the cataloger may have misidentified the subject based on the author's profession.</note>

2. **Light of the Lord** <note>Hebrew: *Or Adonai*; Latin: *Lux Domini*</note>, by **Rabbi Chasdai [Crescas]**, Ferrara. It is an exhortation to Talmudic study. There is also a commentary on the Law <note>The Torah or Pentateuch</note> by **Rabbi Obadiah Sforno** available under this title, from the year 1550.

3. **Light of the Eyes** <note>Hebrew: *Or Einayim*; Latin: *Lux oculorum*</note>. A Kabbalistic book <note>referring to Jewish mysticism</note>. Cremona. <term>Quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>.

4. **A New Light** <note>Hebrew: *Or Chadash*; Latin: *Lux recens*</note>, by **Rabbi Judah bar Bezalel** <note>Known as the Maharal of Prague, a famous 16th-century scholar</note>. Prague, 1600. <term>Folio</term> <note>A large book size where the sheet is folded only once.</note>. A commentary on the Book of Esther. There is also another book under this title: **Rabbi Chaim's** book of thanksgiving and blessings, printed in Amsterdam in 1671 in <term>quarto</term>.

5. **Light of Life** <note>Hebrew: *Or Ha-Chayim*; Latin: *Lumen vitæ*</note>. Ferrara. <term>Quarto</term>. Once again, an exhortation to Talmudic study and a warning against philosophy.

6. **Light of the Intellect** <note>Hebrew: *Or Ha-Sekhel*; Latin: *Lux intellectus*</note>. A Kabbalistic book that is held in little regard.

7. **Precious Light** <note>Hebrew: *Or Yaqar* or *Or Yeqarot*; Latin: *Lux pretiosa*</note>, taken from Zechariah 14:6. Lublin. <term>Folio</term>. A Kabbalistic book.

A critical work containing explanations of foreign words and variant readings in the *Rabboth* <note>Midrash Rabbah, a collection of ancient homiletic interpretations of the Torah</note>.

2. **Sign of the Soul** <note>Hebrew: *Ot Nefesh*; Latin: *Signum animae*</note>. A clarification of certain commentaries by **Abraham ibn Ezra** <note>a celebrated 12th-century Spanish polymath and biblical commentator</note>.

A Kabbalistic book, which was excerpted from the work of Rabbi Moses Cordovero.

# Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

**The Face of Isaac** <note>Hebrew: *Pene Yitzchak*; Latin: *Facies Isaaci*</note>. By **Rabbi Isaac**.

**The Face of Moses** <note>Hebrew: *Pene Mosheh*; Latin: *Facies Mosis*</note>. By **Rabbi Moses Almosnino**. An explanation of the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible</note>.

**Pearls** <note>Hebrew: *Peninim*</note>, also known as **The Book of Pearls** <note>Hebrew: *Sefer Peninim*</note> or **An Abundance of Gems** <note>Hebrew: *Rov Peninim*; Latin: *Copia carbunculorum*</note>, named from Proverbs 20:15. By **Moses Alsheich**. Venice, in <term>Quarto</term> <note>a book size created by folding a printed sheet twice to form four leaves</note>. An explanation of the Proverbs of Solomon.

**The Lily Flower** <note>Hebrew: *Perach Shushan*; Latin: *flos lilii*</note>, named from 1 Kings 7:26 <note>The source text cites verse 27, but the description of floral work usually refers to the temple vessels in verse 26</note>. By **Rabbi Moses ben Habib**. A book on grammar.

**Commentary** <note>Hebrew: *Perush*; Latin: *Commentarius*</note>. A very well-known title, under which the following is particularly famous:
**The Alsheich Commentary** <note>Hebrew: *Perush Alsheich*</note>, on the entire Bible. Venice, in <term>Quarto</term>.

**Commentary on Job** <note>Hebrew: *Perush Iyov*; Latin: *in Jobum*</note>. By **Rabbi Isaac ben Arama**.

**Commentary on the Targum of Jonathan and the Jerusalem Targum** <note>Hebrew: *Perush al Targum Yonatan ve-Targum Yerushalmi*</note>. A short commentary on the Translation of Jonathan and the Translation of Jerusalem <note>A 'Targum' is an ancient Aramaic translation or paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note> regarding the five books of Moses. Prague, 1609.

**The Small Section** <note>Hebrew: *Pesikta Zutarta*; Latin: *Pesikta minus*</note>. A very old commentary on the third, fourth, and fifth books of Moses <note>Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy</note>. Venice, 1546, in <term>Folio</term> <note>the largest standard book size, made by folding a sheet once</note>.

**Chapters** <note>Hebrew: *Pirkei*; Latin: *Capitula*</note>; we find this title placed before many books, such as:
1. **Chapters of the Fathers** <note>Hebrew: *Pirkei Avot*; Latin: *Capitula Patrum*</note>. # Rabbinical Library <note>original Latin: *Bibliotheca Rabbinica*</note>

2. **Chapters of Elijah** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Eliyahu*; Latin: *Capitula Eliae*</note>. This is a book on grammar in which the author treats everything pertaining to Hebrew grammar in four special chapters. **Sebastian Münster** <note>Latin: *Munsterus*, a famous cartographer and Hebraist</note> translated it into Latin and had it printed in Basel.

3. **Chapters of Repentance** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke ha-Teshuvah*; Latin: *Capita poenitentiae*</note>.

4. **Chapters of the Palaces** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Heikhalot*; Latin: *Capitula de Templis*</note>. A highly esteemed Kabbalistic book <note>referring to early Jewish mysticism involving "palaces" or "chambers" of the divine realm</note>.

5. **Chapters of Moses** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Mosheh*; Latin: *Capitula Mosis*</note>. By **Rabbi Moses Almosnino**. Published in Thessaloniki. This is an explanation of the book *Ethics of the Fathers* <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Avot*, a compilation of ethical teachings from the Mishnaic period</note>.

6. **Chapters of Rabbi Berachiel** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Rabbi Berakhiel*</note>. A Kabbalistic book.

7. **Chapters of Rabbi Eliezer** <note>Hebrew: *Pirke Rabbi Eliezer*; Latin: *Capitula R. Eliezeris*</note>, who is called **Rabbi Eliezer the Great**, son of Hyrcanus, by the Jews. Venice, 1544, in <term>Quarto</term>. It is a universal history from the beginning of the world until the author’s own time; he lived not long after the era of the Second Temple <note>the Jewish holy temple in Jerusalem destroyed by the Romans in 70 CE</note> and was married to the sister of **Rabban Gamaliel II**. It is therefore a very famous book among the Jews, although Rabbi **Abraham Zacuto** <note>a Sephardic astronomer and historian</note> sincerely con-

### Bibliotheca Rabbinica.

**Master of Actions** <note>original: "Rabh peálim"</note>, *Great in Deeds* <note>original: "Magnus factis"</note>, by Rabbi Isaac ben Latif. A philosophical book. Rabbi Joseph Zarki’s *Grammar* also carries this title.

**Recanati.** This is what Rabbi Menachem of Recanati called his <term>Perush</term><note>A Hebrew term for a commentary or explanation</note>, or *Commentary on the Secrets of the Law of Maimonides* <note>original: "super secreta Legis Rambami"</note>. Venice, 1523. Quarto.

**Ralbag.** This title <note>An acronym for Rabbi Levi ben Gerson, a 14th-century philosopher and astronomer also known as Gersonides</note> is borne by Rabbi Levi ben Gerson’s *Commentary on the Law* <note>The Torah</note>. Venice, 1547. Folio.

**Ramban.** Similarly, the name for the *Commentary on the Law* by Ramban, or Rabbi Moses ben Nachman <note>A leading 13th-century medieval Jewish scholar and mystic, also known as Nachmanides</note>. Same place [Venice], 1545. Folio.

**The Best of Spices** <note>original: "Rasche besámim"</note>, *the most excellent aromatics* <note>original: "præstantissima aromata"</note>, taken from Song of Solomon 4:14. A grammar by Rabbi Menachem Thamar.

**Rasiel, or Radziel**, is a Kabbalistic-magical book, named after the angel of this name. The Jews believe that this angel, Raziel, brought it to Adam in Paradise; and when Adam sinned, it supposedly flew back toward heaven, but after he had performed penance, it was given back to him. The book **Zohar** relates quite foolish fables about this. Professor Eisenmenger <note>Johann Andreas Eisenmenger (1654–1704), a German scholar whose work "Judaism Unmasked" became a famous polemic</note> is of the opinion that it had never been printed; however, one possesses several printed copies of it, and it is still
<meta>catchword: still</meta>
