It also begins a discussion on the mystical Book of Raziel, addressing its legendary origins and its printing history.</summary>

# Bibliotheca Rabbinica.

...was recently reprinted in Amsterdam in quarto [4to] <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice to form four leaves</note>. It has also been refuted by many, as can be seen in Thomas Bang’s *Heaven of the Orient* <note>original: "Thomae Bangii Coelo Orientis"</note>, page 120. Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a celebrated Hebraist</note> writes in his *Rabbinic Library* <note>original: "Bibliotheca Rabbinica"</note> on page 408: "The book is twofold, a lesser and a greater version. Both are Kabbalistic and magical." <note>original: "Liber duplex est, minor & major. Uterque Kabbalisticus & Magicus"</note> He also cites several blasphemous thoughts from it and mentions that Dr. Daniel Schwenter, a professor at Altdorf, owned both and sent him this extract.

**The Fourth of Israel** <note>original: "Rebhah Jisfraël"</note>, *The Quadrant of Israel* <note>original: "Quadrans Israëlis"</note>, based on Numbers 23:10. By Rabbi Jacob ben Machir, *concerning the quadrant of the astrolabe* <note>An instrument used by astronomers and navigators to measure the altitude of celestial bodies</note>.

**The Beginning of Wisdom** <note>original: "Reschith chochmah"</note>, *The Principle of Wisdom* <note>original: "Principium Sapientiae"</note>.
There are three books with this title:
1. ***The Beginning of Wisdom*** by Elijah de Vidas, which is called the "great" version. An abridgment <note>original: "Compendium"</note> of it was later made, which was printed in Basel in the year 1603 in octavo [8vo]. These are *Theological Commonplaces* <note>General themes or standard topics of theology</note>, which also contain many philosophical, moral, and physical matters. It was printed in Kraków in quarto, as well as in Prague in 1694 in quarto.
2. ***The Beginning of Wisdom*** by Rabbi Abraham ben Ezra; it deals with *judicial astrology* <note>The practice of using the stars to predict future events or human fate</note>.
3. ***The Beginning of Wisdom*** by Rabbi Joseph ben Falaquera, *concerning Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy*.

### Rabbinic Library.

**Reſchith Dáath**, *The Beginning of Knowledge* <note>original Latin: "Principium Scientiæ"</note>, by Rabbi Moses Albelda. These are homilies <note>sermons or moral discourses</note> on the Law <note>the Torah</note>.

**Rikmah**, or the *Book of Embroidery* <note>original Hebrew: "Sepher harikmah"</note>. An *Embroidered Work* <note>original Latin: "Opus Phrygionicum"</note> by Rabbi Jonah [ibn Janah], the first grammarian. This is a book of grammar.

**Rinnath dodim**, *The Song of Friends* <note>original Latin: "Cantio amicorum"</note>. By Elias ben Loanz. Printed in Basel in quarto. A commentary on the Song of Solomon <note>original Latin: "Canticum Canticorum"</note>.

**Rokeach**, or the *Book of the Perfumer* <note>original Hebrew: "Sepher harokeach"</note>, also called the *Book of the Spicemaker* <note>original Latin: "Liber Unguentarii, s. aromatarii"</note>, by Rabbi Eleazar [of Worms]. 
The author wrote several more books under this name because the numerical value of his name, Eleazar <note>Hebrew: אלעזר</note>, and the word *Rokeach* <note>Hebrew: רקח</note> both total 308 through the method of <term>Gematria</term> <note>a mystical Jewish system of assigning numerical values to Hebrew letters and words</note>.

**Roſch amánah**, *The Head of Faith* <note>original Latin: "Caput fidei"</note>, named from the Song of Solomon 4:8. See [Isaac] Abarbanel.

**Roſch chodeſch**, that is, *The New Moon* <note>original Latin: "Novi Lunii"</note>, by Rabbi Jacob, son of Rabbi Asher.

**Ruach chen**, *The Spirit of Grace* <note>original Latin: "Spiritus gratiæ"</note>. A work of Hebrew physics by Rabbi [Samuel] Ibn Tibbon, which Johann Isaac Levita translated into Latin and was printed in Cologne in the year 1555. Also included in the same printing is Rabbi Maimonides' Hebrew letter to the people of Marseille <note>original Latin: "ad Massaliotas"</note> concerning Astrology, which was likewise translated into Latin by the same Levita.

**Rumoth El**, or *Romemoth El*, *The High Praises of God* <note>original Latin: "Exaltationes DEI"</note>, named from Psalm 149:6. By Rabbi Moshe Alshich. Printed in Venice in quarto. A commentary on the Psalms.

### Rabbinic Library.

-> S. <-

**Sapha berurah**, meaning *Pure Language* <note>literally "Pure Lip," original Latin: "Labium purum" or "clarum"</note>.
A work on grammar by Rabbi Abraham ben Ezra. Printed in Constantinople.

**Sar Schalom**, meaning *Prince of Peace* <note>original Latin: "Princeps pacis"</note>. By Samuel Aripol, printed in Venice. An exposition on the book of Ecclesiastes.

**Schaar**, meaning *Gate*, and **Schaare**, meaning *Gates*, are the titles of many and various books, such as:
1. **Schaar haggemul**, meaning *The Gate of Retribution* <note>original Latin: "Porta retributionis"</note>. This is the 30th and final part of the book *Thorah haadam*, or *The Law of Man* <note>original Latin: "Lex hominis"</note>, by Rabbi Moses bar Nachman <note>also known as Nachmanides</note>; and because it is considered the best and most excellent part of this work, it is often cited as an independent book.
2. **Schaar haredzim**, meaning *The Gate of Secrets* <note>original Latin: "Porta Secretorum"</note>. By Rabbi Ammi. A Kabbalistic book concerning the hidden meanings of Psalm 19.
3. **Schaar haschamájim**, meaning *The Gate of Heaven* <note>original Latin: "Porta Cœli"</note>. By Rabbi Isaac Aben Latif. A philosophical book in which he disputes against Aristotle and other philosophers, and also against the <term>Rambam</term> <note>an acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, or Maimonides, the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher</note>.
4. **Schaare dimah**, meaning *The Gates of Tears* <note>original Latin: "Portæ lachrymarum"</note>, by Rabbi Moses Albelda. Venice, in quarto format.
5. **Schaare dura**, meaning *The Gates of Dueren* <note>original Latin: "Portæ Duræ"</note>, by Rabbi Isaac of Dueren. A book concerning the forbidden and permitted types of food and food vessels, which Rabbi Solomon Luria updated. Basel, in quarto format.
6. **Schaare Etz chajim**, meaning *The Gates of the Tree of Life*. By Rabbi Benjamin. Gates

This is a historical and chronological book, spanning from the beginning of the world until the times of the famed <note>original German: "ruhmsüchtigen," which can imply 'ambitious' or 'glory-seeking'</note> author. Also printed in Amsterdam, 1697, in <term>octavo</term> <note>a smaller book size made by folding a sheet into eight leaves</note>.

**Scharschoth gabhluth**, meaning *Finished Chains* <note>original Latin: "Catenae terminatae"</note>, from Exodus 28:22; interpreted as "determined roots." By Rabbi Solomon Almonti. This is a dictionary of all authors who have written about the roots of the Hebrew language; however, it only reaches the letter *Gimel* <note>the third letter of the Hebrew alphabet</note> or N. The remaining portion is considered lost.

**Scharschoth Ceſeph**, meaning *Chains of Silver* <note>original Latin: "Catenae argenti"</note>, or the "Roots of Caspi." By Rabbi Joseph Caspi, concerning the roots of the Hebrew language.

By Rabbi David Kimchi. 1545, in <term>folio</term> <note>a large book format made by folding a printed sheet once to create two leaves (four pages)</note>. This is the beautiful dictionary through which this Rabbi made himself very famous; even our own <term>philologists</term> <note>scholars of historical linguistics and literature</note> admit that they owe a great deal to this dictionary and to his grammar. See Leusden’s *Hebrew Philology* <note>original Latin: "Philolog. Hebr. mixt."</note> Dissertation XVII, p. 125; Buxtorf’s *Jewish Synagogue* <note>original Latin: "Synag. Judaic."</note> p. 413 and following; Pfeiffer’s *Sacred Criticism* <note>original Latin: "Crit. S."</note> p. 374; and Richard Simon in *Exercises on Church Discipline* <note>original Latin: "Exercitatt. de disciplina Eccles."</note> chapter XV, p. 81 and following. The Venetian edition from the year 1522, which contains the observations of Elijah <note>Elijah Levita, a famous Hebrew grammarian</note> and the Latin definitions of the words, is the best.

### Rabbinic Library.

**Schebheth Jehudah**, meaning *The Scepter of Judah* <note>original Latin: "Tribus Judæ," literally "The Tribe of Judah"</note>. Published in Krakow, 1591, and Amsterdam, 1655. This book recounts not only the sorrowful fates of the Jews, but also various customs that were observed during the time of their commonwealth <note>original German: "Republic"</note> and the Second Temple, such that many antiquities can be clarified through it. Georg Gentius published this book in Latin under the title *Jewish History* <note>original: "Historiæ Judaicæ"</note> in Amsterdam, 1651, in <term>quarto</term> <note>a book format where the paper is folded twice to create four leaves</note>.

**Schebhile emunah**, meaning *The Paths of Faith* <note>original Latin: "Semitæ fidei"</note>. A book on physics and astronomy. Published in Riva di Trento.

**Schebhile halléket**, meaning *The Paths of Gleaning* <note>original Latin: "Semitæ collectionis"</note>. Published in Venice. A legal and ritual book concerning what is permitted and forbidden.

**Scheeloth uteschubhoth**, meaning *Questions and Answers* <note>original Latin: "Quæstiones & Responsiones"</note>. Such books exist in such a great number that I cannot list them all here. <note>These are known as "Responsa," a significant genre of Rabbinic literature where scholars provide legal rulings on specific questions of Jewish law.</note>

**Schechithoth, ubhedikoth**, meaning *Slaughtering and Inspections* <note>original Latin: "Mactationes & inquisitiones"</note>. Published in Venice in <term>octavo</term> <note>a book size made by folding a sheet three times to form eight leaves</note>. This book is intended only for Jewish butchers <note>it contains the specialized laws for the ritual slaughter and physical examination of animals to ensure they are kosher</note>.

**Schekel hakkódesch**, meaning *The Holy Shekel* <note>original Latin: "Siculus sanctitatis"</note>. This is the previously cited book **Mibhchar happeninim**, or *The Choice of Pearls* <note>original Latin: "selectæ Margaritæ"</note>, which Rabbi Joseph, son of Rabbi Isaac Kimchi, translated into verse.

...ben Virga. Mantua, 1593. It is an introduction to the <term>Talmud</term><note>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah and the Gemara</note>, and it mainly explains those matters which were omitted in the *Ways of Eternity* <note>original: "Halicoth Olam"</note> by Joshua ha-Levi, and in the *Treatise* <note>original: "Cerithoth"</note> of Rabbi Samson.

**Shemen Ha-mor**, meaning *Oil of Myrrh* <note>original Latin: "Oleum myrrhæ"</note>. A Masoretic book <note>original Latin: "Liber Maforethicus"; referring to the Masorah, the tradition of the textual transmission of the Hebrew Bible</note>.

**Shemen Sason**, meaning *Oil of Gladness* <note>original Latin: "oleum Lætitiæ"</note>, by Rabbi Joseph Sason. Of a similar nature.

**Shem Ha-mephorash**, meaning *The Explicit Name of God* or *The Tetragrammaton Explained* <note>original Latin: "Nomen DEI Tetragrammaton expositum"</note>. These are Kabbalistic dreams, and the name of God—<note>Hebrew: יהוה, the four-letter name YHWH</note>—is interpreted through other names consisting of 12, 42, and 72 letters. Regarding the strange and adventurous things the Jews tell about this *Shem Ha-mephorash*, Professor Eisenmenger has cited them here and there in his laborious book, *Judaism Unmasked* <note>original: "entdeckten Judenthum"; a famous and controversial 18th-century work of Christian Hebraism</note>; see that work for more <note>original Latin: "quem omnino vide"</note>.

**Shemot Devarim**, meaning *Names of Things* <note>original Latin: "Nomina rerum"</note>, or *Hebrew Nomenclature* <note>original Latin: "Nomenclatura Hebraica"</note>, by Elijah Levita. Paulus Fagius <note>a 16th-century German Renaissance scholar of Hebrew</note> added the Latin words.

**Shefa Tal**, meaning *Abundance of Dew* <note>original Latin: "Abundantia roris"</note>, by Rabbi Sheftel Horwitz of Prague. Hanau, 1612. This is a Kabbalistic key <note>original Latin: "Clavis est Kabbalisticus"</note> to the books *Yetzirah*, *Zohar*, *Tikkunim*, and *Pardes Rimmonim*, which contain the foundation of the entire <term>Kabbalah</term><note>the ancient Jewish tradition of mystical interpretation of the Bible</note>.

<meta>catchword: Schil-</meta>

<summary>This page catalogs various Rabbinic works focusing on Talmudic methodology, Hebrew linguistics, and Kabbalistic mysticism, including the significant mystical concept of the divine "Explicit Name."</summary>
<keywords>Talmudic methodology, Shem Ha-mephorash, Tetragrammaton, Eisenmenger, Elijah Levita, Kabbalah, Zohar, Sefer Yetzirah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical catalog of Hebrew literature from the previous page, which concluded with Elijah Levita's "Shemot Devarim." This section focuses on titles beginning with the Hebrew word "Shevach" (Praise), "Shevet" (Scepter/Tribe), and "Shekel" (Coin).</meta>

### Rabbinic Library.

**Shevach Ha-nashim**, meaning *The Praise of Women* <note>original Latin: "Laus fœminarum"</note> by David Messer <note>referring to David ben Judah Messer Leon, a 15th-century Italian rabbi and physician</note>.

**Shevet Yehudah**, meaning *The Scepter of Judah* or *The Tribe of Judah* <note>original Latin: "Tribus Judæ"</note>. Printed in Krakow, 1591, and Amsterdam, 1655. This book recounts not only the sorrowful fates of the Jews, but also various customs that were observed during the time of their Republic and the Second Temple, such that many antiquities can be clarified from it. Georg Gentius published this book in Latin under the title *Jewish History* <note>original Latin: "Hiſtoriæ Judaicæ"</note> in the year 1651 at Amsterdam in quarto format.

**Shevile Emunah**, meaning *Paths of Faith* <note>original Latin: "Semitæ fidei"</note>. A book on physics and astronomy. Riva di Trento.

**Shevile Ha-leket**, meaning *Paths of the Gleaning* <note>original Latin: "Semitæ collectionis"</note>. Venice. A legal and ritual book concerning the permitted and the forbidden <note>original Latin: "de licitis & vetitis"; a common categorization for Jewish law regarding what is religiously allowed or prohibited</note>.

**She'elot u-Teshuvot**, meaning *Questions and Responses* <note>original Latin: "Quæſtiones & Reſponſiones"</note>. There are a greater number of such books than I could possibly set down here <note>these are "Responsa," a vast genre of rabbinic legal decisions written in response to specific queries</note>.

**Shechitot u-Vedikot**, meaning *Ritual Slaughterings and Inspections* <note>original Latin: "Mactationes & inquiſitiones"</note>. Venice, octavo. This is intended only for Jewish butchers <note>it contains the specialized laws for the ritual slaughter of animals</note>.

**Shekel Ha-kodesh**, meaning *The Holy Shekel* <note>original Latin: "Siclus ſanctitatis"</note>, is the previously cited book **Mivchar Ha-peninim**, or *Choice of Pearls* <note>original Latin: "ſelectæ Margaritæ"</note>, which Rabbi Joseph, son of Rabbi Isaac Kimchi, translated into verse.

**She'erit Yosef**, meaning *The Remnant of Joseph* <note>original Latin: "Reliquiæ Joſephi"</note>, by Rabbi Joseph...

...by ben Virga. Mantua, 1593. It is an introduction to the Talmud, and therein are primarily explained those matters which were omitted in the *Halikhot Olam* <note>Paths of the World</note> of Joshua ha-Levi, and in the *Sefer Keritut* <note>Book of Cut-off [Treatises]</note> of Rabbi Samson.

**Shemen Ha-mor**, meaning *Oil of Myrrh* <note>original Latin: "Oleum myrrhæ"</note>. A Masoretic book <note>referring to the Masorah, the tradition of notes on the textual details of the Hebrew Bible</note>.

**Shemen Sasson**, meaning *Oil of Joy* <note>original Latin: "oleum Lætitiæ"</note>, by Rabbi Joseph Sasson. Of a similar nature.

**Shem Ha-Mephorash**, meaning *The Explicit Name of God* or *The Tetragrammaton Explained* <note>original Latin: "Nomen DEI Tetragrammaton expoſitum"</note>. These are Kabbalistic dreams, wherein the Name of God <note>the four-letter name יהוה, or YHWH</note> is interpreted through other names consisting of 12, 42, and 72 letters. What adventurous tales the Jews tell concerning this *Shem Ha-Mephorash* has been cited here and there by Professor Eisenmenger in his laborious book, *Judaism Unmasked* <note>original German: "entdeckten Judenthum." Johann Andreas Eisenmenger was a 17th-century scholar whose work became a foundational text for later anti-Jewish polemics</note>; see him for the full account <note>original Latin: "quem omnino vide"</note>.

**Shemot Devarim**, meaning *Names of Things* <note>original Latin: "Nomina rerum"</note>, or a Hebrew Nomenclature, by Elijah Levita <note>a renowned 15th-16th century Hebrew grammarian</note>. Paulus Fagius <note>a German Renaissance scholar and Hebraist</note> added the Latin words to it.

**Shefa Tal**, meaning *Abundance of Dew* <note>original Latin: "Abundantia roris"</note>, by Rabbi Sheftel Horowitz of Prague. Hanau, 1612. It is a Kabbalistic key to the *Book of Creation* <note>original Latin: "librum Jetzirah"</note>, the *Zohar* <note>Splendor</note>, the *Tikkunim* <note>Rectifications</note>, and the *Pardes Rimmonim* <note>Orchard of Pomegranates</note>, which books contain the foundation of the entire Kabbalah.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical catalog of the "Bibliotheca Rabbinica" (Rabbinic Library), moving from entries under the letter "S" (Shin/Sin). The previous page concluded an entry on Elijah Levita's linguistic work. Here, the author examines works related to the Second Temple, magical uses of the Psalms, and liturgical poetry.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>Rabbinic Library.</header>

**Shilte ha-Gibborim**, meaning *Shields of the Mighty* <note>original Latin: "Clypei fortium"</note>, from Song of Songs 4:4. By Rabbi Abraham Portaleone of Mantua <note>original Latin: "Abraham Ariæ Mutinensis." Portaleone (1542–1612) was a physician and scholar; this work is famous for its detailed descriptions of the Jerusalem Temple</note>. This book provides a beautiful account of everything that was present in the Temple. It belongs among those Jewish books that we Christians can certainly make use of.

**Shimmush Tehillim**, meaning *The Use of the Psalms* <note>original Latin: "Usus Psalmorum"</note>. Venice, 1571. This is a truly ridiculous treatise which has been appended to the Psalms; it demonstrates how one might use the Psalms in a magical way and thereby perform quite adventurous things <note>This refers to a popular manual of "theurgic" or practical Kabbalah that prescribes specific Psalms for healing or protection</note>. In his *Rabbinic Library* <note>original Latin: "Bibliotheca Rabbinica"</note> on page 415, Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder (1564–1629), a prominent Christian Hebraist</note> cites a thought-provoking story from Luther <note>Martin Luther</note> regarding this, which one may read there at one's pleasure.

**Shir ha-Yichud**, meaning *Song of Unity* <note>original Latin: "Canticum unitatis"</note>, by Rabbi Judah the Pious of Regensburg <note>original Latin: "Jehudæ Chasid, Ratisbonensis"</note>. Freiburg im Breisgau. This is a very beautiful poem <note>original Latin: "Carmen"</note> concerning **God**, His unique essence, and the praise due to Him alone. Various authors have commented upon it, among whom the godless Rabbi Lipmann Mühlhausen <note>Yom-Tov Lipmann-Mühlhausen (14th–15th century), a defender of Judaism against Christian conversion efforts; the author's label "godless" reflects the polemical tensions of the era</note> also involved himself.

**Shochad Tov**, meaning *A Good Gift* <note>original Latin: "Munus bonum"</note>. This is a Midrash <note>an interpretive commentary</note>, or commentary; it is also called *Midrash Shmuel* <note>Midrash on the Book of Samuel</note> and *Midrash Mishle* <note>Midrash on the Book of Proverbs</note>.

**Shorashim**, <note>Roots, usually referring to a Hebrew dictionary or grammar</note> see *Shahraschim*.

**Lily of the Valleys** <note>original: "Schofchannath amákim"; Hebrew: *Shoshannat HaAmakim*</note>, from Song of Solomon 2:1. A commentary on the Song of Songs.

In this book, as many Kabbalistic <term>arcana|hidden mysteries or secrets</term> are explained as the Hebrew letters of the word **Shoshan** (שושן) represent numerically, namely 656. <note>This refers to the practice of Gematria, where the sum of the letters Shin (300) + Vav (6) + Shin (300) + Nun (50) equals 656.</note>

Lublin, 1596, folio. These are rules for the table <note>laws regarding food and dining etiquette</note>, along with a description of the great banquet prepared for the righteous in the world to come.

**The Set Table** <note>original: "Schylchan aruch"; Hebrew: *Shulchan Aruch*</note>, or *The Prepared Table*, by Rabbi Joseph Karo. It is a major <term>compendium|a concise but comprehensive summary</term> derived from the book *The Four Rows* <note>original: "Arba turim"</note>; it likewise consists of four parts with the same names. Some newer glosses and notes by Rabbi Moses Isserles have also been added, concerning the rights, customs, and traditions of those times. Amsterdam, 1661, small octavo.

**Table of the Presence** <note>original: "Schylchan happánim"; Hebrew: *Shulchan HaPanim*</note>, by Rabbi Isaac Aboab. A book of rituals.

...by **Raphael Menurtzi**. Venice, quarto. This is a book on ethics.

**The Order of the Day** <note>original: "Seder hajom"; Hebrew: *Seder Ha-Yom*</note>. The *Order of the Day*. The religious rites of Turkish Jews in their synagogues.

**The Great Order of the World** <note>original: "Seder Olam rabba"; Hebrew: *Seder Olam Rabbah*</note>. The *Greater Order of the World*, that is, the *Greater Chronicle of the World*. Whether Rabbi Yose ben Halafta <note>The text lists "R. Mose ben Chilpheta," likely a transcription error for Yose ben Halafta, the traditionally attributed author</note> is truly the author remains uncertain.

**The Lesser Order of the World** <note>original: "Seder olam dzuta"; Hebrew: *Seder Olam Zuta*</note>. The *Order*, or the *Lesser Chronicle of the World*. The author is also uncertain. It was printed as a supplement to the previous work, Basel, 1580.

**The Treasure of Kings** <note>original: "Segyllath melachim"; Hebrew: *Segullat Melakhim*</note>. The *Special Property of Kings*, a title taken from Ecclesiastes 2:8. A work on grammar by Mesir David <note>David ben Solomon ibn Yahya, a 15th-century grammarian</note>.

**Prayers for Forgiveness** <note>original: "Selichoth"; Hebrew: *Selichot*</note>. This is a very well-known prayer book that has been printed in all sorts of formats. ...obtain mercy and forgiveness for their sins. **Buxtorf** <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a prominent Christian Hebraist and professor at Basel</note> writes that he read such a **manuscript** <note>original: "MSSt."</note> of it, in which very bitter words against **Christ** and the **Christians** were found, which were omitted from the printed copies. **Semak** סמ"ק, that is, *Sepher mitzvoth katon*. The **Small Book of Precepts** <note>original Latin: "Liber præceptorum parvus"</note>. See also **Pillars of Exile** <note>original: "Ammúde gólah"; a reference to the same work by Isaac ben Joseph of Corbeil</note>.

The *Masorah* <term>Masorah|the tradition of notes and marginalia used to preserve the exact text of the Hebrew Bible</term> of Rabbi Meir Hallevi.

**The Support of the Elders** <note>original: "Semichath Dzekenim"; Hebrew: *Semikhat Zekenim*</note>, the **Support of the Elders** <note>original Latin: "Fulcimentum Senum"</note>. A debate between two Rabbis concerning certain **ritual precepts**. Venice.

**Sepher**, **Book** <note>original Latin: "Liber"</note>, is the most common title that the Jews place at the beginning of their books; therefore, I will not cite any here <note>The author omits works starting with "Sepher" (Book of...) to avoid an impossibly long list, as "Sepher" is a generic prefix for most titles</note>.

**The Prayer of Isaac** <note>original: "Siach Jitzchak"; Hebrew: *Siach Yitzchak*</note>, the **Prayer of Isaac** <note>original Latin: "Oratio Isaaci"</note>, taken from Genesis 24:63. By Rabbi Isaac. Prague, quarto.

**The Journey of the World** <note>original: "Sebubh haólam"; Hebrew: *Sivuv ha-Olam*</note>, the **Circuit of the World** <note>original Latin: "Circuitus Mundi"</note>. Prague, quarto. In Hebrew and German. It is the **travelogue** <note>original Latin: "Itinerarium"</note> of **Petachiah of Ratisbon** <note>A 12th-century Jewish traveler from Regensburg who journeyed through Eastern Europe and the Middle East</note>.

**Siphra, Siphre**, **Commentary, Commentaries** <note>original Latin: "Commentarius, Commentarii"</note>. These are two ancient books for which the **Gloss** <note>Explanatory notes or commentary</note> of **Rabbi Solomon** <note>Refers to Rashi, the most influential medieval commentator on the Torah and Talmud</note> is well known. **Siphra and Siphre are allegorical commentaries on the book of Leviticus** <note>original Latin: "Siphra & Siphre sunt Commentationes allegoricæ libri Levitici"; though Siphre also covers Numbers and Deuteronomy</note>. The author of *Siphre* is established as **Rabbi Shimon ben Yochai**, as noted in the **Book of Lineage** <note>original: "Juchaſin"; Hebrew: *Sefer Ha-Yuchasin*</note>.

### Page 162

On folio 36 it is written: When one speaks of **Siphra** <note>Hebrew: *Sifra*, meaning "The Book"</note>, also known as **The Law of the Priests** <note>original Latin: "Thorath cohanim"</note>, it is understood to refer to **Rabbi Judah**. When one speaks of **Siphre** <note>Hebrew: *Sifrei*, meaning "The Books"</note>, it is understood to refer to **Rabbi Simeon ben Jochai**. Others, in turn, name different authors; however that may be, it is quite certain that the authors of these works were disciples of **Rabbi Judah the Holy** <note>original: "R. Judae Sancti"; also known as Judah ha-Nasi</note>, who compiled the **Mishnah** <term>Mishnah|The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions, forming the core of the Talmud</term>. See **Buxtorf’s** *Rabbinic Library*, page 389 and following.

**The Lips of the Priest** <note>Hebrew: *Siphte cohen*; original Latin: "Labia sacerdotis"</note>, from Malachi 2:7. It contains **Midrashim** <term>Midrashim|Ancient rabbinic commentaries on the Hebrew Scriptures that explain or expand upon biblical stories</term> on the Law.

**The Lips of the Sleeping** <note>Hebrew: *Siphthe Jeschenim*; original Latin: "Labia Dormientium"</note>, by **Rabbi Shabtai ben Joseph**. Amsterdam, 1670. This is a truly excellent **Rabbinic Library** <note>Here referring to a bibliography or catalog of Jewish books</note>.

**The Secret of the Lord** <note>Hebrew: *Sod Adonai*; original Latin: "secretum Domini"</note>, by **Rabbi Bechaj** <note>Bahya ben Asher</note>. A **Kabbalistic** <term>Kabbalah|A school of thought in Jewish mysticism dealing with the nature of the divine</term> book.

**The Secret of the Chariot** <note>Hebrew: *Sod hammercábhah*; original Latin: "secretum quadrigæ"</note>. This is a **Kabbalistic** explanation of **Ezekiel’s Chariot** <note>Referring to the mystical vision of the divine throne-chariot in the first chapter of the Book of Ezekiel</note>, wherein many alleged secrets concerning **Paradise** and **Hell** are also to be found.

**The Secret of Secrets** <note>Hebrew: *Sod hassodoth*; original Latin: "Arcanum Arcanorum"</note>, or *The Secret of Counsels*. It is said that **Aristotle** sent this book, which deals with counsels and counselors, to **Alexander the Great**, and it is said to have been written in **Greek**, from which it was later translated into **Arabic**, and finally into **Hebrew**.

**The Secret of the Law** <note>Hebrew: *Sod hattorah*; original Latin: "secretum Legis"</note>, by **Rabbi Abraham** <note>Likely Abraham ibn Ezra</note>.

# Rabbinical Library

**Thehillim**, also written **Thillim**, meaning the **Psalms**. <note>original: "Psalmi"</note> These are printed in various different editions.

**Thehillah le David**, or the **Praise of David**. <note>original Latin: "Laus Davidis"</note>

**Themunah**, or **Sepher Themunah**, meaning the **Book of the Image** or **Likeness**. <note>original Latin: "Liber imaginis, l. similitudinis"</note> This is a Kabbalistic <note>Kabbalah refers to the tradition of Jewish mysticism</note> book that treats the **mystical shapes of the letters**. <note>original Latin: "de figura literarum"</note>

**Thephillot mikol hashanah**, the **Book of Prayers for the Entire Year**, <note>original Latin: "Liber precum totius anni"</note> also called **Seder Tephilloth**, the **Order of Prayers**. <note>original Latin: "Ordo precum"</note> This is the title given to the Jewish prayer books which they use daily, as well as on the Sabbath and other minor festivals. For on the three primary festivals—which they call **Shalosh Regalim**, meaning the **Three Pilgrimages** <note>original Latin/German: "Tres vices" or "coitiones." These are the major biblical festivals of Passover, Shavuot, and Sukkot</note>—they also use the **Machzor** <note>a specialized prayer book for festivals</note> and the **Selichoth** <note>a collection of penitential prayers</note> in addition to these. Printed in Frankfurt am Main, 1688, in quarto format. <note>Quarto refers to the book size, where each sheet of paper is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>

**Thena debe Eliahu**, or the **Tradition of the House of Elijah**. <note>original Latin: "Traditio domus Eliæ"</note> Venice, quarto format. **Note:** This tradition from the House of Elijah regarding the world enduring for six thousand years is recorded in both the **Mishnah** and the **Gemara** <note>The Mishnah and Gemara are the two core components of the Talmud, the central text of Rabbinic Judaism</note> with these words:

> "It is a tradition of the House of Elijah that the world shall exist for six thousand years. For two thousand years it was desolation <note>original Latin: "inane"; this refers to the era before the revelation of the Torah</note>. For two thousand years the Law <note>The Torah</note> shall flourish. For two thousand years the Messiah shall hold sway. But because of our sins, which have multiplied, many of those years intended for the Messiah have already passed away."

You may see more regarding this tradition in Dr. Fabricius's *Codex Pseudepigraphus of the Old Testament*, page 1079 and following. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a famous German scholar who compiled ancient texts that were not included in the official Bible.</note>

**Tholedoth Aharon**, the **Generations of Aaron**, <note>original Latin: "Generationes Aharonis"</note> titled from Numbers 3:1. 1.  **Torah Or**, *Law is Light* <note>original Latin: Lex Lux; from Proverbs 6:23</note>. By **Don Joseph of Spain, son of David**. This has been printed in many places. One can see from this how much these people long for a better state in the next world; yet they have a very wretched, indeed quite foolish, conception of it.

3.  **Torath Adonai**, *Law of the Lord* <note>original Latin: Lex Domini</note>. By **Rabbi Joseph Caspi**. An explanation of the Pentateuch <term>Pentateuch|The first five books of the Hebrew Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy</term>.

Published in Constantinople.
5.  **Torath Cohanim**, *Law of the Priests* <note>original Latin: Lex sacerdotum</note>. See *Siphra* <term>Siphra|An ancient legal commentary (Midrash) on the book of Leviticus, often called the 'Law of the Priests'</term>.

# Rabbinic Library

2 Samuel 7:19. **Rabbi Moses bar Nachman** <note>Also known as Nahmanides (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar and mystic</note>. This is quite a famous book, divided into 30 chapters, or "gates" <term>gates|From the Hebrew 'sha'ar,' a common term for chapters in medieval Jewish literature</term>. The majority of the content, however, concerns how Jews are to behave during illness, death, and mourning <note>This work is titled *Torat Ha-Adam* (The Law of Man)</note>.

**7. Thorath habbajith**, *Law of the House* <note>original Latin: Lex Domus</note>, from Ezekiel 43:12. **Rashba** <term>Rashba|An acronym for Rabbi Solomon ben Abraham ibn Adret (c. 1235–1310), a preeminent Spanish rabbi and legal authority</term>. These are Talmudic legal decisions. It is a double-work: one bears the subtitle *Aruch*, which means extensive; the other *Kazar*, which means short. The former was printed with a double commentary in Venice in the year 1608; the latter was printed in Cremona in quarto <term>quarto|A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</term>.

**8. Thorath hachattath**, *Law of Sin* <note>original Latin: Lex peccati</note>, from Leviticus 6. **Rabbi Moses Isserles** <note>A famous Polish rabbi (1520–1572) known for his legal rulings</note>. Cracow, 1577, in quarto. This is a Jewish legal book that deals specifically with permitted and forbidden foods <term>permitted and forbidden foods|Referring to the laws of Kashrut (kosher dietary laws)</term>; thus it concerns us as little as the previous one <note>A common sentiment in 18th-century Christian scholarship, viewing Jewish ritual law as obsolete for Christians</note>.

**9. Thorath haolah**, *Law of the Burnt Offering* <note>original Latin: Lex holocausti</note>, from Leviticus 6:2. **Rabbi Moses Isserles**. A Kabbalistic-philosophical book that is of little use. Prague, 1570.

**10. Thorath Moscheh**, *Law of Moses* <note>original Latin: Lex Mosis</note>. **Rabbi Moshe Alshech**. Venice, 1605, in folio <term>folio|A large book size where each sheet is folded once</term>. A commentary on the Law <note>The Pentateuch or first five books of the Bible</note>.

**11. Thorath Moscheh**. **Rabbi Moshe Almosnino**.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Rabbinic literature, specifically moving from the titles beginning with "Torah" to those relating to "Traditions" (Tosefta/Baraita) and then into titles starting with the Hebrew letter Tav, such as "Totzeot" and "Terumah."</meta>

# Rabbinic Library

...traditions, namely Talmudic ones <note>original Latin: "ditiones, sc. Thalmudicæ." This is the conclusion of the word "Traditiones" from the previous page</note>. Thus Rabbi Hiyya, first among all Jewish teachers, named his explanations of the Mishnah <term>Mishnah|The primary written collection of Jewish oral traditions, forming the core of the Talmud</term> by this name. For he and Rabbi Oshaia were the first to publicly explain this book in the schools. What the subsequent rabbis commented upon it was called *Baraita* <term>Baraita|Aramaic for "external" teachings; refers to Jewish oral laws not included in the Mishnah</term>. (Regarding this word *Baraita*, one can find further information in Mr. von Bashuysen's *The Greatest Talmudic Key* <note>original Latin: "Clavi Thalmudica maxima," a work by Heinrich Jacob van Bashuysen</note>, "which most elegant book should certainly be consulted" <note>original Latin: "quem elegantissimum librum omnino vide"</note>). After the conclusion of the Talmud, since for nearly 500 years their studies lay entirely dormant—partly through persecutions and partly through the ambition of their rabbis who had fallen out with one another—finally, around the time of Rabbi Solomon <note>Refers to Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent commentator on the Bible and Talmud</note>, others were found who also made additions to the Talmud. These the rabbis likewise call *Tosafot* <term>Tosafot|Meaning "additions"; these are medieval commentaries that appear on the outer margin of most Talmud editions</term>, and they constitute the other side of the Talmudic text. Among these, the *Tosafot* of Rabbi Asher <note>Asher ben Jehiel (c. 1250–1327), a major German-Spanish legal authority</note> and Rabbi Joseph <note>Likely Rabbi Joseph Colon (c. 1420–1480)</note> are particularly famous.

**Totzeot Chayim** <note>original Latin: "Egressus Vitæ" (The Outgoings of Life)</note>, from Proverbs 4:23. This is a very small compendium <term>compendium|A concise summary or abridgment</term> of the book *Reshit Chochmah* <note>"The Beginning of Wisdom," a famous 16th-century kabbalistic and ethical work</note>. Published in Krakow in octavo <term>octavo|A book size where each sheet is folded to create eight leaves</term>.

**Terumah** <note>original Latin: "Ablatio" (The Offering/Contribution)</note>. Rabbi Baruch, son of Rabbi Isaac <note>Baruch ben Isaac of Worms (12th–13th century), a French-German Tosafist</note>. Venice, 1528. This is a book of ritual and judicial laws <note>original Latin: "Liber ritualis & judicialis est"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic catalog of Rabbinic literature, specifically focusing on titles beginning with the Hebrew letter Tav (transliterated here as 'Th'), such as Terumot and Teshuvot, followed by titles beginning with Tet (transliterated as 'T'), such as Tobiah and Tov Shem.</meta>

# Rabbinic Library

**Terumat Ha-Deshen** <note>original: "Thrumath haddeschen," meaning "The Contribution of the Ashes," a reference to Leviticus 6:9</note>, or *The Removal of the Ashes* <note>original Latin: "Ablatio cineris"</note>, by Rabbi Israel of Germany <note>Israel Isserlein (1390–1460), the most influential rabbi of 15th-century Germany</note>. Venice, 1546. This consists of **Questions and Answers on the Law of Moses**.

**Terumot**, or the *Sefer ha-Terumot* <note>The Book of Contributions or Offerings</note>, or the *Book of Oblations* <note>original Latin: "Liber Oblationum"</note>. Prague, 1605, in folio format. A legal book.

**Teshuvot** <note>original: "Thschubhoth," meaning "Answers" or "Responsa"</note>. Very many such books are to be found again. I wish here, however, to mention only the one published in 1707 at Amsterdam in quarto under the title <term>Teshuvot ha-Geonim|Responsa of the Excellencies; "Geonim" refers to the heads of the influential Babylonian academies who were the supreme authorities on Jewish law in the early Middle Ages</term>, from which one may see how the letters of modern-day Rabbis are written "simply and plainly" <note>original Latin: "de simplici & plano"</note>, and how these people often "quarrel with one another over trifles" <note>original Latin: "de lana caprina," literally "over goat's wool"</note>. This treatise is reviewed in the *Innocent News* <note>original German: "unschuldigen Nachrichten," a popular 18th-century Lutheran periodical</note> from the year 1714, page 617 and following.

**Tummat Yesharim** <note>original: "Thymmath Jeschárim"</note>, *The Integrity of the Upright*, from Proverbs 11:3. By Rabbenu Jacob Yahya. Venice. A book of rituals. **NB.** The Jews commonly call this author the same way that Jacob was called in the scriptures: *Ish Tam*, "A man of integrity" <note>original Latin: "Virum integrum"</note>, or **Rabbenu Tam** <note>Jacob ben Meir (c. 1100–1171), a famous French Tosafist and grandson of Rashi</note>.

**Tobiah**. *Tobit*. The History of Tobit in Hebrew with a Latin version and notes by Sebastian Münster <note>A famous Christian Hebraist and scholar (1488–1552)</note>, Basel.

**Tov Shem** <note>original: "Tobh Schem"</note>, *A Good Name*, from Ecclesiastes 7:3 <note>The verse "A good name is better than precious ointment" is actually Ecclesiastes 7:1</note>. By Rabbi Eliakim, son of Rabbi Naphtali. Venice.

# Rabbinic Library

Venice, 1607, in quarto format. In the Jewish manner, it is quite a good book, for it shows how one, through diligent contemplation of the Word of God and of one's death, as well as the practice of good works, may acquire a good name here and become an heir of eternal life there. But it is just like that young man in Matthew 19, who also wanted to inherit eternal life through "doing." <note>A reference to the "Rich Young Ruler" who asks Jesus what good deed he must do to have eternal life; the author implies the book focuses on works-based righteousness.</note>

**Tubh Ta’am** <note>original Hebrew: "Tubh táam," meaning "Goodness of Taste" or "Good Judgment"</note>, *Goodness of Sense* <note>original Latin: "Bonitas Sensus"</note>, by Elias Levita, the German <note>Elia Levita (1469–1549) was a renowned Hebrew grammarian and teacher to many Christian scholars</note>, with the Latin version by Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a celebrated Christian Hebraist from Basel</note>. It is a very useful book and deals with <term>Hebrew Accents|The system of cantillation marks used in the Hebrew Bible to indicate musical chanting and grammatical structure</term>, and specifically with the entire nature, property, sequence, and use of the Hebrew accents.

**Tumah ve-Tohorah** <note>original Hebrew: "Tumah vetohorah"</note>, *Impurity and Purity* <note>original Latin: "Immunditia & Munditia"</note>. By Rabbi Saadia Gaon <note>Saadia ben Joseph (882–942), a foundational figure in medieval Jewish philosophy and rabbinic literature</note>.

**Tur Aben Ezra** <note>original Hebrew: "Tur," meaning "Row," "Rank," or "Order"</note>, *Order*, or *Ordination*, or *Arrangement* <note>original Latin: "Ordo, seu Ordinatio, vel dispositio"</note>. By Rabbi Ibn Ezra <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167), a polymath known for his biblical commentary and scientific works</note>. A legal book, and deals in particular with marriage, weddings, dowries, illnesses, and the ritual purification of women.

->V.<-

**Vaad la-Chakhamim** <note>original Hebrew: "Vaad lachachamim"</note>, *Assembly of the Wise* <note>original Latin: "Conventus Sapientum"</note>. Sermons <note>original Latin: "Conciones"</note> by Rabbi Samuel Aripol. The Jews generally think nothing of this author at all; indeed, it seems as if fools, rather than the wise, had gathered with him. For everything he prattles on about is merely fabulous chatter.

# Rabbinical Library

### Page 174

**Vajakhel Moshe** <note>original: "Vajakhel Moſche"</note>, *And Moses Assembled*, from Exodus 35:1. A book of sermons in quarto, published in Venice.

->Z.<-

**Zachuth** <note>original: "Zachuth"</note>, see the *Book of Merits* <note>original: "Sepher hazachuth, Liber meritorum"</note> by Rabbi Moshe bar Nachman <note>Also known as Nachmanides, a major medieval Sephardic scholar and philosopher</note>.

**Zachuth Adam**, *The Merit of Adam*, or *The Justice and Innocence of Adam*. The unknown and impious author of this work excuses and even completely denies that Adam ever committed a sin.

**Zachuth**, see the *Book of Purity* <note>original: "Sepher Zachuth"</note>, spelled with the letter Tsadi [צ]. A *Book of Elegances* by Rabbi Abraham, son of Rabbi Meir the Spaniard, known as Ibn Ezra. Published in Venice, octavo, 1546. A grammatical book concerning the proper formation of speech.

**Zach Sephathajim**, *Splendor of the Lips*.

**Zevach Pesach** <note>original: "Zebhach peſach"</note>, *The Passover Sacrifice*, by Don Isaac Abarbanel. Published in Cremona, quarto. A beautiful commentary on the Haggadah <note>original: "super Haggadam"</note>, providing a historical reading for the festival of Passover.

**Tzedah La-Derech** <note>original: "Zedah laddérech"</note>, *Provision for the Way*, by Rabbi Menachem ibn Zerach of France. It contains legal, ceremonial, and even medicinal matters.

**Tzedek Olamim** <note>original: "Zedek Olámim"</note>, *Eternal Justice*, by Abarbanel. This work has been lost.

**Tze’enah U-Re’enah** <note>original: "Zeénah ureénah"</note>, or according to the Jewish pronunciation, *Tzennorennah*, meaning *Go Forth and See*, from Song of Solomon 3:11. It is a Hebrew-German <note>A reference to the Yiddish language, commonly used for communal study</note> commentary on the Law <term>The Torah: the first five books of the Hebrew Bible</term>. Published in Kraków, folio.

# Rabbinical Library

### Page 175

This is a chronological and historical book, covering the period from the beginning of the world until the year of Christ 1592.

**Tzemach David** <note>original: "Zemach David"</note>, a Hebrew Lexicon <note>A dictionary or encyclopedia of words</note> by David de Pomis. Published in Venice, 1587, in folio. By Rabbi Jacob ben Meir from the province of Guyenne <note>original: "גיאן", likely referring to the region in southwestern France</note>, son of the elder Rabbi Nissim. A Kabbalistic <note>Refers to Jewish mystical traditions</note> book.

**Tzefune Recanati** <note>original: "Zephune Rakanaſi"</note>, *The Hidden Secrets of Recanati* <note>original Latin: "Recondita Rakanati"</note>. This is a Kabbalistic work.

**Tzeri Ha-Yagon** <note>original: "Zeri hajagon"</note>, *The Balm of Sorrow* <note>original Latin: "Opobalsamum lassitudinis"</note>. Published in Cremona, in quarto. A small book of consolation for times of tribulation and miserable, troubled days.

**Tzeror Ha-Chayim** <note>original: "Zeror hachajim"</note>, *The Bundle of Life* <note>original Latin: "Fasciculus Vitae"</note>, by Rabbi Chayim.

**Tzeror Ha-Keseph** <note>original: "Zeror hakkeſeph"</note>, *The Bundle of Silver* <note>original Latin: "Fasciculus argenti"</note>, by the same author. Rabbi Joseph Caspi also wrote a work on logic under this same title.

C." for "Anno Christi"</note> 1248, still knew nothing of this book. One may, however, note the following 3 points concerning it:

1. That in many instances it *contradicts the Gemara* <note>the component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis and commentary</note>, even though it never mentions the same.

2. That its *constitutions* <note>laws or ordinances</note> regarding those things which are not explained in the *Gemara* are held in just as high regard as the *Gemara* itself.

3. That in the oldest *manuscripts* <note>original Latin abbreviation: "MSSt."</note> of this book, many things are found that favor Christianity, which the Jews, however, scratch out in their copies: as the learned **Legerus** testifies to having seen with his own eyes in various *copies* <note>original Latin: "Exemplaribus"</note> belonging to **Rabbi Leonis Sia**, who also later became a Christian for that reason.

# Rabbinical Library.

...[kabba]listic commentary on the Five Books of Moses, and it has been printed in various places in different ways. The Cremona edition of the year 1559 in folio <note>a large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once</note> contains *Concordances of passages from Holy Scripture*. The Mantua edition of 1558 in quarto <note>a medium book size made by folding a sheet twice</note>, however, does not have these. Some things are also arranged differently in it than in the former, which is why the Jews prefer the former [Cremona] far above the latter.

**Zohar Chadash** <term>Zohar chadasch: New Zohar</term>. This is the same book as before, only that it adds those matters which do not appear in the former, taken from the very oldest parchment manuscript <note>original Latin abbreviation: "MSSt."</note> that originates from the Land of Israel. Now, if one could trust this *codex* <note>a manuscript in book form</note>, one would have an excellent proof, particularly *regarding the vowel points and accents* <note>the marks added to Hebrew letters to indicate vowels and musical chanting</note>, that they were of equal age with the letters. For the following sayings are found therein regarding this:

> Folio 10, column 2. *The points and vowels are the tradition received by Moses from Mount Sinai.* <note>original Latin: "Puncta & vocales acceptio sunt Mosis de monte Sinai."</note>
>
> Folio 11. *The movements of the accents were all established by the hand of Moses.* <note>original Latin: "Motiones accentuum firmatae sunt omnes per manus Mosis."</note>
>
> Folio 26, column 2. *Words without points are like a woman without a garment, who cannot go out into a public place.* <note>original Latin: "Dictiones absque punctis sunt sicut mulier absque vestimento, quae non potest prodire in locum publicum."</note>
>
> Folio 28, column 2. *There is no substance or light in any letters without the light of the points, and the whole*

# Bibliotheca Rabbinica.

...then that which was handed down through the secret of the Law at Sinai.

**Zohar harakia**, *Splendor of the Firmament* <note>original Latin: "Splendor expansi"</note>, Constantinople, in quarto <note>a medium book size made by folding a sheet of paper twice</note>.

**Zophnath-Paaneach**, *Revealer of Secrets* <note>original Latin: "Revelator arcanorum"</note>, based on Genesis 41:45 <note>the name given to Joseph by Pharaoh</note>. By Rabbi Shem Tov Shprot. An explanation of the more difficult passages in Abraham ibn Ezra’s commentary on the Law <note>the Torah</note>. There is also another book of this name available by Rabbi Onkenira, which shows how cattle are to be properly slaughtered.

**Zori haggupħ**, *Medicine for the Body* <note>original Latin: "Theriaca Corporis"</note>. By Rabbi Nathan Palakeira. A medical book.

**Zori hannephesch**, by Rabbi Joseph Noubner, *Balsam of the Soul* <note>original Latin: "Balsamum animæ"</note>.

**Zurath beth hammikdasch**, the figure or form of the House of the Sanctuary <note>original Latin: "figura, l. forma Domus sanctuarii"</note>, namely the third [Temple] which is described by the Prophet Ezekiel. Prague, in quarto.

**Zurath haáretz**, *Figure of the Earth* <note>original Latin: "Figura terræ"</note>, by Rabbi Abraham, son of Rabbi Hiyya the Spaniard. *The Sphere of the World* <note>original Latin: "Sphæra Mundi"</note>, describing the shape of the earth, the arrangement of the heavenly spheres, and the movement of the stars. Oswald Schreckenfuchs translated it into Latin, and Sebastian Münster illustrated it with notes. Basel, 1546.

There is also a Geography by Rabbi Abraham bar Hiyya available under this name.

**Zurath haólam**, *Figure of the World* <note>original Latin: "figura mundi"</note>, by Rabbi Isaac ben Lataph, who was a fine philosopher. This is a book of astronomy.

->Books of Converts from Judaism.<- <note>original Latin: Libri Conversorum ex Judaismo</note>

## Books,
### Which some converted Jews have written.

**Antonius Margarita's** *Whole Jewish Faith* <note>original German: gantzer Jüdischer Glaube</note>. Frankfurt am Main. 1689. octavo <note>a book size of approximately 6 by 9 inches</note>.

**Dietrich Schwaben's** *Jewish Cloak* <note>original German: Jüdischer Deckmantel; likely referring to a "cover-up" or "pretense"</note>. Cologne. 1616. quarto <note>a medium-sized book, roughly 9 by 12 inches</note>.

**Ferdinand Hesse's** *The Jewish Whip* <note>original Latin: Flagellum Judaicum</note>, or *Jewish Scourge*. Strasbourg. 1601.

**Friedrich Samuel Brentz's**, *The Shed Jewish Snake-Skin* <note>original German: Jüdischer abgestreiffter Schlangen-Balg; a polemical title suggesting the casting off of an old identity</note>. Nuremberg, alongside Mr. **Wülffer's** annotations on the *Jewish Theriac* <note>a reference to a medicinal antidote or "treacle," often used in titles regarding the "cure" for religious error</note>. 1680. quarto.

**Hieronymus de Santa Fe's** *Two Books Against the Jews* <note>original Latin: libri duo contra Judæos</note>. Zurich. 1552. octavo.

**Joannes Adrianus** of Emden's, *Epistle and Warning Letter to all Obstinate and Stubborn Jews*. Wittenberg. 1610. quarto.

**Jo. Philipp Bleibtreu's** *Enlightened Meir* <note>Meir is a Hebrew name meaning "One who shines" or "Enlightener"</note>. Frankfurt am Main. 1687. duodecimo <note>a small pocket-sized book</note>.

**Paul Christiani's** *Meditations* <note>original Latin: Meditationes</note>, or *Sincere Thoughts and Judgment on Johann Andreas Eisenmenger's "Judaism Unmasked"*; together with a letter to his brothers according to the flesh, to the Hebrews, in the Hebrew-German language. Halle. 1711. quarto.

*Method for Conversing with Jews.* <note>original Latin: Methodus colloquendi cum Judæis</note>

...wishes to engage with them in a religious dialogue, so that they, through the grace of God, might be brought—if not to the acceptance of, then at least to a conviction of the Christian truth.

->Here, I believe, a Christian must<-
->observe:<-

I. What concerns himself. And there, he will surely need to:
$\alpha$. **Prepare** himself with devout prayer, because for these poor people, by the righteous judgment of God, **the veil** <note>original Greek: τὸ κάλυμμα</note>—the veil of Moses over the Old Testament—remains unlifted when they read it, though it is done away with in **Christ**. 2 Corinthians 3:14. Therefore, Christ himself prayed for them, Luke 23:34, whom Paul also followed, pleading with God for Israel that they might be saved, Romans 10:1; indeed, in an **ecstasy of love** <note>original Latin: ecstasi amoris</note>, he even wished to be accursed and separated from Christ for the sake of his brothers. Romans 9:3.
$\beta$. Lead a pious and godly life, so that God may hear his prayer and the Jews can find nothing to reproach him for.
$\gamma$. Understand the Hebrew and Rabbinic languages well, along with everything belonging to them; also to truly know the **spirit** <note>original Latin: genium; referring here to the cultural character or mindset</note> of the Jews and to know how to navigate their **Talmud** <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing legal and folkloric traditions</note> properly. *See van Bashuysen’s Great Key to the Talmud.* <note>original Latin: Bashuysen Clav. Thalmud. maximam</note> Although the Lord God is not bound to these human means, and also certainly

C. The **Talmud**.
D. The Rabbinic **Commentaries**, especially those of **Kimchi** <note>David Kimchi, a medieval grammarian and biblical commentator</note>, **Abarbinel** <note>Isaac Abarbinel, a statesman and philosopher</note>, **Jarchi** <note>A common early modern name for Rashi, the most influential medieval commentator</note>, **Aben Ezra**, etc., of which they make such a great deal that they even set **Moses** and the Prophets aside in comparison. *See Johannes Buxtorf’s Jewish Synagogue, Chapter 3, page 69. # Method of Dialoguing
<note>original Latin header: Methodus colloquendi</note>

Exercises on Daniel, Chapter IX, Preface, page 36 <note>Referring to a theological commentary on the prophecy of the seventy weeks in the Book of Daniel</note> notes that the Jews often encountered him with these words: "Do you then read Rabbi Solomon <note>original Latin: R. Salomonem; referring to Rashi (Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki), the preeminent medieval commentator</note> and yet believe in Jesus the Nazarene?"

III. Regarding the **subject matter** <note>original Latin: Materiam</note> with which one should begin to speak with them. On this, there are differing opinions.

a. The late Dr. **Luther** urges in his letter against the **Sabbatarians** <note>original German: Sabbathen; a group that adopted Jewish Sabbath practices</note>, *Volume 6 of the Jena Edition, folio 31 and following*, that one should make a beginning with their **miserable** <note>original Latin-German: miserablen</note> condition—specifically, that they are scattered here and there throughout the world.

Now, it is indeed true that the poor and suffering among the Jews may well take such things to heart; however, because they still constantly flatter themselves with the supposedly imminent arrival of their worldly **Messiah** <note>Many Jewish expectations of the time focused on a political liberator who would restore the kingdom of Israel</note>, they do not accept this objection. Meanwhile, the wealthy—those of such great fortune that they can live like princes—only laugh at us for it. Indeed, **Ernestus Ferdinandus** testifies in his *Scourge of the Jews* <note>original German: Juden Geissel; a common title for polemical works of this era</note>, chapter V, that they actually consider it a certain sign of their true religion—and proof that the **Messiah** has not yet come—because they obtain so much freedom from the Christian authorities. From this, one sees that they view their present condition with entirely different eyes than the late **Luther** could have imagined.

b. **Michael Havemann** proceeds in his *Jewish...* <note>Text cuts off; likely referring to his work "Jura Judaeorum"</note>

[...that] God, because of their supposed good works, would no longer remember their sins.

A little later: "That the righteous are afflicted, that they cannot redeem their relatives by their merits; and why? Rabbi Abarbinel, in the place cited above on Isaiah 3, page 7: "In this our captivity, the merits of the Fathers cease, as Rabbi Moses Nachmanides <note>A major medieval Jewish scholar, also known as Ramban (1194–1270)</note> writes: nor can we be liberated from the nations, except for the sake of the great NAME OF GOD, which has been profaned among the nations." <note>original Latin: *in hac captivitate nostra cessant merita Patrum, uti scribit R. In this way, Israel were made servants in Egypt, and Moses and Aaron stood and redeemed them; again they were led back into servitude in the days of Sisera, and Barak and Deborah redeemed them. Then from the Midianites by Gideon; and likewise by the hands of Shamgar; and so through the hands of each of the Judges, they stood and redeemed them. Again they became servants in Babylon, and again they were redeemed through the hands of Ananias, Mishael, and Azariah <note>These are the Hebrew names of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego from the Book of Daniel.</note>: and again they were led back into servitude by the hand of Edom, that is, by the Romans. <note>In Rabbinic literature, "Edom" is frequently used as a coded term for the Roman Empire and, later, the Christian world.</note> Israel therefore said: "Behold, we are weary of subjugations and redemptions; we are no longer seeking that flesh and blood should shine upon us, but **GOD** himself," as it is said in Psalm 118:27: **THE LORD IS GOD**, and he has shone upon us; and it is said in Isaiah 45:17: "Israel shall be saved in the Lord with an everlasting salvation." <note>original Latin: "Israël Salvatus erit in Domino, Salvatione seculorum."</note>

Rabbi Abarbinel <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman, philosopher, and Bible commentator.</note> explicitly writes concerning Jeremiah 23:6 that the name Yahweh <note>original Hebrew: יהוה, the Tetragrammaton or the four-letter sacred name of God.</note> is given to no one but **God**. These are his words:

> Nor is any other creature in the world called by this name: for this is indeed the glorious name, this is called the separate name, because it is communicated to no one except **GOD**. <note>original Latin: "Neque ulla alia creatura in mundo hoc nomine appellatur..."</note>

But now Rabbi Kimchi <note>David Kimhi (1160–1235), also known as Radak, a primary medieval Jewish commentator and grammarian.</note> attributes this name to the Messiah, when he writes: Israel

# Method on Matters of Faith #

Just as God expressly forbade that any illegitimate child <note>German: *Huren-Kind*. In this context, it refers to a *Mamzer*, a person born from a forbidden relationship such as adultery or incest, who faces specific legal restrictions in Jewish law.</note> should enter the congregation of the Lord, as seen in Deuteronomy 23:2; regarding this, one should take note of these two passages.

**Sanhedrin, folio 107, column 2.** <note>A tractate of the Talmud dealing with judicial procedures. The passage cited here is a famous and controversial account traditionally associated with Jesus in some manuscripts.</note> When King Jannai <note>Alexander Jannaeus, a Hasmonean King of Judea (103–76 BCE).</note> killed the Rabbis, Rabbi Joshua ben Perachiah went with Jesus to Alexandria in Egypt. But when there was peace, Shimon ben Shetach <note>A Pharisaic scholar and brother of Queen Salome Alexandra.</note> sent him a letter with this content: "From me, Jerusalem, the Holy City, to you, Alexandria in Egypt, O my sister! My husband dwells in you, while I sit here desolate."

In the tractate **Avot de-Rabbi Nathan, folio 5, column 1.** <note>An early secondary tractate of the Mishnah containing ethical teachings and historical anecdotes.</note> To the one who commits a transgression and fathers an illegitimate child, it is said: "You have insulted both yourself and me." They (the schoolboys) sat and learned in Jerusalem, but the illegitimate child sat until he came to Ashdod and said: "Woe is me! If I were not an illegitimate child, I would now also be sitting and learning among the young disciples. But now, because I am an illegitimate child, I do not sit and learn among the young disciples, for no illegitimate child comes into Jerusalem," as is said in Zechariah 9:6: "And the illegitimate child shall sit in Ashdod."

NB. <note>Latin abbreviation for *nota bene*, meaning "note well."</note> It is true that in Zechariah the word **<term>ממזר</term>** <note>Mamzer.</note> appears, meaning a stranger, a separator, or one of illegitimate birth; <note>original Latin: "alienus, Separator, Spurius."</note> however, in a prophetic sense, it has its purpose regarding a particularly heavy visitation.

### Method on Matters of Faith

...shown and named, should be attributed to no other creature, because it is the essential Name of **God**; since it stems from the word *Hayah* <note>original Hebrew: יהיה; this is a form of the verb "to be"</note>, which means as much as "to be." This is how Maimonides <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), a preeminent Sephardic Jewish philosopher and Torah scholar</note> explains it in the commentary *Shem Tov* <note>Referring to the commentary by Shem-Tov ibn Shem-Tov on Maimonides’s *Guide for the Perplexed*, specifically discussing the Divine Names</note>, folio 43, column 2, chapter 61: "Know that the name *Jehovah* <note>original Hebrew: יהוה; the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God</note> teaches that He was, still is, and will be." And as it is written in the book *The Beginning of Wisdom* <note>original title: *Reschith Chochma*, a prominent 16th-century kabbalistic work by Eliyahu de Vidas</note>, under the title *The Gate of Life* <note>original title: *Schaar chajim*</note>, chapter 3, folio 11, column 1: "**God** is called by this name *Jehovah* [יהוה] because He has given all things their essence <note>or "being"</note>."

Rabbi Joseph Albo <note>A 15th-century Jewish philosopher known for his work on the fundamentals of Judaism</note> in the *Book of Principles* <note>original title: *Sepher Ikkarim*</note>, under the title: *Second Treatise* <note>original: *Maamar Scheni*</note>, chapter 28, folio 54, column 1: The name which is written with the letters *Yod, He, Vav, He* is that which is called *Shem HaMephorash* <term>Shem HaMephorash: the "explicit" or "distinct" name of God, which was only pronounced in the Temple</term>, that is, the "distinct name." Thus, *Shem HaMephorash* signifies a name that is set apart from others and is unique to **God** among all names. And this occurs because the other names are applied to **God** and also to others; for they are names given to **God** either in consideration of certain works, or in consideration of another perspective, without necessarily following from the divine essence. One such name is *Elohim* <note>original Hebrew: אלהים</note>, which indicates power or authority; for this reason, it is used for Him and for others <note>In the Hebrew Bible, *Elohim* is used for the true God, but also occasionally for angels, judges, or foreign gods to denote their power</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on interfaith relations, specifically providing theological grounds for dialogue between Christians and Jews. ->### On Dialogue with the Jews<-

Just as Esau and Jacob <note>In the biblical narrative, these are the twin sons of Isaac; here used to symbolize the fraternal though strained relationship between Jews and Christians</note> were united on the part of their father and their faith, so also Christians and Israelites agree in this: that there is one Father of all in their faith, who is the **GOD** of Gods. This is because all confess and look up to the existence of the First Cause <term>First Cause: a philosophical term for God as the original source of all existence</term>, and they do not worship the stars or higher celestial powers. Furthermore, there is one law for them, which both sincerely admit and establish as the Law of Moses.

**Menasseh ben Israel** <note>A famous Portuguese rabbi and diplomat (1604–1657) who lived in Amsterdam</note> writes the following to the Christians in the dedicatory epistle of his book *On the Resurrection of the Dead*: "For we worship one **GOD**, and we subject ourselves to His precepts." And again in the dedication of his book *On Human Frailty*: "Men of both this and that religion, believing nonetheless in the one **GOD** of Israel."

In the Jewish book *The Scepter of Judah* <note>original: "Schebeth Jehudah," a 16th-century historical work by Solomon ibn Verga</note>, there is a conversation between Thomas and King Alfonso, in which Thomas testifies that he has the following from the mouth of the famous Rabbi Abarbanel (pages 8 and 9):

**Thomas:** In the Hebrew language, there is a distinction between *nokhri* <note>original: נכרי</note>, which signifies a foreigner or stranger, and *notzri* <note>original: נצרי</note>, "Nazarene" (as they call Christians), which is equivalent to the term "Gentile" <note>original: τῷ גוי</note>. A *nokhri*, or stranger, is one who alienates himself from his Creator and does not believe in the fundamental articles of the Law. But a *notzri*, or Christian, [is different] insofar as he believes the creation of the world occurred in time, and believes in the miracles of **GOD**, and in the worship...

<meta>catchword: who</meta>

It highlights the distinction made by rabbis between pagans (strangers) and Christians (Nazarenes) regarding their shared monotheistic foundation.</summary>
<keywords>Monotheism, Menasseh ben Israel, Isaac Abarbanel, Shevet Yehudah, Law of Moses, Nokhri, Notzri, interfaith dialogue</keywords>

->Description of the Kabbalah.<-

...ed the <term>Oral Torah</term> <note>original: תורה שבעל פה, Torah she-be’al peh</note>, that is, the law which is [given] by mouth; and this is because the exposition of the law was to be kept in the mind, so that it might be passed down to posterity by mouth and living voice; from which it also takes the name <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: קבלה, meaning "reception" or "tradition."</note>. Therefore, looking toward this double law, the Lord brought forth those different words. But since we have reached this place, it seems altogether worthwhile to explain what <term>Kabbalah</term> is and into how many parts it is divided; then, how its truth and use can be asserted against those who deny that there is any <term>Kabbalah</term> at all. The subject matter is extensive, but we will gather only the main points and those things most necessary.

First, therefore, it must be known that all other languages were devised by human skill, except for <term>Hebrew</term>; which, because it had God as its author, is called the <term>Holy Tongue</term> <note>original: לשון הקדש, Lashon Ha-Kodesh</note>. And just as it proceeds from the supreme and infinite wisdom, so its individual words contain the greatest mysteries, all of which no mortal is able to attain. Hence the divine Rabbi Shimon ben Yochai <note>A 2nd-century sage traditionally credited with the authorship of the Zohar.</note> says in the sacred book <term>Zohar</term> <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism.</note> (*in the portion Behaalotecha*): "Woe to the man who thinks that the law contains nothing beyond those things which its words seem to signify at first glance. For if that were true, men would be found even today who could tell more excellent things than those."

But it must be noted that the law came by assuming a body. Indeed, if it is necessary for angels descending into this world to assume bodies—since otherwise they could neither remain in it, nor could the world endure them—how much more so for the law, which created them and was the instrument through which the world [was created].

->Description of the Kabbalah.<-

[...by which all things] were created and are still sustained, it was necessary that it be adapted to human understanding and clothed in a body, without which the world would in no way be capable [of receiving] it. That body is the history <note>In this context, 'history' refers to the literal narrative or the "plain meaning" (Peshat) of the biblical text.</note>, and whoever does not believe that a soul—that is, the mysteries—dwells within it, shall have no part in the world to come. These are the words of that great theologian <note>Referring to Rabbi Shimon ben Yochai.</note>, whose words are as certain as they are worthy of the highest admiration. For with sublime and most effective words, he proves that the law is a living soul, whose body (as he adds) only the ignorant enjoy; while the wise servants of the Most High King consider only the soul, which is the superior part; and other wise men [consider] the soul of this soul.

From this we clearly see the various levels of men in the study of the law. The simpler ones consider only the external shell—that is, the history—whose highest goal in study is to know how each individual story ended. Such people are saved because of their good intentions, even if they do not understand the deeper secrets of the law. For others, it is sufficient to know the explanations of the Commandments and the ceremonies, a knowledge we call **משנה** <term>Mishna</term> <note>original: Mischna; the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions.</note>, that is, the "reading"; or by another name, **תורה שבעל פה** the <term>Oral Law</term> <note>original: Torah she-be'al peh</note>, because it is contained in the mouths and memories of the wise. The first of these, the same Rabbi Shimon ben Yochai calls the **מראי מקרא** <term>Masters of Scripture</term> <note>original: Mari Mikra; those who focus on the literal text.</note>; the second, the **מראי משנה** <term>Masters of the Study</term> <note>original: Mari Mischna; those who focus on the legal traditions and Mishna.</note>; and finally, the **מראי קבלה** <term>Masters of Kabbalah</term> <note>original: Mari Kabbalah</note>, that is, of the traditions which teach the secret expositions of the law; which Moses, having received them from the mouth of the Lord, revealed to the wise, and they in turn to their descendants.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the depth of Jewish law, moving from the literal history to the legalistic Mishna, and finally to the mystical Kabbalah. ### ->Description of the Kabbalah.<-

From this we see that under the name of the mental or traditionary law <note>original: legis mentalis sive traditæ; referring to the Oral Torah handed down through generations.</note>, two parts are included into which it is divided: namely the *Mishna*, which is the clear commentary on the written law, and the hidden science, which is preeminently called the *Kabbalah*. Those who occupy themselves with the *Mishna* can be compared to apothecaries, who, preparing their potions and medicines from various simple ingredients, follow only the prescription of the physicians, since they themselves are ignorant of their properties. But the students of the *Kabbalah* are like doctors, who understand the causes and reasons for those things that are prescribed to the sick.

For example: the law commands that *Tephilin* <term>Tephilin</term> <note>original: Tephilin; ritual leather boxes containing Torah verses, worn during prayer, also called phylacteries.</note> be placed between the eyes and bound upon the hands as a sign. But what *Tephilin* actually are, or what the word טטפות <gloss>frontlets</gloss> <note>original: totaphot; the archaic biblical term for the objects worn between the eyes.</note> signifies, is not declared in the text. However, because it is in no way possible for everyone to agree on such a matter, one must listen to the *Mishna* and the oral law revealed by God to Moses, which teaches that they must be made of such-and-such material, in such-and-such a form, and placed in such-and-such a location.

Although this suffices for observing the divine commandment, it does not satisfy our soul, which wishes to investigate the cause of this mandate and what is denoted by it: what mystery lies hidden beneath the black leather, from which alone they may be prepared; what secret is held by those two letters—the ש <gloss>Shin</gloss> <note>The Hebrew letter Shin appears embossed on the head-tephillin, specifically with three branches on one side and four on the other.</note> which has three points, and the other which has four points; what the four compartments of the *Tephilla* <note>The ritual prayer-box applied to the head contains four separate parchment scrolls.</note> applied to the head signify; or what the opening through which the strap is inserted means. All these things are explained in the *Kabbalah*, and for this reason it is called the *soul of the soul*. It is likely, moreover, that Moses, having stayed on the mountain for three periods of forty days, [devoted himself] to this threefold

Thus, from this, it is sufficiently clear what the Kabbalah itself is and what its parts are.

Now, indeed, I wish to show those curious about this science by what means the Kabbalists investigate the deepest mysteries of the Law <note>The "Law" refers to the Torah, the first five books of the Bible</note>. Just as the Talmudists <note>original: *Thalmudici*</note> or doctors of the <term>Talmud (the name given to the explanation of the Mishnah, the oral tradition of Jewish law)</term> follow those Thirteen Rules, or dialectical canons, which were received through tradition from Rabbi Ishmael, are mentioned in the *Sifre* (on the section *Vayikra* <note>The book of Leviticus</note>), and were most learnedly explained by Rabbi Aaron ibn Hayyim in the *Middot Aaron*; so I find that there are also thirteen modes by which the Kabbalists draw out the secrets of Holy Scripture and their primary <note>original: *protologicos*</note> meanings, which we shall demonstrate as briefly as possible with clear examples. 

Therefore, beginning with the first word of the Law, *Bereshit* <note>original: בראשית; translated as "In the beginning"</note>, a significant difficulty presents itself to us. For according to the rules of the Grammarians, it should have been said as *Barishona* <note>original: בראשונה</note>; insofar as *Bereshit* is in the form of "regimen" <term>the "construct state," a grammatical form where a noun is linked to a following noun, usually meaning "In the beginning of..."</term>, yet no noun follows for it to govern.

Since it is certain that nothing in Holy Scripture was written incorrectly or in vain, the Theologians say that this word contains many mysteries, because *Bereshit* signifies *Chochma* <note>original: חכמה; Wisdom</note>, one of the ten most illustrious lights <note>referring to the Sephirot, the ten attributes or emanations through which the Infinite reveals itself</note>. -># Description of the Kabbalah.<-

We see from this that under the name of the **mental** or **handed-down law**, two parts are included into which it is divided: namely the <term>Mishnah (the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions)</term>, which is a clear commentary on the written law, and the hidden science, which is preeminently called the <term>Kabbalah</term>. Those who are occupied with the <term>Mishnah</term> can be compared to apothecaries <note>druggists or pharmacists</note> who, when preparing their potions and medicines from various simple ingredients, follow only the prescription of the doctors, since they themselves are ignorant of the actual powers of those ingredients. But students of the <term>Kabbalah</term> are like the doctors, who understand the causes and reasons for what is prescribed to the sick. 

For example: the law commands that <term>Tephillin</term> <note>phylacteries; small leather boxes containing scrolls of parchment with Torah verses</note> be placed between the eyes and bound upon the hands as a sign. But what <term>Tephillin</term> properly are, or what the word *totafot* <note>original: טוטפות; a biblical term for frontlets</note> signifies, is not explained. Since it is in no way possible for everyone to agree on such a matter, one must listen to the <term>Mishnah</term> and the oral law, revealed by God to Moses, which teaches that they must be made from a specific material, in a specific form, and must be placed in a specific location.

Although this is sufficient for observing the divine commandment, it does not satisfy our minds, which wish to investigate the cause of this mandate and what is denoted by it: what mystery lies hidden beneath the black leather from which alone it is permitted to prepare them; what secret is contained in those two versions of the letter <term>Shin</term> <note>original: שׁ</note>, one which has three points <note>referring to the three branches of the letter on one side of the head-tephillin</note>, and the other which has four <note>a mystical variation of the letter found on the other side of the head-tephillin</note>; what the four compartments of the <term>Tephillah</term> applied to the head signify; or what the hole through which the strap is inserted means. All these things are explained in the <term>Kabbalah</term>, and for that reason, it is called the **soul of the soul**.
