<meta>This page continues the transition from a general overview of the Kabbalistic arts into the specific technical methods used for scriptural interpretation. It picks up mid-sentence discussing how wisdom served as the tool for creation.</meta>

### Description of the Kabbalah

...they gather that it is hinted here that the world was created by God through the highest wisdom, according to the words of the wise man (Proverbs 3:19): "The Lord by wisdom founded the earth" <note>original: *Dominus cum scientia fundavit mundum*</note>. That is, knowledge was the instrumental cause in the creation of the world. These things having been explained, they proceed to those rules by which the mysteries of the Law are extracted.

I. The first of these is called <term>Notaricon</term> <note>original: נוטריקיון; a shorthand method where each letter of a word serves as the initial for a new word, or a word is split into multiple parts</note>. This teaches how to form as many words as there are individual letters in a specific vocabulary. Thus, from the word *Bereshit* <note>original: בראשית; "In the beginning," the first word of Genesis</note>, which consists of six letters, they form these words: *Be-reshonah ra'ah Elohim she-yekabelu Yisrael Torah* <note>original: בראשונה ראה אלהים שיקבלו ישראל תורה</note>, meaning: "In the beginning, God saw that the Israelites would receive the Law." From this, they conclude that the world was created for the sake of the Law which Israel was to receive. 

By this same rule, a single word is also dissolved into two. From *Bereshit* they make *Bara shit* <note>original: ברא שית</note>, meaning "He created six"—referring, of course, to the six days of creation, or the six extremities of the world: the top, the bottom, and the four sides. At other times, they place the same letter twice in different words; from *Shamayim* <note>original: שמים; heavens</note>, they make *sham maim* <note>original: שם מים</note>, meaning "there is water," because of the crystalline waters that are within it.

Again, they also add a letter at the beginning, so that from *Shamayim* <note>original: שמים</note>, there arises *Esh, mayim* <note>original: אש מים</note>, "fire and water." From this, they prove, alongside **Plato** <note>The author references the Greek philosopher's theory of the elements to find common ground with Kabbalistic thought</note>, that the heaven is composed of fire and water. 

The teachers of the <term>Talmud</term> <note>original: *Thalmudici*; referring to the rabbis of the central text of Jewish law</note> also use this rule, and they extract excellent things from the words of Scripture through it. Thus, in the passage where **Jacob** is said to have given his son **Joseph** a tunic of many colors <note>original: פסים, *passim*</note>, they wish to imply by this word his four misfortunes; for from its four letters, they form four...

<meta>catchword: four</meta>

<summary>This page explains the first rule of Kabbalistic interpretation, Notaricon, which uses acrostics and word-splitting to find hidden meanings in the Book of Genesis. It demonstrates how words like "Bereshit" and "Shamayim" are decoded to reveal philosophical truths about creation, the Law, and the elements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Kabbalistic interpretation methods, moving from the conclusion of Notaricon (acronyms and word-splitting) into the second method, Tziruf (letter permutation). It concludes a discussion on the hidden meanings within the names of Joseph's captors and the name of Adam.</meta>

### Description of the Kabbalah

...four words: **Potiphar**, **merchants**, **Ishmaelites**, and **Midianites** <note>original: פוטיפר סוחרים ישמעאלים מדנים (*Potiphar, socharim, Yishmaelim, Medanim*)</note>; to all of whom he <note>Joseph</note> was sold.

In the *Gemara* of tractate *Sotah*, chapter 1, the sages say that the letters of the name **Adam** (אדם) signify **dust**, **blood**, and **gall** <note>original: *אפר דם מרה* (*efer, dam, merah*)</note>. However, this rule—as **Rabbi Samuel Archivolti** says in *Arugat ha-Bosem* <note>meaning "The Bed of Spices," a 16th-century work on Hebrew grammar and rhetoric</note>, chapter 29—is only to be used for seeking honorable and useful things from the Law, several examples of which are mentioned in the *Kol Bo* <note>A significant medieval collection of Jewish ritual laws</note>. In the *Gemara* of tractate *Shabbat*, chapter 12, you may also read that this rule is taken from Holy Scripture itself; for when the Lord changed the name of **Abram** to **Abraham**, He said He bestowed it because he would become a **father of a multitude** <note>original: *אב המון* (*Ab Hamon*)</note>, dissolving one word into the two that it contained.

The second rule is named **<term>Tziruf</term>** <note>original: צירוף; meaning "combination" or "permutation"</note>, that is, combination: when the letters of the same word are transposed in various ways, new and different words are formed. Thus, the word **Bereshit** <note>The first word of Genesis: "In the beginning"</note> becomes, through rearrangement, **"On the first of Tishrei"** <note>original: *בא תשרי* (*Ba Tishrei*); Tishrei is the first month of the Hebrew civil calendar</note>. From this they conclude that the world was created in the month of Tishrei, or September. Again, by another transposition, it becomes **"You created that which is"** <note>original: *בראת יש* (*Barat Yesh*)</note>, by which they mean to signify that the world was created not from prime matter, but from nothing. And yet again, by another rearrangement, **"Covenant of fire"** <note>original: *אש ברית* (*Esh Berit*)</note>, which agrees with the words of Moses in Deuteronomy 33:2 <note>The source text cites chapter 23, but the "fiery law" reference is found in 33:2</note>.

And not to add more, **Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai** <note>The traditional author of the Zohar</note> composed his book *Tikkunim* <note>Referring to the *Tikkunei ha-Zohar*, a work consisting of 70 interpretations of the word "Bereshit"</note> concerning seventy-two combinations of this single word. For as it is said in the *Sefer Yetzirah* <note>The "Book of Formation," one of the oldest extant books on Jewish esotericism</note>, from a word of two letters, two words are formed; from one of three letters...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Kabbalistic interpretation methods, specifically focusing on Tziruf (letter permutation) and the introduction of Gematria (numerical interpretation). It follows a discussion on the transposition of the first word of Genesis.</meta>

### Description of the Kabbalah

...six; from a four-letter word, sixteen; and so on. This occurs because, since the letters are spiritual and the shapes are simple, they must (as the wise **Rabbi Moses Cordovero** <note>A leading 16th-century Kabbalist in Safed, known for systematizing mystical thought</note> says in his "Gate of Letters" <note>original: *Pardes saar a-otiiot*, a section within his major work *Pardes Rimonim* or "The Orchard of Pomegranates"</note>) be transposed in various ways. He who knows how to do this correctly can even perform miracles.

The truth of this rule, in my judgment, can also be asserted from Holy Scripture. For in Genesis, chapter 6, **Noah** is said to have found grace in the eyes of the Lord; but the name **Noah** <note>original: נח (*Noach*)</note> when transposed becomes **grace** <note>original: חן (*Chen*)</note>. And you may read of **Er** <note>The firstborn son of Judah in Genesis 38:7</note>, "and **Er** was wicked in the eyes of the Lord." The name **Er** <note>original: ער (*‘Er*)</note> when transposed is **evil** <note>original: רע (*Ra‘*)</note>.

The third rule they call **<term>Gematria</term>** <note>A system of Hebrew exegesis where the numerical values of letters are calculated and used to find hidden connections between words or phrases</note>; its foundation is numbers. For since Hebrew letters are simultaneously both letters and numbers, they contain all things. **Pythagoras** <note>The Greek philosopher who believed that the universe is governed by mathematical ratios</note> surely used to say that the world was created by God through numbers; and **Plato’s** "Ideas"—as we demonstrated in our *Third Question on Exodus*—are actually letters. We have an example of this rule in the word **Bereshit** <note>original: בראשית; the first word of the Bible, "In the beginning"</note>, whose letters result in the same numerical sum as the phrase **"He formed by the Law"** <note>original: בתורה יצר (*Be-Torah yatzar*)</note>. From this, they conclude that the Law <note>The Torah</note> was the instrumental cause of the world. The word for **conception** <note>original: הריון (*Herayon*)</note> is worth 271; from this they conjecture that, by natural logic, the fetus remains in the womb for that many days <note>271 days is roughly nine months, the standard duration of human pregnancy</note>.

They also hold that the Law began with the letter **Bet** (ב), which is worth **two**, to signify the double law—the written and the oral—or justice ☩ mercy, and other things of that kind. We use this **Gematria** in various ways, as it is handed down in the *Pardes Rimonim* <note>The "Orchard of Pomegranates" by Moses Cordovero</note> (in the "Gate of Combination," chapter 8), and often according to the number which...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Gematria (numerical exegesis) and introduces the fourth rule of Kabbalistic interpretation, Zurà, which focuses on the physical shapes of Hebrew letters.</meta>

### Description of the Kabbalah

...the sum that the letters of a word make, a unit is added which they call <term>Kolel</term> <gloss>Colel</gloss> <note>original: כולל; a Kabbalistic rule where one may add a "unit" representing the word as a whole to its numerical sum</note>, because it stands for the entire word. Thus, the word <term>Covenant</term> <gloss>Berit</gloss> <note>original: ברית</note> has a value of 612; by adding the *Kolel*, it becomes 613. From this they say that the same word, which denotes the Law, also signifies the six hundred and thirteen commandments <note>The *Mitzvot*, or divine precepts, which tradition says number 613</note> of which the Law consists.

I also judge this secret of numbers to be derived from Holy Scripture. For (as we read in 1 Kings, chapter 18, verse 31) the Prophet **Elijah** took twelve stones to perform a miracle; not only because there were as many sons of **Jacob**—which would have contributed little to the matter—but because of the mystery contained in the number twelve. For this same reason, Jacob himself had twelve sons, even though Holy Scripture conceals that fact beneath historical narrative. This Gematria is used everywhere by the Talmudists, and much more so by more recent authors. Among these are **Rabbi Solomon Molcho** <note>A 16th-century Portuguese mystic and martyr who reclaimed his Jewish identity and preached messianic messages</note> in his sermons, **Rabbi Jacob ben Habib** <note>Author of the *Ein Yaakov*; the text here associates him with the *Baal ha-Turim*, a famous legal code and gematria commentary actually written by Jacob ben Asher</note>, **Rabbi Mordecai Kohen** in *The Lips of the Priest* <note>original: *Sipte Kohen*</note>, **Rabbi Sabbatai** in *The Abundance of Dew* <note>original: *Shefa Tal*, a key work on the system of Moses Cordovero</note>, and the author of *A Mingled Offering* <note>original: *Mincha Belula*</note>; through this method, sublime mysteries are discovered.

The name of the fourth rule is <term>Form</term> <gloss>Zurà</gloss> <note>original: צורא; referring to the physical shape or "figure" of the letters</note>. This relies on the shapes of the characters. Thus they hold that the Law began with the letter **Bet** <note>The letter ב, which is the first letter of the first word of Genesis, *Bereshit*</note>, because this letter consists of three lines—that is, three **Vaus** <note>The letter ו, which resembles a vertical line</note>. If you write these out with their own letters in this way—**Vau-Aleph-Vau** <note>ואו</note>—they are worth three times thirteen, or thirty-nine, which is the same number made by the phrase **The LORD is One** <note>original: יהוה אחד (*YHWH Echad*)</note>. They also say that this letter has a point in its upper part and another in its lower part, to denote that the Lord, who is in the heavens, created the world—which [is continued in] the following let-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Zurà," the fourth rule of Kabbalistic interpretation concerning the mystical shapes of Hebrew letters. It concludes the analysis of the letter Aleph and begins the fifth rule, Notarikon (the use of acrostics and letter positions).</meta>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

...the [let]ter is signified, namely <term>Aleph</term> <note>original: א</note>, which has a point or <term>Yod</term> <note>original: י</note> on both sides and a <term>Vav</term> <note>original: ו</note> in the middle. These together make the number 26, which is also contained in the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter divine name YHWH (יהוה). In Hebrew numerology, the letter Aleph is visually composed of two Yods (value 10 each) and one Vav (value 6), totaling 26—the same numerical value as the name of God.</note>.

In the same way, when the word **Aleph** is written out with its letters as <term>אלף</term>, it signifies *Aluf*, which means "Lord" or "Master." If the sound is reversed so that the last letter becomes the first, you have <term>Pele</term> <note>original: פלא</note>, meaning "Wonderful." Indeed, reason itself seems to dictate that the shapes of the characters, devised by the Supreme Architect, contain mysteries; for He did not without cause give this or that form to one letter or another.

The fifth rule is called <term>Rashe Tevot and Sofe Tevot</term> <gloss>Beginnings of words and ends of words</gloss> <note>original: ראשי תיבות וסופי תיבות; this is a form of *Notarikon*, a shorthand or acrostic system</note>. For example, in Deuteronomy 30:12, it asks: *Who shall go up for us to heaven?* <note>original: מי יעלה לנו השמימה (Mi ya’aleh lanu ha-shamaimah)</note>. The four initial letters of these words form the word <term>Milah</term> <gloss>circumcision</gloss> <note>original: מילה</note>; the four final letters form the venerable name of God, the **Tetragrammaton** <note>original: יהוה (YHWH)</note>. Thus, the same words contain both the question and the answer. The question is: "Who shall lift us up to heaven?" The answer is: "The Lord, through circumcision."

Sometimes even the middle letters of words are taken, called <term>Tokhi Tevot</term> <gloss>the middles of words</gloss> <note>original: תוכי תיבות</note>. Modern authors use this rule extensively, through which the ineffable name of God is elicited from various passages of Holy Scripture with good reason. For instance, in the phrase *Let the heavens rejoice and the earth be glad* <note>original: ישמחו השמים ותגל הארץ (Yismehu ha-shamayim ve-tagel ha-aretz) from Psalm 96:11</note>, the initial letters of the words are the name of the **Lord** <note>YHWH</note>. At other times, the same name is found written in reverse order, so that the word begins from its last letter; the Kabbalists determine this to be a sign of divine **rigor** <note>In Kabbalah, a reversed name often symbolizes the attribute of *Gevurah* (Judgment or Rigor) rather than *Chesed* (Mercy)</note>.

<summary>This page explains how the physical shape of the letter Aleph numerically equals the name of God and introduces Notarikon, the Kabbalistic practice of forming new words or divine names from the first, last, or middle letters of scriptural phrases.</summary>
<keywords>Aleph, Tetragrammaton, Rashe Tevot, Notarikon, Circumcision, Divine Rigor, Hebrew Alphabet, Numerology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the seven rules of Kabbalistic interpretation. Having previously discussed the shapes of letters (Zura) and acrostics (Notarikon), the text now moves to the sixth and seventh rules: letter permutation and the significance of full or deficient spelling.</meta>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

The sixth rule is <term>Temurah</term> <gloss>permutation</gloss>, which teaches one to take <term>Tav</term> <note>the last letter, ת</note> for <term>Aleph</term> <note>the first letter, א</note>, <term>Shin</term> <note>ש</note> for <term>Bet</term> <note>ב</note>, and so on for the others. <note>This specific system of letter-swapping is known as *Atbash*, a cipher where the first letter of the Hebrew alphabet is replaced by the last, the second by the second-to-last, etc.</note> This rule is likewise supported by the authority of Holy Scripture. For the Prophet Jeremiah, in chapter 25, so as not to provoke the king, cleverly changed the name **Babel** <note>Babylon</note> into **Sheshach** <note>Jeremiah 25:26</note>, placing two <term>Shins</term> for the double <term>Bet</term>, and a <term>Khaf</term> <note>ך</note> for the <term>Lamed</term> <note>ל</note>. By this method, many names of God are investigated, such as those derived from the Priestly Blessing. One may read of this in the book *Sipte Kohen* <note>original: *Sipte coën*; "The Lips of the Priest," a 17th-century commentary by Mordecai Ha-Kohen of Safed</note> in the section on the Torah portion *Nasso*.

The name of the seventh rule is <term>Chaser ve-Male</term> <gloss>deficient and full</gloss>; this is frequently used by both the Talmudic scholars and the Kabbalists. It is supported by a remarkable logic. For it seems that the rules of spelling must always be observed in Holy Scripture; yet sometimes they are neglected. For example, while **Eloah** <note>God, אלוה</note> should be written with the letter <term>Vav</term> <note>ו</note>, in Deuteronomy 32:17 it is written without it as *lo eloah* <gloss>not God</gloss> <note>Hebrew: לא אלה</note>. In the same way, you will find that **Elohe** <note>the form of the word "God" used when joined to another noun</note>, or when it has a suffix, is always written "deficiently" <note>missing the letter Vav</note>, except in two places noted by the **Masoretes** <note>Jewish scholars between the 6th and 10th centuries who standardized the pronunciation and spelling of the Hebrew Bible</note>, who made it their task to collect how many times the same word is found written in the same way in Holy Scripture.

Rabbi **Meir Angel** published a book on this subject titled *Masoret ha-Berit* <gloss>The Tradition of the Covenant</gloss>, and Rabbi **Moses ben Samuel** [the King] wrote the work *Sefer ha-Mesorot* <gloss>The Book of Traditions</gloss>. The Kabbalists provide the reasoning for this, and anyone deprived of their explanation might easily fall into error. Thus the word **Kalot** <gloss>completing</gloss> is usually written with a <term>Vav</term>; but in Numbers 7:1, that letter is missing. The Kabbalists say it is written as **Kalat** <note>Hebrew: כלת</note>. For indeed, the Law <note>the Torah</note> was given without vowel points, similar to those **Sifrei Torah** <note>original: *Sepharim*; Torah scrolls</note> which the Jews have in their synagogues today...

<summary>This page explains the Kabbalistic rules of Temurah (letter substitution ciphers like Atbash) and Chaser ve-Male (the mystical significance of "missing" or "extra" letters in Biblical spelling).</summary>
<keywords>Temurah, Atbash, Jeremiah, Sheshach, Chaser ve-Male, Masoretes, Hebrew spelling, Eloah, Meir Angel, Sifrei Torah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the seventh rule of Kabbalistic interpretation, Chaser ve-Male (deficient and full spelling), which notes when words are written with or without certain vowel-letters like Vav or Yod. It then introduces the eighth rule regarding the structural layout of the Torah text.</meta>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

...as the Hebrews testify (as is attested by Rabbi Judah Halevi in the *Kuzari*, book 3, article 30; Rabbi Abraham ibn Ezra in *Tzachot*; and Elia Levita the Grammarian in *Masoret Ha-Masoret*, Introduction 3): whenever a certain word occurs whose letters do not restrict it to fixed vowels, one is permitted to assign whichever vowel points one prefers to it. Thus, the word *Kalot* <note>original: "Calot," meaning completions or perfections</note>, because the letter <term>Vav</term> <note>ו</note> is absent, can also be read as *Kallat* <note>meaning "bride"</note>, on account of an enclosed mystery. <note>This refers to the "completion" of the Tabernacle being likened to a wedding between God and Israel.</note>

The Talmudic scholars observe many and various things in the Law through this rule, as can be seen in the *Gemara* <note>the component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis</note> in the tractate *Pesachim* chapter 11, and in *Kiddushin*, and other places. Moreover, when they read a certain word with different vowels, they are accustomed to say: *There is a basis for the reading, and there is a basis for the tradition* <note>original Hebrew: "yesh em la-mikra ve-yesh em la-masoret"</note>; implying that words can be read both in the way they are written <note>the Masoretic spelling</note> and in the way they are traditionally recited.

The eighth rule is called <term>Setumot u-Petuchot</term> <gloss>closed and open sections</gloss>. By this, they investigate the reason why, between two different subjects, a letter <term>Pe</term> <note>פ</note> is sometimes placed, signifying *Petucha* <gloss>open</gloss>, while at other times a <term>Samech</term> <note>ס</note> is placed, signifying *Setuma* <gloss>closed</gloss>. <note>In a Torah scroll, these letters indicate different types of paragraph breaks: an "open" section starts on a new line, while a "closed" section begins after a space on the same line.</note> Furthermore, they investigate why a space is left in certain places where it ought not to be, such as in the story of Cain in Genesis 4:8, where there is a <term>Pisca</term> <gloss>separation or gap</gloss>. In this manner: *And Cain said to Abel his brother*—and then there is a pause—and soon follows, *and it happened, when they were in the field*. <note>The text indicates Cain said something, but the words are missing, leaving a physical gap in the scroll.</note> In Numbers 26:1, you have: *And it happened after the plague*—followed by an inserted letter <term>Pe</term> <note>פ</note> in the middle of the sentence—*and the Lord said*. In contrast, the Torah portion <term>Vayechi</term> <note>meaning "And he lived," the final portion of Genesis</note> has no distinction <meta>catchword: distinction-</meta> in the Torah Scroll...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the eighth rule of Kabbalistic interpretation, which concerns the structural layout of the Hebrew text—specifically the "open" and "closed" sections (Setumot and Petuchot) marked by the letters Pe and Samekh.</meta>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

...is separated by a [closed] section, which nevertheless all the others have.

It is certain that these things are not without mystery. For a similar reason, you will sometimes find "open" <note>non-final forms of Hebrew letters</note> letters at the end of words, which grammatical rules teach should only be placed at the beginning or in the middle. Thus, in the Book of Ezra, you may read *Hem* <note>meaning "they" or "them"; original: הם</note> with an open letter at the end. At other times, "closed" letters <note>final forms of letters</note>, which are only suited for the end of a word, are placed in the middle; as in Isaiah 9:6, *le-marbe* <note>meaning "to the increase"; original: לםרבה</note>. The Kabbalists explain the reason for this in a divine manner. Since everything in the divine letters has its cause, it is certain that to investigate them, the use of this rule is necessary—a rule that the Talmudic scholars also use everywhere.

The ninth rule they call *Keri ve-la Ketiv, Ketiv ve-la Keri* <note>original Hebrew: קרי ולא כתיב כתיב ולא קרי</note>, which means "it is read but not written; it is written but not read." For we learn from the marginal notes that many words must be read differently than they are written; and often we are commanded to supply a whole word that is not contained in the main text. Thus, in Psalm 24:4, you find: *v’lo nasa la-shav nafsho* <note>meaning "who has not lifted up his soul to vanity"; original: ולא נשא לשוא נפשו</note>. The Kabbalists advise that it should be read as "my soul" <note>Hebrew: *nafshi*</note> because of a hidden mystery. There is much use of this rule among the Talmudic scholars to establish the <term>Dinim</term> <gloss>legal rulings or judgments</gloss>. And who can doubt that there is a mystery in those things which are written, and in those which are meant to be read?

The tenth rule is based on the *Ketannot u-Gedolot* <note>original Hebrew: קטנות וגדולות</note>, the "small and large" letters, because sometimes in Holy Scripture one finds a letter smaller than the rest of the script. Thus, in Genesis 2:4, in the word *Behibbaream* <note>meaning "when they were created"; original: בהבראם</note>...

<summary>This page explores the 8th, 9th, and 10th rules of Kabbalistic and Talmudic interpretation, focusing on scribal anomalies like unusual letter forms (open/closed), differences between the written text and the spoken tradition (Keri and Ketiv), and the use of oversized or undersized letters.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rules of Kabbalistic interpretation. The previous page concluded rule nine regarding words that are read differently than they are written (Keri and Ketiv). This page begins mid-sentence, discussing the tenth rule concerning the size and orientation of specific Hebrew letters.</meta>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

...[He created] them <note>original Latin: *crearentur illi*</note>, the letter *He* <note>ה</note> is small; by contrast, in Deuteronomy 32:6, the *He* is large in the phrase *Ha-la-YHVH* <note>original Hebrew: ה ליהוה</note>, meaning "to the Lord." You will sometimes find such differences, as in the first word of Leviticus <note>the word *Vayikra*, where the final *aleph* is written smaller than the other letters</note>, and in Chapter 34 of Exodus, and many other places. There are also instances where a letter is placed higher than the others, as in Judges 18:30, the letter *Nun* <note>נ</note> in the name Menasseh; or conversely, it is lowered and inverted, as in Numbers chapter 10, where two *Nuns* are written in this fashion <note>inverted nun symbol</note>. Rabbi Ephraim correctly noted in the work *Ir Gibborim* <note>City of Heroes; specifically in the section on the Torah portion Ha'azinu</note> that these things are not without mystery. Such variations might indeed be attributed to the negligence of scribes in other books; but in the divine scriptures, one must judge that they were written not by chance, but intentionally, because of a hidden secret.

The eleventh rule is *Hiluf* <note>original Hebrew: חילוף</note>, or variation, which involves the changes of letters when one is substituted for another: as in Genesis 13:2 <note>The text likely refers to the orthography of "his tent" in Gen 12:8 or 13:3</note>, *Aolo* <note>original: אהלה</note>, meaning "his tent," in which place, according to the rules of Grammarians, a *Vav* <note>ו</note> should have been written instead of a *He* <note>ה</note>. Many examples of this kind of substitution occur; the Grammarians assert no other reason for them than saying one letter is used for another. But a more hidden cause lies beneath, reserved for the knowledge of the Kabbalists alone. Thus, the letters *He, Aleph, Mem, Nun, Tav, Yod* <note>original: האמנתי</note> are often redundant at the end of words or are superfluous; however, this is not without mystery, for nothing was written in vain by the supreme Creator.

The foundation of the twelfth rule is the points which we call *Nekudot* <note>original Hebrew: נקודות; refers to the vowel points and accents added to the Hebrew text</note>, where again the mystery lies in the fact that among them are five kings <note>a grammatical and mystical categorization of the Hebrew vowel sounds</note>,

<summary>This page details the tenth and eleventh rules of Kabbalistic interpretation, focusing on scribal anomalies like oversized, miniature, or inverted letters, and the mystical significance of substituted or redundant letters in the biblical text.</summary>

->### Description of the Kabbalah<-

...five ministers, one of which is called the "minister of ministers"; as the learned Cordovero <note>Moses Cordovero (1522–1570), a central figure in the Safed school of Kabbalah</note> correctly observes in his work *Pardes Rimonim* <note>The Orchard of Pomegranates</note>, in the section *Sha'ar ha-Ta'amim* <note>The Gate of Accents</note>. This can even be proven from Holy Scripture itself: for the ineffable name, which is always written as **YHVH** <note>יהוה, the Tetragrammaton</note> in Deuteronomy 3:24, is found with different vowel points as **Yihovih** <note>יהֹוִה; the vowels here indicate the name should be pronounced as *Elohim* (God) rather than the usual *Adonai* (Lord)</note>, which did not happen by chance. Elsewhere we find superfluous dots written above the letters, such as in the last verse of Deuteronomy chapter 29, on the two words **lanu u-le-vanenu** <note>לָנ֧וּ וּלְבָנֵינ֧וּ</note>, meaning "to us and to our children"; and so it is in other places, all of which are known to signify something hidden. One may look at what the Talmudic scholars have written concerning this passage in the *Gemara* <note>The analytical part of the Talmud</note> of the tractate *Berachot* <note>Blessings</note>, chapter 1, and in other treatises.

The thirteenth and final rule is based on the <term>Taamim</term> <note>Hebrew: accents or tastes</note>, or musical accents; some of these are "regal" <note>disjunctive accents that indicate a pause</note> and others are "servile" <note>conjunctive accents that connect words</note>. Some distinguish between full periods or parts of them, much like commas and periods among the Latins; others, in turn, connect them, as the sense of each passage requires. A difficulty arises from these, however, because in some places words that should be connected are separated, or those that should be separated are connected. For instance, in Numbers 27:3, we read that the daughters of Zelophehad said, "**avinu met ba-midbar**" <note>אבינו מת במדבר</note>, "our father died in the wilderness." Here the word "**avinu**" <note>our father</note> has the accent <term>Zarka</term> <note>a disjunctive accent mark</note>, by which it is separated from the words that follow, even though the sense seems to allow no such distinction.

For this reason, many secrets are said to be hidden here in the *Zohar* <note>The primary text of Kabbalistic mysticism</note>. In the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments</note>, truly, every single word is marked with two accents, whereas throughout the rest of Holy Scripture...

And first, indeed, something seems to be missing in the principal commandment, which is handed down in these words in Exodus 12:2: *This month shall be to you the beginning of months; it shall be the first month of the year to you.* <note>original Latin: "Mensis hic vobis erit principium mensium, primus ille vobis erit ex mensibus anni."</note> For besides the fact that the name of the month is omitted here, it is not even expressed whether solar or lunar months are to be understood. At that time, different nations had different systems: for the year of the Egyptians comprised 365 days and a quarter of a day, divided into twelve months; whereas the year of the Chaldeans <note>The Babylonians</note> was 365 days and a fourth or fifth part of an hour. Furthermore, it is not added by what method a solar year can be reconciled with a lunar one, or the lunar with the solar. For by true calculation, a solar year contains 365 days, 5 hours, 817 points, and 12 fragments; whereas the lunar year contains 354 days, 8 hours, and 777 points. This lunar total matches the number contained in the word *shanah* <note>original: "שנה" (Shanah), meaning "year"</note>, which is worth 355 <note>The OCR text reads "CCLV" (255), but as noted, the gematria (numerical value) of Shin (300) + Nun (50) + He (5) is 355; a lunar year is approximately 354-355 days.</note>. And yet, it is necessary to know all these things to understand the commandment.

For the Lord commanded the Passover of the Unleavened Bread to be celebrated on the fifteenth day of that same month, Nisan, or March; and that at the time when the barley is already ripe. Then the Festival of Weeks <note>Shavuot</note>, on the sixth...

**XIV.** Deuteronomy 25:5. Regarding marriage, it is stated when a man should take a wife, but it is not added what specific actions constitute the marriage ceremony. **XV.** In the same book, 17:16. A king is forbidden from multiplying horses, wives, gold, and silver for himself. Now, since human ambition is insatiable, it is clear that a specific limit <term>limit; a defined numerical maximum</term> should have been established, yet this is not done in the text.

**XVI.** Leviticus 23:40. At the Feast of Tabernacles <term>Sukkot; a festival where participants dwell in temporary booths</term>, it is commanded to take branches of beautiful trees; yet the specific name of the tree is not added, even though so many varieties of beautiful trees are found.

**XVIII.** In the same book, chapter 18. Among the list of illicit marriages, marriage with the wife of an uncle is not prohibited, nor with one's paternal grandmother, nor one's maternal grandmother.

### Description of the Kabbalah.

---

...nor yet will you find any place in Holy Scripture <note>original: "S. Scriptura"</note> where this is commanded. Concerning this, as with all other matters, the traditions must be heard, for they resolve all those difficulties. And were it not for this, contrary to what David testifies, the Law would be imperfect. And this is certainly true regarding the words themselves. For furthermore, the Talmudists <note>Scholars of the Talmud, the central text of Jewish civil and ceremonial law</note> are able to derive everything from the Law through the rules they have received by tradition.

For a similar reason, many things would seem superfluous to us if we did not possess the traditions. For the commandment against boiling a young goat in its mother’s milk is found twice—Exodus 23:19 <note>The original text cites XXXIII, likely a printer's error for XXIII</note>, Exodus 34:26, and Deuteronomy 14:21 <note>The original cites XVI, likely a printer's error for XIV</note>. Likewise, when it is said that a man shall die on the testimony of two or three witnesses (Deuteronomy 17:6 <note>The original cites verse 7</note>), it seems redundant to name three witnesses when two would have been sufficient. 

Similarly, in Exodus 21:21, we read regarding a servant struck by a master that the death must be avenged unless the servant survives the wound for a day or two: if surviving one day acquitted the master of the charge of murder, why is the second day added, since the fact of the survival itself demonstrates the point? All these things the traditions and the <term>Kabbalah; the Jewish mystical and oral tradition</term> reveal to us, providing wonderful reasons for them; without these traditions, we would wander about like blind men and offer nothing but uncertain conjectures. Thus, there should be no doubt that the traditions, or the <term>vocal law; also known as the Oral Torah, believed to have been given to Moses alongside the written text</term>, are absolutely necessary.

And for that reason, in Exodus 18:16, the people of Israel are commanded to consult Moses regarding difficult questions, which would not have been necessary if everything in the Law had been clear. But because not everyone understood the Law equally or correctly, it is stated in Deuteronomy 17:9 that it is necessary for each person to consult the authorities of his own time...

Prophet. And so *Moses* delivered the law to the priests, the sons of *Levi*, so that they might interpret it for the rest; he did not wish to write down those expositions, which are better understood when passed on by word of mouth, whereas written words often possess ambiguous meanings.

Relying upon tradition, we can prove that the Prophets and holy men did certain things that appear to contradict the written law. Thus, *Solomon* <note>original: "Selomoh"</note> sanctified the middle of the courtyard and offered sacrifices in a place other than the altar, and he celebrated the *Passover* <note>original: "Pascha"</note> for fourteen days, which included the Day of Atonement <note>original: "Kipur"</note>. *David* and *Samuel* established singers with musical instruments to perform in the temple. *Uriah* <note>original: "Hurias"</note> the Prophet fled into *Egypt* against the commandment of the law. *Hezekiah* <note>original: "Hizquiahu"</note> celebrated the *Passover* in the second month because the people had not yet been purified. *Elijah* <note>original: "Eliahu"</note> offered a sacrifice on Mount *Carmel* outside of the temple. They would never have done such things, which contradict the literal words of scripture, had they not relied upon the traditions—which, for specific reasons, occasionally permitted one thing or another, as can be read in various places in the *Talmud* <note>original: "Thalmude"</note>. Therefore, from all these examples, one can see how necessary are the traditions, whether called the <term>Kabbalah; literally 'received tradition'</term> or the Oral Law <note>original: "lex vocalis"</note>.

Indeed, *Josephus* writes in Book 13 of his *Antiquities* <note>original: "ad Roman.", likely referring to the Jewish Antiquities written for a Roman audience</note>, chapters 9 and 18, as well as in Book 2, chapter 2, and in Book 2 of *The Jewish War*, chapter 7, that at the time of the Second Temple the Hebrews were divided into three classes. Of these, the **Pharisees** <note>Hebrew: פרישים (*Perushim*)</note> followed the traditions alone. The **Sadducees** <note>Hebrew: צדוקים (*Tzadokim*)</note> and **Essenes** <note>Hebrew: איסיאי (*Isiaei*)</note>—the latter also called **Boethusians** <note>Hebrew: ביתוסים (*Baitosim*)</note>, whom *Rabbi Nathan* mentions in the *Ethics of the Fathers* <note>original: "Abot"</note>, chapter 5, and *Solomon* in the tractate *Baba Batra*, chapter 8—rejected these same traditions, as is passed down in the

# Description of the Kabbalah.

---

Gemara Yoma Chapter 1. Scroll of Tamid chapters 1, 4, and 10. Menachot chapter 8. Bava Batra chapter 8. Shabbat chapter 14. Tractate Sopherim <note>The "Tractate of Scribes."</note> chapter 14. Kiddushin chapter 3. The *Kuzari* Book 3. The commentary composed by Rabbi Moses <note>Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, also known as Maimonides.</note> on the first chapter of *Pirkei Avot* and *Chullin*, and the *Heritage of the Fathers* <note>original: "Nahalat Abot"</note> by Isaac Abravanel.

The Sadducees were worse than the Essenes, because they denied the immortality of souls and the resurrection of the dead; but they did not last long, and after the destruction of the Temple they were greatly diminished. Later, during the times of the <term>Geonim; the heads of the Babylonian rabbinical academies</term>, Hanan and his son Saul (according to the testimony of <term>Abrabad; Abraham ibn Daud</term> in his *Book of Tradition* <note>original: "Sepher a-Cabala"</note>) became angry because they had not been chosen as the Exilarch <note>original: "ducem ac principem captivitatis," the leader of the Jewish community in exile.</note>. Out of spite, they revived that same sect, and with several disciples, they denied the traditions. Following him in the time of Rabbi Saadia [Gaon] was Hiwi al-Balkhi <note>original: "Hivai Alcalabi," a 9th-century skeptic.</note>, who was the author of a thousand absurdities and a "new law."

Furthermore, the same Rabbi Saadia so thoroughly overcame them that very few continued to adhere to their perverse opinion; and even these few were unrefined and unskilled men who were incapable of composing a book. Hence, they were excluded from the communion of the Israelites, who in all places received with one consent the Oral Law <note>original: "legem vocalem"</note>, which we call by another name, the <term>Kabbalah; meaning 'received tradition'</term>. We call it this because the Lord revealed it to Moses by word of mouth, and looking to that fact, He said: *I speak with him mouth to mouth.* <note>A reference to Numbers 12:8.</note> May God be our guide to the truth!

This is now the beautiful foundation upon which one should base the understanding of divine pronouncements. However, I leave it to the consideration of an impartial <term>philologist; a scholar of language and history</term> whether these riddles... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "ænig-" (ænigmata/riddles).</note>

In their Talmud—which, as is well known, they consider to be God's Word just as much as the written Law—it says in Tractate Sopherim <note>The "Tractate of Scribes," one of the minor tractates of the Talmud.</note>, chapter 16, folio 13, column 3: Rabbi Jannai said that God gave the Law to Moses in such a way that a single thing can be considered unclean in 49 ways, and also considered clean in 49 ways, as is said in the Song of Solomon (2:4), *Vediglo* <note>original Hebrew: ודגלו, meaning "and his banner."</note>, because the word *Vediglo* makes the number 49 through <term>Gematria; a system where each Hebrew letter corresponds to a number, allowing words to be interpreted by their total numerical value</term>.

## The Opinion of the Kabbalists ##

3) That these are precisely those deviations from the Law about which God, throughout all ages and through His prophets, complained against their equally stubborn and headstrong fathers, and which Christ condemned in their Pharisaic ancestors, and of which He so earnestly warned His own followers.

Should one wish to object and say: that even the most clever among the Rabbis in their **commentaries** <note>original Latin: *Commentariis*</note> have remained solely with the **literal sense** <note>original Latin: *sensu literali*</note>: then I answer as follows:

a. That they nevertheless are and remain Jews and venerators of the **Talmud** <term>Talmud<gloss>The central body of Jewish civil and ceremonial law and legend</gloss></term>; they cover and hide it as much as they know how and are able.

b. That they never understand the **literal sense** <note>original Latin: *Sensum literalem*</note> as referring to the true Messiah. And even when they explain this or that as referring to the Messiah, it happens either only **by accommodation** <note>original Latin: *per accommodationem*; a term for adapting a text to fit a specific circumstance or secondary meaning</note>, or it refers to their supposed two earthly Messiahs <note>A reference to the Jewish concept of two Messiahs: Mashiach ben Yosef (a suffering warrior) and Mashiach ben David (the ultimate king)</note>; but never ever to our Jesus of Nazareth. **For example** <note>original Latin: *E. g.*</note>, does it not seem to us to be a beautiful interpretation when Rabbi Eliezer, in his **Chapters, chapter 3** <note>original: *Capitulis cap. III.* refers to the *Pirkei De-Rabbi Eliezer*, an ancient collection of Jewish lore and ethical teachings</note>, explains the words of **Micah 5:2** <note>The prophecy regarding the ruler coming out of Bethlehem</note> as referring to the Messiah? Yet does he not remain, just as before, a Jew and an enemy of our true **Messiah** <note>original Latin: *Messiae*</note>?

c. That a Jew can never ever correctly explain the **literal sense**, because he uses the **vowel points** <note>original Latin: *puncta vocalia*; the marks added to the Hebrew text to indicate vowel sounds, which can change the meaning of words</note> according to his own pleasure. Its main purpose is this: to recognize clearly the weakness and the uncertainty of the supposed Jewish aids used to explain the pronouncements of divine truths.

### On the Roots
<note>original Latin: *De Radicibus*</note>

...those who have looked around even a little will not find this unknown. Now, many learned men have indeed been found who have applied much diligence in various ways to, if not completely resolve, then at least ease this difficult matter. For this, one is bound to them with immortal thanks. However, there are nevertheless here and there—and to confess the truth, within the work of every single one of them—so many <term>exceptions<gloss>original: Exceptiones</gloss></term> made, that even at this hour one does not know to which one should say: "Come, and be our king." <note>A reference to the parable of the trees in Judges 9:8–15, where the trees look for a leader; the author suggests that no single system of Hebrew grammar is yet perfect enough to "reign" supreme.</note>

The learned former professor at Leipzig, Elias Hutterus <note>Elias Hutter (1553–1602), a German Hebraist famous for his polyglot Bibles and innovative methods for teaching Hebrew</note>, indeed took much trouble in the publication of his great Hebrew Bible to mark the <term>roots<gloss>original: Radices; the three-letter base of Hebrew words</gloss></term> in a very special way. But when one considers how many unequal and often even contradictory meanings are to be found under a single <term>root</term>—an evil that stems mainly from the <term>Rabbis</term>—this often makes the text more difficult rather than easier for an <term>interpreter<gloss>original: Exegeten; a scholar who explains or interprets scripture</gloss></term>.

I will cite only a few examples of this. The word גָּדַד <note>gadad</note> has a threefold meaning: **to come in troops, to cut oneself, or to plunder.** <note>original Latin: Turmatim venire, incidere, depopulari</note> גּוּר <note>gur</note> has a fourfold meaning: **to sojourn, to fear, to dwell, or to gather together.** <note>original Latin: peregrinari, timere, diversari, convenire</note> דָּבַק <note>dabaq</note> has five...

# Concerning the Roots

...[by] prefixing his name, he made his intention known to the learned world in order to solicit their opinions on it. This work clearly teaches that the commonly so-called "roots" are not the true primitive words of the Hebrews, but are terms derived from a certain other principle, prior to and simpler than these roots. Alphabet
<page-num>248</page-num>
# Hieroglyphic Alphabet of Hebrew Words
## [Insert here] <note>original German: *hieher setzen*; likely a note to the printer to place the specific alphabet list at this point in the layout</note>

### א [Aleph]

*Aleph*, whether it be a root letter <term>radical: the core letters that form the base meaning of a word</term> or a functional letter <term>servile: letters added to a root to indicate grammar, like "in" or "the"</term>, is the symbol of activity and motion in all Hebrew words.

The name אֶלָן <note>original: "Elan"; Neumann provides a symbolic breakdown of this specific word</note> will signify: א [Aleph] activity or motion; ל [Lamed] in opposition; ן [Nun] a winding or curve <term>anfractum: a bend or circuitous path</term>. That is: motion forming opposite angles.

### ב [Beth]

*Beth* indicates mass, and is a sign of three dimensions, or as those in the East are accustomed to count, four extensions, as referenced in Ephesians 3:18 <note>Ephesians 3:18 refers to the "breadth, length, depth, and height" of Christ's love, which Neumann links to the symbolic spatiality of the letter Beth</note>.

When *Beth* is a root letter, depending on the nature of the thing being named, you can translate it sometimes as *matter, body, corpulence, a thing, or something*; at other times as *place, space, or the capacity of a thing* <term>capedinem: the space or container that holds an object</term>.

As a functional letter, *Beth* takes on the nature of a preposition in the translated language; it implies *existence within* and the *location of a thing in space*, or *within its capacity*. It follows that it can be translated as *in, among, within, or something similar*.

The noun בַּיִת [Bayit], which we commonly translate as "House," signifies letter by letter: ב [Beth] the capacity, י [Yod] of extension, ת [Tav] continued.

### ג [Gimel]

*Gimel* carries the meaning of *a bend and slant*, but when doubled, it signifies *a continued bend* or *a frequently repeated bending*.

<meta>catchword: ג [Gimel]</meta>

<summary>This page begins a systematic breakdown of the Hebrew alphabet according to Caspar Neumann's "hieroglyphic" theory, where Aleph represents motion, Beth represents three-dimensional space/containers, and Gimel represents bending.</summary>
<keywords>Aleph, Beth, Gimel, hieroglyphic Hebrew, symbolic linguistics, Caspar Neumann, biblical philology, dimensions, radical letters, servile letters</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the breakdown of the Hebrew alphabet according to Caspar Neumann's "hieroglyphic" theory, where each letter represents a physical motion or spatial concept. This follows the entries for Aleph and Beth on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>Hieroglyphic [Meaning of Letters].</header>

For the word *Gamal* [גָּמָל] <note>original: "גָּמָל"; usually translated as "camel"</note> may be translated as a *camel-like curvature*; or as ג [Gimel] a *curvature*, י [Yod] *extended in length*, with מ [Mem] and ל [Lamed] indicating a movement *from one position to another*, and ל [Lamed] specifically being *twisted toward the opposite side*. In short, *Gamal* notes: ג [Gimel] *the bending of something* מ [Mem] *out of its original state* ל [Lamed] *into an opposite part or condition*.

->ד [Daleth]<-

*Daleth* is the sign of a *protrusion* made toward another place, of *promotion, and of all motion or position* found in things that *proceed, stand out, emerge, stretch forward, are propelled, pushed out, or are brought forth and advanced by any other movement*. We call this letter *Deleth* [דֶּלֶת] a *projection*, or ד [Daleth] a *protrusion* ל [Lamed] *oppositely* ת [Tav] *continued*. 

*That is: a thing that stands out toward the viewer, or has been pushed out into the distance.* Such are *Daliyot* [דָּלִיּוֹת] <note>original: "דָּלִיּוֹת"; refers to the trailing branches of a vine</note>, *shoots, vine-branches, or boughs*; that is, ד [Daleth] *protrusions made* ל [Lamed] *in the opposite direction* י [Yod] *extended far*. Likewise, *Deleth* [דֶּלֶת] means a *Door*, which, in order to be opened or closed, ד [Daleth] is *pushed* ל [Lamed] *to the opposite side* ת [Tav] *continuously*.

->ה [He]<-

*He* carries the meaning of *presence or being-at-hand*. It refers to something that is present in such a way that it can be pointed to with the fingers, having become known to such an extent that this letter is commonly called *He Ha-Yediah* <note>original Hebrew: הֵא הַיְּדִיעָה; the "He of Knowledge," known in grammar as the definite article "the"</note>, the *He of demonstration*, or the *Demonstrative, the Indicative, and the Presentative*. 

It is *not*, however, the *Distinctive, Determinative, or Restrictive*—two concepts which our Philologists, misled by Kimchi <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), also known as Radak, was a foundational Hebrew grammarian whose work Neumann is here critiquing</note> and others, commonly confuse. Because of this confusion, they fall into superfluous questions concerning...

<page-num>250</page-num>
# The Alphabet

The letter He [ה] when prefixed, or not prefixed, to proper names.

In this regard, I cannot leave unmentioned what I observed many years ago: that everywhere in Holy Scripture where this *demonstrative* He [ה] <term>demonstrativum: a grammatical term for the definite article "the," used to point out a specific thing</term> is found with the *Urim* and *Thummim* <note>The Urim and Thummim were sacred objects carried by the Jewish High Priest, used to determine the will of God in legal or national crises.</note>, the Urim and Thummim were actually present. However, where one does not find this letter used, they were either not present at that time, or had already been lost.

Regarding the word *Hinne* [הִנֵּה] <note>original: "הנה"; usually translated as "Behold" or "Look!"</note>, it signifies "Indication": that is, the letter ה [He] indicating and demonstrating the activity of א [Aleph] directed toward another—that is, toward the thing which is to be shown. Commonly, people translate this as *Behold!* or *See!* <note>original Latin: "En! Ecce!"</note>

->ו [Vau]<-

*Vau* is the symbol of *coupling* and *annexation* [joining things together]. For the name *Vau* [וָו] <note>original: "ו ו"</note> signifies a hook <note>original Latin: "uncum"</note>, whose purpose is ו *to annex* ו *to annex*, or to which any thing can be so joined that it remains attached. This power of repetition and continuation is usually inherent in the doubling of letters.

->ז [Zayin]<-

*Zayin* <note>The OCR spells this "Dzajin," reflecting an older German phonetic rendering of the Hebrew letter.</note> is the symbol of motion, which pushes a thing [ז] *greatly* [ש], in the *highest degree*. When the discussion concerns the position and shape of things, this will denote a protrusion that is *greatly pushed out*, that is, *sharp*, and *ending in a point*. However, regarding *motion* itself, when that is the subject, it indicates a *movement* which—whether *acting* or *being acted upon*, or both—presses and urges by pushing forward: that is, a motion that breaks forth and protrudes into something with vehemence and singular efficacy.

<page-num>251</page-num>
# Hieroglyphics

The name *Zayin* [זַיִן] among the Chaldeans <note>The "Chaldeans" here refers to speakers of Aramaic, a language closely related to Hebrew used in several books of the Bible and later Jewish commentary.</note> signifies any thing armed with a point: a weapon, a dart, a javelin, or anything else similar to these. <margin>ז</margin> This is זַ, a very sharp protrusion stretched out lengthwise and <margin>ז</margin> propagated, or intended to be propagated, from one thing to another.

->ח [Cheth]<-

*Cheth* is like a double *He* [ה], and in its meaning, it doubles the meaning that is proper to the letter *He*. Thus, it denotes two things present to one another at the same time, or which exist together; this, in the end, implies *Association* or *combination*.

The character of this meaning is חח <note>Neumann uses the repetition of the letter to show the concept of doubling or pairing</note>, which is ח to combine ח successively, by placing one after another. From this comes the name of our letter *Cheth* [חֵית], which is ח a successive combination along the length of ח, or a combination made in such a way that one thing is placed after another.

->ט [Tet]<-

*Tet* is the sign of *regression* or *backwards-action*, occurring when a thing, established in its own motion, is held back and driven backwards. Therefore, this letter is the irreconcilable opposite of the letter *Daleth* [ד] <note>Neumann previously defined Daleth as a sign of "protrusion" or "forward movement."</note>.

It is commonly written *Teit* [טֵית], but incorrectly; for the syllable *Tei* [טֵי] does not occur in the whole of the Hebrew language <term>Hebraismo: referring to the Hebrew linguistic system or the rules of the language</term>, nor can it occur, just like the syllables *Dt* [דט] and *Td* [טד]. Since *Daleth* [ד] pushes forward and advances, while *Tet* [ט] acts backwards, the Hebrews perhaps did not wish to combine these letters because of their opposing forces. Likewise, since *Tet* [ט] drives motion backwards, while *He* [ה] commands it to *succeed* and *continue*, these letters also can never be combined. Therefore, the name of our letter should be written as *Tet* [טֵת], which was already [acknowledged] long ago by *Antoine Rodolphe Chevalier* <note>A 16th-century French Hebraist and professor</note> and *Bonaventure Corneille Bertram* <note>A 16th-century Huguenot scholar of Hebrew and Oriental languages.</note>

# Alphabetum

...they have admitted. Indeed, **Tet-Tet** [טט] <note>Neumann often doubles a letter to show its intensified or repeated action.</note> denotes a thing greatly twisted back, according to the habit of doubled letters; or **Tet** [ט] "twisted back," a length [ט] and again "twisted back" [ט], that is, a length [ט - ט] twisted back here and there: such a thing as the horns of rams are often shaped. For in *Talmudic Aramaic* <note>original: "Chaldaismo Thalmudico"</note>, according to *Buxtorf* <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629) was a celebrated scholar of Hebrew and Aramaic whose dictionaries were standard reference works in the 17th century.</note>, it denotes a horn. From this perhaps comes the phrase of certain Germans *mit der Titte titten* <note>meaning "to toot with the horn"</note>, to blow horns. The Jews in Germany use the word *tötschen* <note>original: "tötschen," likely referring to the sounding of the Shofar</note>.

->י [Jod]<-

*Jod* is the indicator of all tension, or extension and length: both of that which is in matter, extended according to length; as well as that which is in time and its duration, through which the being, or essence, of any thing flows and remains. It also preserves this meaning even there where it is commonly said to *Serve* <note>In Hebrew grammar, "servile letters" are those used to form prefixes, suffixes, and grammatical moods rather than being part of the root word itself.</note>. Indeed, in the vowel point <term>Chireck [ִ]</term> <note>The vowel representing the "ee" sound.</note>, when it is added as if in the place of a support near the end of nouns, together with the letter **Mem** [ם] (the sign of amplification and multitude), it helps to form the dual and plural number, and thus extends the meaning of the word to which it is attached far and wide; that is, it shifts it from its unity into a plural number.

At the end of a word, its characteristic meaning is clearly unique: for it denotes the extension of a finger, or of any indicator, to point out or determine some thing; and thus it has the power of demonstrating, or indicating some thing with a distinction from others, just as the letter *He* [ה] demonstrates a thing by presenting it and placing it here. Finally, *Jod* attached at the end has the power of pointing, by which the speaker [points] a finger as if into...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Hebrew letter Jod [י] and introduces the letter Caph [כ]. The text continues Caspar Neumann’s "hieroglyphic" method, which argues that the physical shape of Hebrew letters and the mechanics of their pronunciation reveal their divine, essential meanings.</meta>

# Hieroglyphics

...extends itself, and thus demonstrates itself to the listener. By the power of its constituent letters, **Jod** [י] denotes a pushed-out and flowing *length* [ד] <note>Neumann is analyzing the name of the letter Jod, which is spelled Jod-Vav-Daleth [יוד]. He interprets the letter **Daleth** [ד] within that name as representing "length" or "protrusion."</note>, or what is the same thing, the protrusion and emanation of length.

->כ [Caph]<-

**Caph** is the sign of a semicircle, of curvature, and of concavity; it represents every motion by which a thing turns in any direction, is rotated, is bent, or is rendered concave and convex. 

**Caph**, when used as a prefix <note>In Hebrew grammar, a "prefix" is a letter attached to the start of a root word to change its function, such as "like" or "as."</note>, depicts a curvature which, when overlaid upon an object, adapts itself to the circuit of that object’s surface, applies itself to it, and makes itself similar to it. This is just as our own hand, in grasping something, is required to adapt itself by curving to whatever shapes the objects have, and thus becomes like them. For this reason, **Caph** is not incorrectly translated by some as *about* or *around* <note>original Latin: "circiter"</note>. In a metaphorical sense, however, it is a sign of **Agreement** or of any **Similarity** by which one thing approaches the nature or character of another. Hence it is also known as the **Caph of similarity**, and is usually translated as *so* or *like*.

**Caph** is also frequently employed to designate the motion of any joints within an animal body; and this is clearly in accordance with the laws of nature. For every movement of the limbs in a jointed body occurs by turning the heads of the joints within the socket <term>acetabulum</term> <note>From the Latin for "vinegar cup," this refers to the cup-shaped socket of the hip joint. The letter Caph [כ] resembles this hollow shape.</note>, which accurately reflects the shape of the letter **Caph**, and is called *Kaph* [כף] <note>The Hebrew word for "palm of the hand" or "hollow."</note> by the Hebrews. *Genesis 32:26* <note>This biblical reference describes the "hollow" of Jacob's thigh being touched during his struggle with the angel.</note>.

The **final Caph** [ך] <note>Hebrew has "final forms" for five letters; when Caph appears at the end of a word, it is written as ך instead of כ.</note> conveys the gesture of a speaker who—by a movement of either the head or hand, or by some other action—is turned or inclined toward the listener, indicating that he is the one we intend, or toward him...

# Alphabet

This sense of the mind is expressed in other languages through the words *You*, *To You*, *You* <note>original Latin: "Tu, Tibi, Te"</note>, and similar terms <note>Neumann is referring to Kaph [ך] when used as a suffix at the end of a word, which functions as the second-person pronoun "you."</note>.

The name **Kaph** [כף] denotes **K** [כ] as *curved* or *bent*, and **Ph** [ך] as *winding* or *jagged* <note>original Latin: "anfractum"</note>. This is confirmed by the usage of both Hebrew and Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note>, where **Kaph** denotes a curvature or something hollow—specifically the socket of a joint <note>original Latin: "acetabulum," referring to the hip socket or a small cup</note>, and the palm or the hollow of the hand.

-> ל [Lamed] <-

**Lamed** is a sign of *adversative* motion <note>In this context, "adversative" refers to motion directed toward or facing something</note>, which tends toward or *against* a thing and reaches it: whether this occurs by approach, warning, giving, attribution, impulse, contact, pressure, wearing down, or in any other way. Most especially, however, the meaning of **giving** and the **Dative Case** <note>The grammatical case typically used for the indirect object, indicating the person or thing to or for whom something is done</note> is inherent in this letter.

The name **Lamed** [למד] denotes a goad <note>original Latin: "stimulum"</note>, which the ancients used for pricking and urging on beasts, or for any other such uses. By the power of the letters, it is: **L** [ל] (adversatively) toward something, **M** [מ] (out of), **D** [ך/ד] (to thrust); or **L** [ל] (by the application of a thing), **M** [מ] (out of its place), **D** [ך/ד] (to protrude and move forward).

-> מ ם [Mem] <-

**Mem** is double in its letter, sound, shape, and meaning <note>Hebrew uses two forms for the letter Mem: the "open" Mem [מ] used at the start or middle of words, and the "closed" or "final" Mem [ם] used only at the end of words.</note>. Generally speaking, however, it is a mark of **abundance** or **enlargement**. When applied to continuous quantity, it denotes the **width** and **dilation** of a surface; but where the quantity is discrete <note>Referring to individual, countable items</note>, it signifies **multitude**. This works in such a way that the initial and **open** Mem [מ] excludes things from that embrace of abundance, while the **final** and **closed** Mem [ם] brings things into it. Middle [Mem], for...

# Hieroglyphics

By the reasoning of the preceding and following letters, each makes up the initial and final **Mem** composed together. That is, the letter **Mem**, doubled within itself, gives the name **Mem** [מם], which literally denotes *explanation*, or **M** [מ] out of **M** [מ], something expanded—a *breadth*, or a flat expansion of some surface. A surface, or a broad spot <note>original German: "Eine Fläche, oder ein breiter Fleck"</note>.

-> נ [Nun] <-

**Nun** conveys *motion*, *progression*, or *propagation* turning from one thing into or toward another. When used at the end of a word, **Nun** likewise denotes motion departing from one and proceeding toward something else. In grammatical usage, when prefixed to the <term>Niphal</term> <note>The passive or reflexive verb stem in Hebrew</note> conjugation, it is a sign of an action passing from the agent into the sufferer <note>original Latin: "patiens," the person or thing receiving the action</note>; hence its passive meaning.

The name **Nun** [נון], which is the letter **Nun** doubled within itself, was described long ago by **Bertram** <note>Bonaventure Corneille Bertram (1531–1594), a notable Hebraist and grammarian</note> in his Grammar and elsewhere as the *propagative letter*, moving from one to another. It signifies nothing other than an *offshoot* <note>original Latin: "propaginem"</note>, or **N** [נ] as something propagated from one thing to another. **Nan** [נן] means *to propagate oneself*, commonly referred to as *to proliferate* <note>original Latin: "sobolescere," meaning to multiply or sprout</note>. **Nin** [ננ] or **Nun** [נ] represents length propagated here and there; in common speech, it refers to a **son** <note>The Hebrew word for son or progeny is "Nin" [נין]</note>, acting as the *propagator of the family*. **Manon** [מנון] involves motion where, out of one **N** [נ], propagation into many others occurs: which is again a **symbol of the son**.

-> ס [Samech] <-

**Samech** is and remains a perpetual sign of *girding* and *restriction* <note>original Latin: "coarctationis," a drawing together or narrowing</note>, wherever you may find it. Moreover, the name **Samech** [סמך] undoubtedly denotes a belt or girdle; for by the power of its constituent letters, **Samech** [סמך] is a sign of embrac-

# The Alphabet

...connection <note>original Latin: "nexus," likely the end of a word from the previous page</note> or a girdle encompassing some thing: that which, letter by letter, is **Kaph** [כ], a girdle tightening or restricting, **Beth** [ב], the breadth of a thing, into **Kaph** [כ], a curved cavity, and any crescent shape.

In its proper sense, it is said of an apprehension made by the hand—for example, of *those who bless*, who embraced the head of those to be blessed with their hand, as in *Deuteronomy 34:9*; or of a staff, perhaps rounded, for those leaning on it, *2 Kings 18:21*; or of architectural arches and vaults, where the stones of a building are restricted and tightened into hollow and curved arches, as in *Judges 16:29*. It is commonly, though poorly, translated as *supported* <note>The author is likely discussing the root *samak* [סמך], which usually means to lean or support, arguing it implies the physical "grasping" or "arching" of the hand or structure</note>.

->ע<-

**Hajin**, or **Ajin**, or **Gajch**, or **Gnajin** [ע], almost coincides with **Aleph** [א], except that the sound of this letter is usually thicker and stronger than that one; as is the pronunciation, so is the meaning. It brings forth the notion of an *object*, or *objectivity*, or an *encounter*—that which we have before us, which is present, and which is observed by us from the opposite side.

Truly, what the name **Ayin** [עין] actually signifies is not sufficiently learned from our Lexicons <note>Reference to standard Hebrew dictionaries of the time</note>. By the power of its constituent letters: **Ayin** [ע] imports an *encounter* of one thing with another, or the motion by which one thing comes to meet another. By inserting the letter **Yod** [י], the root **Ayin** [עין] emerges, composed as if from **Ayin** [ע], that is, the mark of an *encounter* moving far off, and **Nun** [ן], that is, the indicator of a *propagation* sloping at length from one thing to another: thus denoting a motion where something comes into view <note>The word *Ayin* commonly means "eye" or "fountain" in Hebrew</note> through a long propagation.

->פ<-

**Pe** [פ], a letter of the corner and of a winding path, both internal and—

# Hieroglyphic Meaning

is the mark of an external [corner], much like the Latin letter **V** (or **Λ**) whether upright or inverted, which has countless uses in every position and movement of matter. Just as the Greek word *gonia* <note>original: γωνία</note> usually denotes an angle—whether an internal one, as in Acts 26:26, or an external one, as in Matthew 6:5—the Hebrews use the letter **Pe** to signify whatever attributes belong to both internal and external angles. Consequently, it is certain that this element of ours, **Pe**, signifies branching, motion that bends to another side, winding paths, and reciprocal or regressive movements (which typically occur through angles). It also signifies vaulted or winding archways, wedges, and sharp edges (which also possess a very acute angle), and similar concepts.

The name **Pe** <note>original: פה</note> therefore designates winding activity, or motion that creates nothing but twists and turns, such as the constant movement of our own mouth. This is confirmed by the feminine form of the name which survives in the sacred scriptures, *Pe’ah* <note>original: פאה</note>, meaning "angulation" or "cornering."

-> צ ץ <-

**Tsade**, because it is a sibilant <term>sibilant: a hissing sound, like 'ts'</term> version of the letter **Nun** (נ), denotes the same thing as that letter, but adds a higher or more intense degree to the original meaning, brought about by the vehement activity of the hiss. And just as **Nun** is a mark of that which hangs over or follows something else, **Tsade** designates something similar, except the attached hiss increases that meaning and makes it more forceful through its activity. **Tsade** is therefore a sign of juxtaposition, contiguity <term>contiguity: the state of being in direct contact with something else</term>, and the closest possible succession. For if we assume the letter **Tsade** is a composite of **Nun** (נ) and **Shin** (ש), it must indicate **Nun** (Succession) and **Shin** (the Highest Degree)—that is, contiguity, where one thing follows another joined closely together.

Regarding the name **Tzad** <note>original: צד</note>, it means "side," where the **Tsade** (צ) represents contiguities—that is, things that follow (**Nun**) and succeed one another very closely (**Shin**)—and the **Daleth** (ד) represents protrusion; indeed, all contact between bodies usually occurs through their sides. # Alphabet

If you accept this, that most troublesome passage in Judges 2:3 will be free of all difficulty. <note>original: Jud. II, 3. The author is likely referring to the phrase "thorns in your sides," which he interprets through the etymological root of 'nets.'</note> *Tsud* <note>original: צוד</note> means to hunt *by ensnaring*; *Tsad* and *Matsud* <note>original: צד & מצוד</note> *signify a net*. What, then, prevents me from saying that **Tsade** (צ) *denotes a reticulation* <term>reticulation: a net-like pattern or structure</term>, where there are **Tsade** (צ) *contiguities* <note>things touching or bordering each other</note> succeeding one another closely, and **Daleth** (ד) *promoted or pushed out into a long extension*? And this is the very name of our letter whose meaning we are seeking.

->ק<-

**Koph**, in its shape, sound, and name, is related to the letter *Caph* (כ), and its meaning also corresponds to it. However, just as the condition of the **Koph** (ק) is fuller and "fatter" <note>original: pinguior; here meaning a more robust or complete version</note> in all those aspects, so too in its mode of signifying: it renders the semicircular *curvature* of **Caph** (כ) fuller, and completes it into a perfect circle. Indeed, it is established by a constant induction of examples that **Koph** *is a mark of a circuit, a motion, and a circular path of any kind*.

<margin>

</margin>

The *name* **Koph** <note>original: קוף</note> is rightly compared with the name *Caph* <note>original: כף</note>; for in both cases, the letter being named forms its own name by taking on a final **Pe** (ף). Just as *Caph*, as explained above, denoted something *curved* or a *winding* <note>original: anfractum</note>, so also **Koph** will be a *circular winding, or a winding made of various circulations*, such as a circular or spiral line usually produces.

->ר<-

**Resch**, the last and final of all the letters that modify motion and matter, is a mark of extremity and exteriority. Furthermore, **Resch** agrees in some way with **Daleth** (ד) regarding its shape, and thus its meaning does not entirely depart from that letter either. Just as **Daleth** *pushes forward, drives, and promotes*, so **Resch** *indicates a motion of going forth, going out, or emerging*, and is a mark of all exteriority. It is commonly used in describing things that *flash out, flow out, stand out*, and similar concepts.

# Hieroglyphic Meaning

For our lexicographers, *Rish* and *Rush* <note>original Hebrew: ריש & רוש</note> mean "he lacked" or "was impoverished"; hence, they commonly translate these words as *poverty*. Properly speaking, however, all these terms denote **destruction, a going out, and extermination**. This is most clearly seen in the Book of Malachi 1:4, where it is used in opposition to the word *shub* <note>original Hebrew: שוב</note>, which means **to return** or rather **to enter one's house**. Just as the Latin word for destruction (*exitium*) is derived from "going out" (*exeundo*) and began to denote a state of adverse fortune and total ruin, so too among the Hebrews, destruction—that is, the letter **Resh** (רש) as the ultimate sign of "going out"—is an indicator of poverty: a state where everything has flowed out, leaving both the house and the purse empty.

Given these facts, the author intends to interpret the name of our letter, **Resh** (רש), as **destruction**, **outflow**, or a **downward course**, such as that of brooks and rivers. It is as if you were to say that the **Resh** (ר) signifies an "extended or long-reaching egress" and the **Shin** (ש) signifies "greatly" or "exceedingly." This conjecture is confirmed by the Hebrew idiom which uses a very similar word, *rashim* <note>original Hebrew: ראשים</note>, to mean the "outgoing" or the outlets of rivers. <note>The author provides a German explanation: "Outflows of water when it forms a new stream in a certain direction."</note> In Genesis 2:10, it is said that the river of Paradise was divided into four *rashim*, which 

people poorly translate as "heads." They do so because they are ignorant of the formal meaning of the letters **Resh** and **Shin** (רש), which signify the "highest or outermost activity of egress." It is the outermost or first beginning of an outgoing, which the seventy translators <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> rightly rendered with the word *arche* <note>original Greek: $\alpha\rho\chi\dot{\eta}$, meaning 'origin' or 'beginning.'</note>. See the work of Huetius <note>Pierre Daniel Huet (1630–1721), a French scholar who wrote *On the Situation of Paradise*.</note> regarding the location of Paradise, Chapter V, number 3. He recognized even in his own time that what the author says here is true. Furthermore, when the word *rosh* <note>head</note> is used to describe a military "wedge" <term>cuneus militum: a tactical formation in the shape of a V or wedge</term>, it refers to the shape of the troop line, which "flows out" into a sharp, pointed tip.

If you wish to turn your thoughts to the artistic construction of the script, the name **Resh** (רש) clearly states once more what the letter itself is: namely, a **Resh** (ר) representing an egress or the outflow of a <unclear>$\tau\rho$</unclear> **Yod** (י) <note>original: Jod</note> tripled. For the shape of the **Resh** consists of a line running downward like a stream, which is formed by three **Yods** connected in succession.

# The Alphabet

->ש<-

The letter *Shin* <note>original: ש</note> breathes out the strongest and most "frothy" aspiration of all. By this sound, it serves as an indicator not only of the most rapid movements but also of vehemence or excellence in general, introducing a sort of superlative degree to any word to which it is joined. *Sin* <note>The version of the letter with the dot on the left: שׂ</note>, however, is a middle ground between *Samekh* <note>ס</note> and *Shin* <note>ש</note>, and it differs from the latter only by being a milder degree of the same sound.

Just as this letter is the only one among all the others that has three points <note>original Latin: *tricuspidalis*</note>, it is also found to express the power of three letters rather than one through its sound; thus, its meaning follows both its shape and its sound.

For *Shin* serves as the indicator of the <term>number three|original: *ternarii*</term> in things of distinct quantity, consisting of three units. The Hebrews prize this number above all others so much that they included it in their Alphabet depicted as a unique element. This is similar to how, when they use their letters for counting, they divide all of them (including the five "final" letter forms) into three classes of three times three: nine <term>units|original: *monadicorum*</term>, nine <term>tens|original: *decadicorum*</term>, and nine <term>hundreds|original: *centenariorum*</term>. This is mentioned here in passing.

In its figurative and metaphorical meaning, this letter marks the *third degree*—that is, the *superlative*—as was said above. In the same sense that the Romans used *ter* <note>Latin for "thrice," as in the phrases "thrice happy and thrice blessed"</note> or the French use *très* <note>French for "very," which the author links to the word for "three"</note>, this letter greatly emphasizes, increases, intensifies, and commands attention to the meaning of the letters it is placed before, after, or between.

The "Left-side" *Sin* <note>שׂ</note> has exactly the same meaning as the "Right-side" *Shin*, but it differs by being a somewhat softer and more subtle degree of that meaning.

To demonstrate this difference in degrees, the Author points to the single *Shin* <note>ש</note> versus the triple *Shin* <note>ש ש ש</note>. He uses examples to show that the single letter refers only to material things and to signify their quantity or the number three, whereas the triple form is transferred to the soul and the emotions of the mind.
