### Hieroglyphics

...going from one thing to another, and thus is a symbol of all *alteration, duality, and duplication*, as the most distinguished <note>Caspar Neumann (1648–1715) was a German professor and clergyman known for his theories on the internal structure of the Hebrew language.</note> Neumann noted in the first chapter of his *Twofold Physical-Sacred Difficulties* <note>original Latin: *Biga Difficultatum Physico-Sacrarum*</note>, to speak in his very own words.

For you can translate the letter *Shin* [ש] literally as "three extended lengths," or lengths arranged in an order proceeding from one to the others; and regarding its written form, *Shin* is composed of three *Yods* [י] that are "nunated" <note>The term "nunated" (from the letter *Nun*) here refers to being joined by a horizontal base line</note>, that is, connected by a line going from one to the other.

->ת<-

*Tav* [ת], the attendant of all letters and a kind of appendix to the entire Hebrew alphabet, imitates its own place and order in its meaning; for it is a sign of *sequel*, or of that which follows and succeeds, whether by local movement or by succession of time, or in whatever other way that "following" is to be understood.

Our *Tav* continues the meaning of the remaining letters—the same meaning which the addition of *Shin* tripled and raised to the highest degree of all. It seems to have a particular relationship with the letter *Daleth* [ד], which it imitates in both shape and sound, so that it is commonly called a "full *Daleth*." Just as *Daleth* extends and pushes things forward, so *Tav* makes *subsequent things follow*.

A solitary *Tav* used in the formation of words, whether as a <term>prefix|a letter added to the start of a word</term> or a <term>suffix|a letter added to the end of a word</term>, is always the sign of "subsequent persons." For example: the Past Tense, being the most remote of all, begins with the person most remote from the speaker (the third person); therefore the second person, as the one immediately following, uses a *Tav* [ת] in both the masculine and feminine gender, but placed at the end of the word. The Future Tense—that is, the subsequent and succeeding time, whose beginning we still await—starts in reverse order from the first person; but the second person, as the one immediately following in both genders, is marked by a *Tav*, but at the beginning of the word. Similarly, the third person is marked this way, at least in the "following" sex—that is, the feminine. For the feminine gender, because of the masculine, comes from the masculine, and after this...

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Hebrew letter Tav (ת), moving from its grammatical role as a suffix to its symbolic meaning as a "signature" or "mark." # Alphabet

...was made in the creation itself, the Hebrew language also considers it as a subsequent thing in its grammatical formations.

The name *Tav* [תו] denotes the *Tav* [ת] as a sequel of the *Vav* [ו] connections, or the making of connections in succession, by always placing one after another—exactly what writers do. Hence, *Tav* is a **signature**, which most scholars freely acknowledge in Ezekiel 9:4 <note>In this biblical passage, a "mark" (Tav) is placed on the foreheads of the faithful. Historically, the letter Tav was the final letter of the alphabet, representing a seal or completion.</note>. For those dreams about the letter *Tav* having the form of a cross have long since been discarded <note>Early versions of the Hebrew script (Paleo-Hebrew) wrote the Tav as a cross-shape (+ or X), leading many early Christian scholars to see it as a prophecy of the Crucifixion; the author here dismisses this as mystical speculation.</note>.

The matter is no different in the passage of Job 31:35, where Job seeks an official hearing for his case using purely legal formulas, in this manner: *Oh, that I had one to hear me!* [הן תוי] *Behold my **signature**, or my consignment;* <note>original German: *Hier ist meine Defensions-Schrift*</note> *Here is my defense document.* Let the Almighty answer me, and let my adversary write a book.

The verb *tavah* [תוה] means to present such signatures by painting or writing them. This is how David, in his feigned madness, perhaps scratched marks with his fingers and nails. 1 Samuel 21:14: *On the doors of the gate,* seeking an exit through the closed doors in this way.

With these things thus understood, it will finally not be entirely impossible to guess at the meaning of the name *Vayn* <note>original: וין. This is a variant spelling or phonetic representation of the letter Vav (ו), which the author is analyzing through its constituent parts.</note>, which is otherwise quite obscure. Namely, it seems to signify an **appendix**, which in this context can be described by the power of the letters as *Tav* [ת] the **sequel**, or the **dependency** and **succession** of the *Vav* [ו] **extension** of the **annexed** or **coupled** *Yod* [י]; or, if you prefer, *Tav* [ת] the **backwards** part and the *Yod* [י] of **some length** made behind the *Vav* [ו] **annexation**.

So much for the hieroglyphic alphabet, according to the mind and hand of the Blessed <note>Caspar Neumann (1648–1715) was a theologian who believed Hebrew letters were symbols of physical properties.</note> Neumann; to which I add only this: **Traction** <note>The act of pulling or drawing out.</note> does not exist in Hebraism. Certainly, no letter is present that indicates *traction*; and this being granted, no word exists either. For *Samekh* [ס], properly speaking, denotes only **constriction** or **compression**, where the *Samekh* [ס] **compresses**...

<meta>catchword: ...us (we compress)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Caspar Neumann’s "hieroglyphic" theory of the Hebrew language, specifically analyzing how the meanings of individual letters combine to form the definitions of words. ### Hieroglyphics

...collecting <note>The text likely completes a word from the previous page, such as *conjectamus* (we cast together).</note> one with another [ב / Bet] within itself, or within its own capacity, making them together into a heap, into one another; this implies a destruction or confusion of things. 

Truly, the word *mashak* [משך, to draw or pull] consists of the letter *shin* [ש]—signifying "to a high degree"—and the letter *kaf* [כ]—signifying to turn something, someone, or oneself from its place [ך / final Kaf] for the purpose of walking, or any other movement. This could be shown extensively in Song of Solomon 1:4 <note>The verse begins "Draw me, we will run after thee."</note> and other examples. Therefore, those modern philosophers who laboriously deny the existence of *physical traction* <note>original Latin: *tractionem Physicam*. This refers to a 17th-century physics debate where some argued that "pulling" doesn't exist in nature—only "pushing" (propulsion) from behind.</note> have cause to congratulate themselves. For the entirety of the Hebrew language favors their opinion; being most skillfully composed according to the laws of nature, it mentions many things regarding propulsion [pushing], but makes no mention of *traction* [pulling] at all.

If we wish to follow the lead of this Hieroglyphic literature <note>The author uses "Hieroglyphic" to describe the theory that Hebrew letters are symbols of physical actions or natural laws.</note>, we will no longer need to seek the "roots" of words from elsewhere. Consequently, as far as interpretation [exegesis] is concerned, we will not need to make such a great fuss over the Arabic language. 

However, should anyone wish to know how many "lost roots" <note>original Latin: *radices deperditas*. These are Hebrew words found in the Bible whose meanings were forgotten and had to be rediscovered by comparing them to similar-sounding words in Arabic.</note> were sought out from the Arabic language, I would refer them primarily to the "Miscellaneous Notes on the Gate of Moses" <note>original Latin: *Notas Miscellaneas ad Portam Mosis*</note> by the Oxford professor Edward Pococke, as recently edited by the learned Reineccius in Leipzig. I also refer them to J. H. Hottinger’s *Smegma Orientale* <note>Literally "Oriental Soap" or "Purification," a scholarly compendium of Middle Eastern languages.</note>, as well as the various dissertations of Frischmuth, Abraham Hinckelmann’s preface to the Quran, and the works of Bochart.

Arnoldus Boolius made a small beginning in deriving roots from the Syriac language. Similarly, Ludovicus de Dieu in his "Observations on the Old Testament" <note>original Latin: *Animadversionibus ad V. T.*</note> and Jobus Ludolphus in his "Historical Ethiopic Commentary" used the Ethiopic language. Finally, Edmund Castell, in his massive work, through the diligent comparison of the Chaldean, Syriac, Arabic, Samaritan, Ethiopic, and Persian languages—as he himself describes in the preface to his *Polyglot Lexicon*—recovered several hundred Hebra-

<meta>Catchword: Hebra-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Hebrew language is built on natural laws, supporting the physical theory that 'pulling' is actually 'pushing.' It also surveys the history of scholars using other Semitic languages to decipher obscure Hebrew roots.</summary>

<keywords>Hieroglyphic Hebrew, Caspar Neumann, physical traction, philology, Semitic languages, Edward Pococke, Edmund Castell, Lexicon Heptaglotton, Hebrew roots</keywords>

### Hieroglyphic Alphabet

...and restore almost all Hebrew <term>hapax legomena</term> <note>original Greek: *ἅπαξ λεγόμενα*; words that appear only once in the entire Bible, making their definitions difficult to determine without outside context.</note>.

Now, because of the many examples already provided, one cannot entirely reject this method of restoring the Hebrew system; indeed, many <term>roots</term> <note>The fundamental three-letter units of Hebrew words.</note>—at least as we have understood them until now—do not actually appear in the Hebrew Bible, yet they can be found in other **Oriental languages**. However, when one considers how far all these languages have strayed from the original Hebrew—partly due to the **Babylonian confusion of tongues** <note>The biblical account of the Tower of Babel, used here to explain linguistic divergence.</note> and partly through the passage of extreme antiquity, even where we believe they might align—it becomes clear that one cannot proceed with total certainty in such an important matter. This is especially true since we can reach the truth much more directly through the **hieroglyphic literature** demonstrated here. This is unless the work were carried out in the manner of the highly experienced Dr. Löscher, who made a learned beginning in his excellent book *On the Causes of the Hebrew Language*, page 484 and following. Regarding that, I might well say: *let him who gave the advice also provide the help.* <note>original Latin: *qui dedit consilium, ferat auxilium*.</note>

Nevertheless, let everyone follow their own thoughts. May God, however, grant that this small and humble, yet well-intentioned labor, not be without blessing! Written on the Day of Concord, which was February 18, 1729. Magdeburg, at the Prelacy of the Evangelical Monastery of the

->**Blessed Virgin Mary**<- <note>original Latin abbreviation: *B. M. V.*, for *Beatae Mariae Virginis*.</note>
